# Ages - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened on the web: 14 (3 IBPS Clerk Prelims spot-checks, 4 SBI Clerk Prelims, 3 IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims, 4 IBPS PO Prelims). PYQs found: 21 IBPS Clerk Prelims questions from 21 paper labels (2016-2025, taken from your bank) plus 11 sister-exam questions from 9 papers. Bank questions used: 245 (30 constructed, 150 Guidely, 65 Sreedhar; 13 are repeats). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts plus 6-10 sister questions is met. All papers are memory-based reconstructions, so wording and numbers may differ from the real exam.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Exact Mechanics & Conversion Grid
**Central law.** Everybody grows older by the same number of years. So the gap between two people never changes, while their ratio does change. The total of a group grows by (number of people) x (years).
A **unit** is one part of a ratio. If ages are 4 : 7, the ages are 4 units and 7 units. Every Ages question is solved by finding the value of 1 unit.
### Base operations
1. **Find 1 unit.** Match a real number to its parts, then divide.
	- Sum given: 1 unit = sum / (sum of parts). Ages 4 : 7, sum 66: 1 unit = 66 / 11 = 6.
	- Gap given: 1 unit = gap / (difference of parts). Ages 4 : 7, gap 18: 1 unit = 18 / 3 = 6.
	- One age given: 1 unit = that age / its part.
2. **Move a total in time.** New total = old total + people x years (minus for “ago”). Three people, average 33 now: total 99; five years ago the total was 99 - 15 = 84.
3. **Average to total.** Total = average x people. Always convert first; never work with the average itself.
4. **Gap stays fixed.** If A is 6 years older than B today, A was 6 years older ten years ago and will be 6 years older forever.
5. **Join two ratios.** A : B = 4 : 5 and B : C = 3 : 4. Make B the same in both: multiply the first by 3 and the second by 5. A : B : C = 12 : 15 : 20.
### Percent and fraction words into ratios
Read “A is p% more than B” as A : B. Read “A is p% of B” differently: it is plain p : 100.

### Calculation facts to keep ready
- Tables of 7, 9, 11, 13, 17 and 23 up to x10. The sum of parts is often one of these.
- Squares up to 30 and cubes up to 10, for product questions.
- Fractions to percent: 1/6 = 16 2/3%, 1/8 = 12.5%, 1/16 = 6.25%, 3/8 = 37.5%, 5/6 = 83 1/3%.

Worked examples below are real questions from the papers. “Hence” means after; “ago” means before.
### A1. Present ratio plus one real number (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “The ratio of the present ages of A and B is 4 : 7” followed by a sum, a difference, one age, or a third person placed between them (“12 years older than A and 6 years younger than B”).
**Shortcut engine: unit method.**
1. Decide what the real number measures: sum, gap or one age.
2. 1 unit = real number / matching parts. Then read the asked age.
**Worked example (SBI Clerk Prelims, 20 Sep 2025, Shift 1).** Sarah : Farah = 4 : 7. Marah is 12 years older than Sarah and 6 years younger than Farah. Farah’s age four years ago? Farah minus Sarah = 12 + 6 = 18 years = 7 - 4 = 3 units, so 1 unit = 6. Farah = 42, four years ago = 38.
### A2. Same two people, two dates (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** Two ratios or multiples for the same pair at two dates, and both people are shifted by the same years each time. “Seven years hence 2 : 1, three years hence 5 : 2.” Percent words count too: “150% more” is 5 : 2.
**Shortcut engine: equal-gap method.**
1. Write both ratios in the same person order. Find each ratio’s gap (bigger part minus smaller part).
2. Multiply the ratios so both gaps become equal (use the LCM of the gaps).
3. Now one person’s parts change by some units between the two dates. Those units = the years between the dates. That gives 1 unit.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 24 Aug 2024, Shift 1).** Seven years hence A : B = 2 : 1; three years hence 5 : 2. Find B now. Gaps are 1 and 3, so write 2 : 1 as 6 : 3. A moves from 5 parts (3 years hence) to 6 parts (7 years hence): 1 unit = 4 years. B three years hence = 2 units = 8, so B now = 5.
**Unknown time variant (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2023).** “x years hence 7 : 5, x + 4 years hence 4 : 3, A is 26 now.” Gaps 2 and 1, so 4 : 3 becomes 8 : 6. A moves 7 to 8 = 1 unit = 4 years. A at the first date = 28, so x = 2. B at the first date = 20, so B now = 18.
### A3. Different time shift for each person (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “The ratio of A’s age five years hence to B’s age five years ago is 16 : 5”, or “A’s age 8 years hence is twice B’s age 2 years hence”. The two people are moved by different years, so the gap is not fixed and the equal-gap method fails.
**Shortcut engine: cross rule.** Let the base ratio be m : n, the target ratio r : s, and the shifts p for the first person and q for the second (hence is plus, ago is minus).
1. Top = r x q - s x p.
2. Bottom = s x m - r x n.
3. 1 unit = Top / Bottom. Ages on the base date are m units and n units.
This is the one-line balance s x (m units + p) = r x (n units + q) already solved for you. The cross rule is used because no pure ratio shortcut exists when the shifts differ.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 1).** A : B = 9 : 5 now. A five years hence to B five years ago = 16 : 5. Sum of ages after 10 years? m = 9, n = 5, r = 16, s = 5, p = +5, q = -5. Top = 16 x (-5) - 5 x 5 = -105. Bottom = 5 x 9 - 16 x 5 = -35. 1 unit = 3. Ages 27 and 15; after 10 years the sum is 42 + 20 = 62.
**No present ratio variant (SBI Clerk Prelims, 20 Sep 2025, Shift 2).** X ten years ago to Y six years ago = 2 : 3; X four years hence to Y six years hence = 5 : 6. Treat the first ratio as the base and count shifts from its dates: X moves from -10 to +4, so p = 14; Y moves from -6 to +6, so q = 12. Top = 5 x 12 - 6 x 14 = -24. Bottom = 6 x 2 - 5 x 3 = -3. 1 unit = 8. X = 16 + 10 = 26, Y = 24 + 6 = 30, difference 4. The paper carried no answer key for this question; 4 is my computed answer.
### A4. Shifted ratio plus a sum, average or gap (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** One ratio or multiple at shifted dates, plus a real sum, average or “older by” for the present. No second ratio.
**Shortcut engine: shift the total, or shift the gap.**
1. Apply each person’s own shift to the sum (or to the gap) so the number sits on the same dates as the ratio.
2. Shifted sum / sum of parts = 1 unit. Or shifted gap / difference of parts = 1 unit.
3. Undo the shift to get the present age.
**Worked example, sum (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2024, Guidely paper).** A four years ago to B six years hence = 2 : 3. Average of present ages 19. Find A. Present sum = 38. Shifted sum = 38 - 4 + 6 = 40 = 5 units, so 1 unit = 8. A four years ago = 16, A now = 20.
**Worked example, gap (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2016).** A three years ago to B five years ago = 4 : 5. A is 4 years younger than B. Find B. B five years ago minus A three years ago = 4 - 5 + 3 = 2 years = 1 unit. B five years ago = 10, B now = 15.
### A5. Three or more people linked, with a total (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** A third name appears with “older than”, “younger than” or “average of all three”. Often one ratio plus one “older by” plus an average.
**Shortcut engine: units plus extras = total.**
1. Convert the average to a total, and move the total to the date of the ratio.
2. Write each person as units plus an extra number of years. Add: (total units) + (total extras) = total.
3. Solve for 1 unit, then read the asked age and move it to the asked date.
If there is no ratio at all, pick the youngest person as the base: total = people x base + extras.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 12 Dec 2021, Set 1).** Five years ago Palani : Baba = 7 : 5. Ramar is 17 years older than Baba. Average of the three is 39. Baba’s age 2 years hence? Total now = 117; five years ago = 117 - 15 = 102. Then: 7 units + 5 units + (5 units + 17) = 102, so 17 units = 85 and 1 unit = 5. Baba then = 25, now = 30, two years hence = 32.
### B2. Family or group average (Seen once)
**Wording clues.** “Average age of a family of 5”, “a teacher joins”, “a child was born”, “one member leaves”.
**Shortcut engine: total bookkeeping and the deviation method.**
1. Work in totals: total = average x people. Each living person adds 1 year per year. A person not yet born adds nothing.
2. One person joins: new person = new average + (old count x rise in average).
**Worked example (IBPS PO Prelims, 23 Aug 2025, Shift 2, inside a quantity comparison).** 21 people average 64; one joins and the average becomes 64.5. New person = 64.5 + 21 x 0.5 = 75. Second part: 14 members average 30 and a baby is born now. Average after 4 years = (420 + 14 x 4 + 4) / 15 = 480 / 15 = 32.
### B5. “Cannot be determined” check (Seen once)
**Wording clues.** One option says “Cannot be determined”. Three people but the facts feel thin.
**Shortcut engine: count fresh facts.** You need as many fresh facts as people. A sentence that can be built from earlier sentences is not fresh.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2022, Adda247 paper).** Six years ago Amit + Den = 20, so now Amit + Den = 32. Mohit is 6 younger than Amit. Average of Mohit and Den is 13, so Mohit + Den = 26. But Mohit + Den = (Amit - 6) + Den = 32 - 6 = 26 already. The third sentence is not fresh: three people, two fresh facts. Answer: cannot be determined.
### B1. Product of ages (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Parts x parts = parts squared. Ages 2 : 3 with product 864: 2 units x 3 units = 6 square units = 864, so unit squared = 144 and 1 unit = 12. Three ages multiplied give unit cubed. For “sum and product”, move the sum to the product’s date and find the factor pair with that sum.
### B3. Spoken-statement puzzles (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Translate the sentence into a ratio or gap using the phrase table in Section 4, then use the unit method. Example: “I am twice as old as you were when I was as old as you are now” always gives 4 : 3. With a sum of 63, 7 units = 63, so the speaker is 36.
### B4. One person across time, or “after how many years” (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** The years between two dates equal the difference in parts. “His age after 15 years will be 5 times his age 5 years ago”: the two dates are 20 years apart = 5 - 1 = 4 units, so 1 unit = 5. He was 5 then and is 10 now. For two known ages (25 and 17) and a wanted ratio 5 : 3: the gap 8 = 2 units, so the ages were 20 and 12, which was 5 years ago.
### B6. An x or a blank inside the data (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Ignore the x at first. Solve the people with the engines above, then read x at the end. Example (Sreedhar model test 53): Karan is x + 7 and Riya is x - 1; after 5 years the ratio is 19 : 15. The gap is 8 years = 4 units, so 1 unit = 2. Karan after 5 years = 38, so x + 12 = 38 and x = 26.
## 3. Drill Set
Nine original questions, one per Core archetype plus the product and phrasing traps. Every answer and every elimination hack was checked by script. Try each before reading the method.
### Drill 1. Easy cutoff-buster (A1)
The ratio of the present ages of Meena and Tara is 5 : 8. Kiran is 9 years older than Meena and 6 years younger than Tara. Find the age of Tara five years ago (in years).
1. 30 (B) 35 (C) 40 (D) 45 (E) 25
- **Conventional method.** Let the ages be 5x and 8x. Then 5x + 9 = 8x - 6, so x = 5. Tara = 40, five years ago 35.
- **30-second exam method.** Kiran sits between them, so Tara - Meena = 9 + 6 = 15 years. That gap is 8 - 5 = 3 units, so 1 unit = 5. Tara = 8 x 5 = 40. Five years ago = 35. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Tara’s present age must be a multiple of 8, so (option + 5) must divide by 8. Only 35 + 5 = 40 works. Back-check: Meena 25, Kiran 34 = 25 + 9 = 40 - 6. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (C) 40 is Tara’s present age. Read the last line again: “five years ago”.
### Drill 2. Two-step moderate (A2)
Four years ago, the ratio of the ages of P and Q was 5 : 3. Eight years hence, the ratio of their ages will be 4 : 3. Find the present age of Q (in years).
1. 12 (B) 16 (C) 18 (D) 20 (E) 24
- **Conventional method.** Two equations: 3(P - 4) = 5(Q - 4) and 3(P + 8) = 4(Q + 8). Solve together.
- **30-second exam method.** Gaps are 2 and 1. Double the second ratio: 4 : 3 becomes 8 : 6. Now P goes from 5 parts to 8 parts = 3 units in 4 + 8 = 12 years, so 1 unit = 4. Q four years ago = 3 units = 12. Q now = 16. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** (Q - 4) and (Q + 8) must both divide by 3. Only 16 passes (12 and 24). Back-check: P = 24; eight years hence 32 : 24 = 4 : 3. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** The years between the dates are 4 + 8 = 12, not 8. “Ago” and “hence” add up. Option (A) 12 is Q’s age four years ago.
### Drill 3. Product, non-linear trap (B1)
The ratio of the present ages of A and B is 3 : 4 and the product of their present ages is 432. Find the sum of their ages five years hence (in years).
1. 42 (B) 47 (C) 52 (D) 57 (E) 62
- **Conventional method.** 3x times 4x = 432, so 12x squared = 432, x squared = 36, x = 6.
- **30-second exam method.** Parts x parts = parts squared. 3 x 4 = 12 square units = 432, so unit squared = 36 and 1 unit = 6. Ages 18 and 24, sum 42. Two people, five years each: 42 + 10 = 52. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The present sum is 7 units, so (option - 10) must divide by 7. Only 52 - 10 = 42 works. Back-check: 18 x 24 = 432. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** 432 / 12 = 36 is the unit squared, not the unit. Take the square root. Option (A) 42 is the present sum; option (B) 47 adds five years for only one person.
### Drill 4. Phrasing trap (A2 with percent words)
Six years ago, Ravi’s age was 200% more than Sonu’s age. Ten years hence, Ravi’s age will be 66 2/3% more than Sonu’s age. Find the present age of Sonu (in years).
1. 10 (B) 12 (C) 14 (D) 16 (E) 18
- **Conventional method.** R - 6 = 3(S - 6) and R + 10 = (5/3)(S + 10). Solve together.
- **30-second exam method.** 200% more = 3 : 1. 66 2/3% more = 5 : 3. Gaps are 2 and 2, already equal. Ravi goes from 3 parts to 5 parts = 2 units in 6 + 10 = 16 years, so 1 unit = 8. Sonu six years ago = 1 unit = 8. Sonu now = 14. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** (Sonu + 10) must divide by 3. Only 14 + 10 = 24 works. Back-check: Ravi = 30; ten years hence 40 : 24 = 5 : 3. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** “200% more than” is 3 times, not 2 times. “200% of” would be 2 times. The same trap appears as “two times more than”.
### Drill 5. Different shifts (A3)
The ratio of the present ages of Anil and Bina is 7 : 4. The ratio of Anil’s age two years ago to Bina’s age six years hence is 4 : 3. Find the present age of Bina (in years).
1. 16 (B) 20 (C) 24 (D) 28 (E) 32
- **Conventional method.** Let the ages be 7x and 4x. Then 3(7x - 2) = 4(4x + 6), so 5x = 30 and x = 6.
- **30-second exam method.** Cross rule with m : n = 7 : 4, r : s = 4 : 3, p = -2, q = +6. Top = 4 x 6 - 3 x (-2) = 30. Bottom = 3 x 7 - 4 x 4 = 5. 1 unit = 6. Bina = 4 x 6 = 24. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** (Bina + 6) must divide by 3. Only 24 + 6 = 30 works. Back-check: Anil = 42; 40 : 30 = 4 : 3. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** The equal-gap method does not work here because the two people are moved by different years. Also watch the sign: “ago” is minus.
### Drill 6. Shift the total (A4)
The ratio of Kavya’s age three years ago to Lata’s age seven years hence is 3 : 5. The average of their present ages is 26 years. Find the present age of Kavya (in years).
1. 20 (B) 22 (C) 24 (D) 26 (E) 28
- **Conventional method.** 5(K - 3) = 3(L + 7) and K + L = 52. Solve together.
- **30-second exam method.** Present sum = 26 x 2 = 52. Shifted sum = 52 - 3 + 7 = 56 = 3 + 5 = 8 units, so 1 unit = 7. Kavya three years ago = 21. Kavya now = 24. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** (Kavya - 3) must divide by 3. Only 24 - 3 = 21 works. Back-check: Lata = 28; 21 : 35 = 3 : 5. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** 26 is the average, not the sum. Option (E) 28 is Lata’s age.
### Drill 7. Three people with a total (A5)
Five years ago, the ratio of the ages of Uma and Vani was 4 : 3. Wasim is 14 years older than Vani. If the average of the present ages of all three is 33 years, find the age of Wasim two years hence (in years).
1. 38 (B) 40 (C) 42 (D) 44 (E) 46
- **Conventional method.** Let the ages five years ago be 4x and 3x. Then (4x + 5) + (3x + 5) + (3x + 19) = 99, so x = 7.
- **30-second exam method.** Total now = 99. Five years ago = 99 - 3 x 5 = 84. Then Uma 4 units, Vani 3 units, Wasim 3 units + 14. So 10 units + 14 = 84 and 1 unit = 7. Wasim then = 35, now = 40, two years hence = 42. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Vani’s age five years ago = option - 2 - 5 - 14 = option - 21, and it must divide by 3. Only 42 - 21 = 21 works. Back-check: 33 + 26 + 40 = 99. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Three people each lose 5 years, so subtract 15, not 5. Option (B) 40 is Wasim’s present age.
### Drill 8. Family average (B2)
Four years ago, the average age of a husband, his wife and their son was 26 years. Six years ago, the average age of the wife and the son was 18 years. Find the present age of the husband (in years).
1. 36 (B) 38 (C) 40 (D) 42 (E) 44
- **Conventional method.** H + W + S - 12 = 78 and W + S - 12 = 36. Subtract.
- **30-second exam method.** Total of three now = 26 x 3 + 4 x 3 = 90. Wife and son now = 18 x 2 + 6 x 2 = 48. Husband = 90 - 48 = 42. **Answer (D).**
- **Option elimination hack.** No safe divisibility hack here. Two multiplications and one subtraction are faster than testing options.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Add the years once per person: 4 x 3 = 12 and 6 x 2 = 12. Adding 4 and 6 only once gives a wrong total.
### Drill 9. Cannot be determined (B5)
Six years ago, the sum of the ages of Asha and Dev was 34 years. Mohan is 4 years younger than Asha. If the average of the present ages of Mohan and Dev is 21 years, find the present age of Dev.
1. 20 years (B) 22 years (C) 24 years (D) Cannot be determined (E) 26 years
- **Conventional method.** A + D = 46, M = A - 4, M + D = 42. Three unknowns, try to solve.
- **30-second exam method.** Asha + Dev now = 34 + 12 = 46. Mohan + Dev = 42. But Mohan + Dev = (Asha - 4) + Dev = 46 - 4 = 42 already. The last sentence is not fresh, so only two facts exist for three people. **Answer (D).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Test two options. Dev 20 gives Asha 26, Mohan 22, and 22 + 20 = 42 fits. Dev 22 gives Asha 24, Mohan 20, and 20 + 22 = 42 also fits. Two options fit, so the answer cannot be fixed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Do not pick “Cannot be determined” just because the question looks long. Pick it only after you show that one sentence repeats another.
## 4. Cheat Sheet & 30-Second Recall Matrix
### Which engine to use
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>What the question gives</td>
<td>Engine</td>
<td>Key step</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Present ratio + sum, gap or one age</td>
<td>Unit method</td>
<td>Number / matching parts = 1 unit</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Same pair at two dates, same shift for both</td>
<td>Equal-gap</td>
<td>Make gaps equal; change in parts = years between dates</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Each person shifted by different years, two ratios</td>
<td>Cross rule</td>
<td>Unit = (r x q - s x p) / (s x m - r x n)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>One shifted ratio + present sum, average or gap</td>
<td>Shift the total or gap</td>
<td>Apply each person’s shift to the number first</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Three or more people with an average</td>
<td>Units + extras = total</td>
<td>Average x people, move the total, then add up</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Family, class, member joins or is born</td>
<td>Total bookkeeping</td>
<td>New person = new average + old count x rise</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Cannot be determined” among options</td>
<td>Count fresh facts</td>
<td>Need one fresh fact per person</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Product of ages</td>
<td>Parts squared</td>
<td>Divide, then take the square root</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Universal fallback
When a question fits no engine, use the **one-date balance**. Put every person on one single date as units plus extra years. Use the one real number in the question to write a single balance line, and solve for 1 unit. If even that is unclear, plug in the middle option: build every age from it and test the sentence you have not used yet. Move up or down from there.
### Phrase to maths
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Phrase in the question</td>
<td>Meaning</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“N years hence”, “after N years”</td>
<td>Add N to that person</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“N years ago”, “before N years”</td>
<td>Subtract N from that person</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A is twice as old as B”</td>
<td>A : B = 2 : 1</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A is two times more than B”, “200% more”</td>
<td>A : B = 3 : 1</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A is 150% of B”</td>
<td>A : B = 3 : 2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Father was 28 when the son was born”</td>
<td>Gap = 28</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“I was as old as you are now when you were born”</td>
<td>Speaker = 2 x listener</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A is as much younger than B as he is older than C”</td>
<td>A = average of B and C; the gap itself stays unknown</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“I am twice as old as you were when I was as old as you are now”</td>
<td>Speaker : listener = 4 : 3</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“X celebrated his 16th birthday 4 years ago”</td>
<td>X = 20 now</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Present age is 6/5 of the age at marriage 8 years ago”</td>
<td>6 - 5 = 1 unit = 8 years</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Children born at intervals of 3 years”</td>
<td>Youngest, +3, +6, +9 …</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Sum of ages of A and B after 5 years”</td>
<td>Present sum + 10</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Difference of ages after 12 years is 7”</td>
<td>Present difference is also 7</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Average of A, B and C is 33”</td>
<td>Total = 99</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Ratio of B and A” after an earlier “A and B”</td>
<td>Order has flipped; rewrite it</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Red flags
- **Wrong date.** The question often asks an age “4 years hence” or “5 years ago”. The present age is usually sitting among the options.
- **Wrong person.** Finding A when B was asked. Similar names are used on purpose (one bank question uses Yuvan and Yuvansh as two people).
- **Average used as sum.** Multiply by the number of people first.
- **Years added once for a group.** Three people over 5 years add 15.
- **Parts squared.** A product gives unit squared; take the root.
- **Fractional ages are allowed.** One IBPS Clerk 2025 paper had the answer 42.5 years. Use divisibility hacks only when every option is a whole number, never when “None of these” is an option, and always back-check.
- **Equal-gap misuse.** It works only when both people move by the same years.

## 6. Verification Log
Built 5 Oct 2026. This module is checked, not guaranteed; a new exam variant can still fall outside it.
### What was tested
- **Answers.** All 266 bank questions were rewritten as relations and solved by script. 258 match the answer key. 7 could not be machine-solved (cannot-be-determined items, a fill-in-the-blanks item and a “possible total” item) and were checked by hand; all 7 agree with the key. 1 does not match (see below).
- **Methods.** The three mechanical engines were run by script on every question that uses them and compared with the solved ages: equal-gap on 45, shift the total or gap on 35, cross rule on 20. All 100 pass. The unit method, units + extras and total bookkeeping are direct arithmetic, so their only check is the answer check.
- **Sister-exam questions.** All 11 were solved by script. Ten match the key printed in the paper. The SBI Clerk 20 Sep 2025 Shift 2 paper printed no key; the answer 4 is computed.
- **Drills and ladder.** All 9 drill answers were solved by script, and each divisibility hack was tested to leave exactly one option. All 36 ladder answers were re-checked against the solver.
### What failed and was fixed
- The equal-gap method fails when the two people are shifted by different years. Those questions were split into archetype A3 with the cross rule.
- The cross rule was first written only for “present ratio given”. The SBI Clerk question with two shifted ratios broke it. It was rewritten to count shifts from the first ratio’s dates and re-tested on 7 such questions.
- “Cannot be determined” and “x inside the data” started as one archetype and were split, because only the first has PYQ support.
- Drills 2 and 4 first had two options passing the divisibility test. One option in each was changed.
### Stated exceptions in the bank
- **S-MT42-38 (Sreedhar).** The question asks Varun’s age 9 years ago, which is 18 (option A). The key says 12, which is Arun’s age 9 years ago. Treat the key as wrong or the question as mistyped.
- **G-relation-and-based-on-family-6.** Exact answer is 193/7 = 27.57; the keyed option is 27.50.
- **G-older-younger-8.** Exact answer is 83.33%; the keyed option is 83%.
- **G-three-persons-or-more-4.** The key (37) needs “average of B and C” read as their present average. Read as their average nine years hence, the answer is 25, which is not an option.
- **G-twice-thrice-n-times-3.** Uses Yuvan and Yuvansh as two different people. The key is right only with that reading.
- **PYQ-AGE-04.** The bank key (18) matches the maths. A web read of the source PDF returned option (b) 12, but that file is in Hindi and the text extraction was garbled. Not resolved.
### What could not be opened or checked
- 18 of the 21 IBPS Clerk source PDFs were not re-opened. Those questions are used as transcribed in your bank. The 3 opened (4 Oct 2025 Shift 1, 5 Oct 2025 Shift 1, 24 Aug 2024 Shift 1) matched the bank’s numbers.
- Two pages were blocked: govtskill.com (IBPS PO Prelims 2024) and learntheta.com (IBPS Clerk 5 Oct 2025).
- Three opened pages had no Ages question: IBPS RRB Clerk 6 Dec 2025 Shift 2, IBPS PO 24 Aug 2025 Shift 1, and the PW SBI Clerk memory-based page.
- No Mains papers were found or used. This module reflects Prelims only.
- Web PDFs were read through a text-extraction tool, so quoted wording may differ slightly from the PDF.
- Paper counts use paper labels. Undated labels such as “IBPS Clerk Prelims 2024, Guidely” may be the same paper as a dated one; this was not resolved.
- The method books and channels in your Priority 3 list were not read or watched. The methods here are the standard ratio, unit and deviation methods those sources are known for.

# Simple & Compound Interest - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened on the web: 12 sister-exam papers (4 SBI Clerk Prelims, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims, 4 IBPS PO Prelims); 11 of them had an interest question. PYQs found: 20 IBPS Clerk Prelims questions from 18 paper labels (2016-2025, taken from your bank) plus 12 sister-exam questions. Bank questions used: 414 (30 constructed, 311 Guidely, 73 Sreedhar; 25 are repeats and 1 is a misfiled profit-and-loss question). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts is met; the sister count (12) is just above the 6-10 target. All papers are memory-based reconstructions.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Exact Mechanics & Conversion Grid
**Central law.** Call the sum invested 100%. In simple interest (SI) every year adds the same slice, so total interest % = rate x years. In compound interest (CI) each year’s interest is charged on the grown amount, so the yearly increases multiply instead of adding. Almost every question is solved by finding what 1% of the sum is worth.
### Base operations
1. **SI total percent.** Interest % = rate x years. 12% for 5 years = 60% of the sum. Amount = 100% + that.
2. **Sum from interest.** 1% of the sum = interest / (rate x years). Interest 2,880 at 20% for 6 years: 120% = 2,880, so 1% = 24 and the sum is 2,400.
3. **Rate or time from interest.** Interest as a % of the sum, divided by the one you know. Interest 1,800 on 4,000 in 3 years: 45% / 3 = 15% a year.
4. **CI for 2 years.** Effective % = 2 x rate + (rate x rate) / 100. At 20%: 40 + 4 = 44%. Multiply the sum by this once.
5. **CI for 2 years at two different rates a and b.** Effective % = a + b + (a x b) / 100. At 10% then 15%: 25 + 1.5 = 26.5%.
6. **CI for 3 years.** Use the table below, or apply the 2-year % and then one more year.
7. **Months and days.** Time = months / 12 or days / 365. 146 days = 2/5 year, 73 days = 1/5 year, 219 days = 3/5 year.
8. **Half-yearly and quarterly.** Half-yearly: halve the rate, double the number of periods. Quarterly: quarter the rate, four periods a year.

Use the fraction table backwards: an amount of 12,167 after 3 years at 15% is 23 x 23 x 23 parts, so the sum is 20 x 20 x 20 = 8,000.
### Calculation facts to keep ready
- Squares of 11 to 25 and cubes of 11, 12, 21, 23: 1,331; 1,728; 9,261; 12,167.
- Half-yearly at 20% for one year is the same 21% as 10% for two years. Quarterly at 20% for one year is 21.55%; for nine months 15.7625%.
- Non-linear warning: CI does not scale with the rate. Doubling the rate from 10% to 20% takes 2-year CI from 21% to 44%, more than double.
## 2. Archetypes
Sixteen archetypes cover the bank and the sister-exam questions. In this sample the papers ask SI far more than CI, and CI almost always arrives as a 2-year calculation after an SI step. The classic “difference between CI and SI” formula question did not appear in any of the 30 papers; it is Insurance only. “Papers” counts paper labels.

Worked examples below are real questions from the papers.
### L1. SI first, then CI (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “A man invested Rs X at simple interest … and received Rs … as interest. If he invests the same amount (or X + something, or at R + 5%) at compound interest for two years, find …”
**Shortcut engine: 1% value, then the 2-year table.**
1. SI part: total % = rate x years. Divide to get the sum (or the rate).
2. Adjust the sum or rate as the question says.
3. CI part: multiply by the 2-year effective % for interest, or by 100% + that for amount.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 3).** A sum at 10% SI for 3 years earns 1,800. Amount at 20% CI for 2 years? 30% = 1,800, so the sum is 6,000. At 20% for 2 years the amount is 144% = 8,640.
**Rate version (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2023, Adda247 paper).** 4,000 earns 1,800 in 3 years at R% SI. Interest on 5,000 at (R - 5)% CI for 2 years? 1,800 / 4,000 = 45% in 3 years, so R = 15. New rate 10%: 21% of 5,000 = 1,050.
### I4. Two SI deposits (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** Two schemes, two people or one sum split in two parts, all at simple interest. The question gives the total, the difference or the ratio of the two interests.
**Shortcut engine: one percent per deposit.**
1. For each deposit write its total % (rate x years).
2. Turn the sentence into one balance: total, difference or ratio of the two interests.
3. If one sum is “X + 1,400”, take the % of the extra 1,400 out as a plain number first.
**Worked example (SBI Clerk Prelims, 21 Sep 2025, Shift 1).** Scheme X: 3,000 at 6% for 9 years. Scheme Y: P at 12% for 6 years. Y’s interest is 1,260 more. X earns 54% of 3,000 = 1,620. Y earns 1,620 + 1,260 = 2,880 = 72% of P, so 1% = 40 and P = 4,000.
**Extra-sum version (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2017).** X at 8% and (X + 1,400) at 12%, both for 2 years; interests differ by 880. Second earns 24% of X plus 24% of 1,400 = 336. First earns 16% of X. So 8% of X + 336 = 880, 8% of X = 544, X = 6,800.
### I2. Becomes n times, or interest is a fraction of the sum (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “Doubles in 5 years”, “becomes 1.6 times”, “interest is three-fifths of the amount invested”, “interest is 16/25 of the principal and years equal the rate”.
**Shortcut engine.** Becoming n times means interest = (n - 1) x 100% of the sum. At SI, time is proportional to (n - 1).
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 4).** A sum doubles in 5 years at SI. When is it 12 times? Doubling needs 1 sum of interest in 5 years. Twelve times needs 11 sums: 11 x 5 = 55 years.
**Fraction version (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 5 Oct 2025, Shift 1).** 3,400 at 12% for (x + 1) years earns three-fifths of the sum. Three-fifths = 60%; 60 / 12 = 5 years; x = 4.
### L2. SI against CI, or two CI deposits (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** The same sum, or two sums in a given ratio, placed in two schemes of which at least one is CI. The question gives the total or the difference of the two interests.
**Shortcut engine: effective percent on each side.**
1. Replace each scheme by one %: SI = rate x years; CI = table value.
2. Same sum: the difference (or total) of the two % equals the given rupees. Sums in a ratio: multiply each part by its % and add.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 24 Aug 2024, Shift 1).** X at 20% CI for 2 years and X at 30% SI for 2 years; interests differ by 896. CI = 44%, SI = 60%. 16% = 896, so 1% = 56 and X = 5,600.
**Ratio version (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 5 Dec 2020).** Sums 2 : 3 at 20% and 10% CI for 2 years; total interest 1,208. Interest = 2 x 44% + 3 x 21% = 0.88 + 0.63 = 1.51 parts = 1,208. 1 part = 800. Total = 5 parts = 4,000.
### I3. Same sum, rate or time changed (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “Had the rate been 5% higher”, “at (X + 5)% for 3 years”, “is Rs 1,500 more than the interest at 12%”.
**Shortcut engine.** Only the change in total % matters: extra interest = sum x (new rate x new years - old rate x old years)%.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2016).** SI at 15% for 5 years is 1,500 more than at 12% for 5 years. Extra = 3% x 5 = 15% = 1,500. Sum = 10,000.
### I1. Plain SI (Confirmed)
**Shortcut engine.** Total % = rate x years, then 1% value.
**Worked example (IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims, 13 Dec 2025, Shift 1).** P at 20% for 6 years earns 2,880. Find P - 200. 120% = 2,880, 1% = 24, P = 2,400, answer 2,200.
### K1. Plain CI (Confirmed, IBPS PO only)
**Shortcut engine.** 2-year or 3-year effective % from the table. To go backwards from an amount, use the fraction table.
**Worked example (IBPS PO Prelims, 22 Aug 2026, Shift 2).** P at 25% CI for 2 years. Amount minus interest = 2,000. Find the CI on (P + 800). Amount minus interest is simply the sum, so P = 2,000. 56.25% of 2,800 = 1,575.
### I5. SI amounts at two dates (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** The gap between the two amounts is pure interest for the years in between. Find one year’s interest, then subtract back to year zero.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2023, Guidely paper).** Amount 6,960 after 2 years and 8,400 after 5 years. Three years’ interest = 1,440, so 480 a year. Sum = 6,960 - 2 x 480 = 6,000.
### K8. Rate changes from year to year (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** SI: add the yearly rates into one total %. CI: use a + b + ab/100 for two years, or multiply the yearly factors.
**Worked example (IBPS PO Prelims, 23 Aug 2025, Shift 2).** SI on 6,000: 10% for 2 years, then 15% for 3 years. Total = 20 + 45 = 65% = 3,900.
### K9. Reinvested in a second scheme (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** Work scheme by scheme. Read carefully whether the interest or the whole amount moves on. Going backwards, undo the last scheme first.
**Worked example (IBPS PO Prelims, 24 Aug 2025, Shift 1).** 12,500 at r% SI for 2 years; the interest goes into 10% CI for 2 years and becomes 3,630. Undo the CI: 121% = 3,630, so the interest was 3,000. 3,000 / 12,500 = 24% in 2 years, so r = 12.
### K2. Difference between CI and SI (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Two years: difference = rate% of rate% of the sum. Three years: difference = sum x (rate/100) squared x (3 + rate/100).
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Rate</td>
<td>2-year difference per 100</td>
<td>3-year difference per 100</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>5%</td>
<td>0.25</td>
<td>0.7625</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>8%</td>
<td>0.64</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>10%</td>
<td>1</td>
<td>3.1</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>12%</td>
<td>1.44</td>
<td></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>15%</td>
<td>2.25</td>
<td>7.0875</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>20%</td>
<td>4</td>
<td>12.8</td>
</tr>
</table>
Example: difference 240 for 2 years at 20%. 4% of the sum = 240, so the sum is 6,000. To find the rate instead: (rate/100) squared = difference / sum, then take the square root.
### K3. SI and CI both given for 2 years (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** CI minus SI is one year’s interest on the first year’s SI. Rate = (CI - SI) / (half of SI). Example: SI 800, CI 832. First-year SI = 400; extra 32 is 8% of 400, so the rate is 8% and the sum is 400 / 8% = 5,000.
### K4. Half-yearly or quarterly compounding (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Convert, then use the normal table. 20% half-yearly for 1 year = 10% for 2 periods = 21%. 20% quarterly for 9 months = 5% for 3 periods = 15.7625%. 40% quarterly for 1 year = 10% for 4 periods = 46.41%.
### K5. CI becomes n times (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** At CI, times multiply while years add. Doubles in 4 years, so 8 times = 2 x 2 x 2 takes 12 years. Non-linear warning: at SI, 3 times in 15 years means 5 times in 30 years; at CI it would be 9 times in 30 years.
### K6. CI amounts or interests of consecutive years (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** The jump from one year’s amount to the next is exactly the rate. 4,840 after 2 years and 5,324 after 3: the rise 484 is 10% of 4,840. Sum = 4,840 x 100 / 121 = 4,000. Yearly interests grow the same way: 660 then 726 means 10%.
### K7. Instalments and loan repayment (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Equal yearly instalments: write the fraction ratio and add the present values. At 10% for two instalments, an instalment of 121 parts pays off 110 + 100 = 210 parts of loan, so instalment = loan x 121 / 210 (6,300 gives 3,630). For three instalments at 10%: loan x 1,331 / 3,310. Unequal part-payments: roll forward one year at a time (add interest, subtract the payment).
## 3. Drill Set
Eleven original questions: one per Core archetype plus the non-linear trap. Every answer was checked by script. Try each before reading the method.
### Drill 1. Easy cutoff-buster (I1)
A man invested Rs P at 15% p.a. simple interest for 4 years and earned Rs 2,700 as interest. Find the value of P + 500.
1. 4,500 (B) 5,000 (C) 5,500 (D) 4,000 (E) 5,200
- **Conventional method.** P x 15 x 4 / 100 = 2,700, so P = 4,500.
- **30-second exam method.** Total = 15 x 4 = 60%. 60% = 2,700, so 1% = 45 and P = 4,500. P + 500 = 5,000. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** No divisibility shortcut is needed; one division finishes it.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 4,500 is P itself. The question asks P + 500.
### Drill 2. Two-step moderate (L1)
A man invested Rs X at 12% p.a. simple interest for 5 years and received Rs 3,000 as interest. If he invests Rs (X + 1,000) at 20% p.a. compound interest for 2 years, find the interest received (in Rs).
1. 2,400 (B) 2,520 (C) 2,640 (D) 2,880 (E) 2,300
- **Conventional method.** X = 3,000 x 100 / 60 = 5,000. CI = 6,000 x (1.2 x 1.2 - 1) = 2,640.
- **30-second exam method.** 60% = 3,000, so X = 5,000. New sum 6,000. 20% for 2 years = 44%. 44% of 6,000 = 2,640. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The answer is 44% of a whole number of hundreds, so it must divide by 44. Only 2,640 does. Back-check: 2,640 / 44 x 100 = 6,000 = X + 1,000. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 2,400 is simple interest at 20% for 2 years. CI adds the 4% “interest on interest”.
### Drill 3. Non-linear trap (K2, Insurance)
The difference between the compound interest and the simple interest on a certain sum for 2 years at 15% p.a. is Rs 180. Find the sum (in Rs).
1. 6,000 (B) 8,000 (C) 9,000 (D) 12,000 (E) 4,000
- **Conventional method.** P x (1.15 x 1.15 - 1) - P x 0.30 = 180, so 0.0225 P = 180.
- **30-second exam method.** Two-year difference = 15% of 15% = 2.25% of the sum. 2.25% = 180, so 1% = 80 and the sum is 8,000. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Test 15% of 15% on the middle option: 15% of 8,000 = 1,200; 15% of 1,200 = 180. It fits at once.
- **IBPS trap warning.** The difference depends on the rate squared. If the rate doubles to 30%, the difference becomes four times (9%), not double.
### Drill 4. Phrasing trap (I2)
A sum of money becomes 2.5 times itself in 6 years at simple interest. In how many years will it become 6 times itself at the same rate?
1. 14.4 (B) 15 (C) 18 (D) 20 (E) 24
- **Conventional method.** 1.5 P = P x r x 6 / 100, so r = 25%. Then 5 P = P x 25 x t / 100, so t = 20.
- **30-second exam method.** “2.5 times” means interest of 1.5 sums in 6 years. “6 times” means interest of 5 sums. Time = 6 x 5 / 1.5 = 20 years. **Answer (D).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None. The proportion is one line.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 14.4 comes from 6 x 6 / 2.5, which forgets to remove the original sum. Always subtract 1 from “times” first.
### Drill 5. Rate and time both changed (I3)
The simple interest on a certain sum at 9% p.a. for 4 years is Rs 1,440 less than the simple interest on the same sum at 12% p.a. for 5 years. Find the sum (in Rs).
1. 4,800 (B) 6,000 (C) 7,200 (D) 5,400 (E) 8,000
- **Conventional method.** P x 60 / 100 - P x 36 / 100 = 1,440.
- **30-second exam method.** First case 9 x 4 = 36%. Second case 12 x 5 = 60%. Gap 24% = 1,440, so 1% = 60 and the sum is 6,000. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Do not subtract the rates first. 3% x 4 years gives 12,000 and 3% x 5 years gives 9,600; both are wrong because the years also changed.
### Drill 6. Two SI deposits with a ratio (I4)
Scheme A offers simple interest at 8% p.a. for 5 years and the amount invested in it is Rs 4,500. Scheme B offers simple interest at 12% p.a. for 4 years and the amount invested in it is Rs P. If the ratio of the interest received from scheme A to that from scheme B is 5 : 8, find P.
1. 5,000 (B) 5,400 (C) 6,000 (D) 6,400 (E) 7,200
- **Conventional method.** (4,500 x 40) / (P x 48) = 5 / 8. Cross-multiply.
- **30-second exam method.** A earns 40% of 4,500 = 1,800. That is 5 parts, so 1 part = 360 and B earns 8 parts = 2,880. B’s total is 48%, so 1% = 60 and P = 6,000. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Check the order of the ratio. “A to B is 5 : 8” puts A on 5. Reversing it gives 2,343.75, which is not an option, so a strange number is a signal to re-read.
### Drill 7. Amounts at two dates (I5)
A sum of money amounts to Rs 5,520 in 3 years and to Rs 6,240 in 6 years at simple interest. Find the rate of interest per annum.
1. 4% (B) 5% (C) 6% (D) 7.5% (E) 8%
- **Conventional method.** P + 3I = 5,520 and P + 6I = 6,240. Solve for I and P.
- **30-second exam method.** Three extra years add 720, so one year’s interest is 240. Sum = 5,520 - 3 x 240 = 4,800. Rate = 240 / 4,800 = 5%. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Divide the yearly interest by the original sum, not by 5,520. The amount already contains interest.
### Drill 8. SI against CI on the same sum (L2)
A man invested Rs X in scheme A at 10% p.a. compound interest (compounded annually) for 2 years and the same amount in scheme B at 12% p.a. simple interest for 2 years. If the difference between the interests is Rs 180, find X.
1. 4,500 (B) 5,000 (C) 6,000 (D) 7,500 (E) 9,000
- **Conventional method.** X x 0.24 - X x 0.21 = 180.
- **30-second exam method.** CI at 10% for 2 years = 21%. SI = 12 x 2 = 24%. Gap 3% = 180, so 1% = 60 and X = 6,000. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** CI is not always the bigger one. Here the SI scheme has the higher rate and earns more, so subtract in the right direction.
### Drill 9. Rate changes with the years (K8)
A man borrowed a sum at simple interest. The rate is 6% p.a. for the first 2 years, 9% p.a. for the next 3 years and 12% p.a. for the period beyond 5 years. If he pays Rs 9,000 as total interest at the end of 8 years, find the sum borrowed (in Rs).
1. 10,000 (B) 12,000 (C) 12,500 (D) 15,000 (E) 9,000
- **Conventional method.** P x (6 x 2 + 9 x 3 + 12 x 3) / 100 = 9,000.
- **30-second exam method.** Total = 12 + 27 + 36 = 75%. 75% = 9,000, so 1% = 120 and the sum is 12,000. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** “Beyond 5 years” up to 8 years is 3 years, not 8. Count each slab’s own years.
### Drill 10. Interest reinvested (K9)
A man invested Rs 10,000 in scheme A at r% p.a. simple interest for 3 years. He invested the interest received from scheme A in scheme B at 20% p.a. compound interest for 2 years. If the amount received from scheme B is Rs 4,320, find r.
1. 8 (B) 9 (C) 10 (D) 12 (E) 15
- **Conventional method.** I x 1.44 = 4,320 gives I = 3,000. Then 10,000 x r x 3 / 100 = 3,000.
- **30-second exam method.** Undo scheme B first: 144% = 4,320, so the interest invested was 3,000. That is 30% of 10,000 over 3 years, so r = 10. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Only the interest moved to scheme B, not the whole 13,000. And 4,320 is an amount, so divide by 144%, not 44%.
### Drill 11. Plain CI with a twist (K1)
A man invested Rs P at 30% p.a. compound interest for 2 years. The difference between the amount received and the compound interest earned is Rs 3,000. If he had invested Rs (P - 500) at the same rate for the same period, find the compound interest received (in Rs).
1. 1,500 (B) 1,650 (C) 1,725 (D) 1,800 (E) 2,070
- **Conventional method.** Amount - CI = P, so P = 3,000. CI on 2,500 = 2,500 x (1.3 x 1.3 - 1).
- **30-second exam method.** Amount minus interest is the sum itself: P = 3,000. New sum 2,500. 30% for 2 years = 69%. 69% of 2,500 = 1,725. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 1,500 is simple interest (60%). Option (E) 2,070 is the CI on the full 3,000.
## 4. Cheat Sheet & 30-Second Recall Matrix
### Which engine to use
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>What the question gives</td>
<td>Engine</td>
<td>Key step</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>SI with one value missing</td>
<td>1% value</td>
<td>Total % = rate x years; divide</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>SI data, then “at compound interest for 2 years”</td>
<td>1% value, then table</td>
<td>Find sum or rate, then use 21%, 44%, 32.25% …</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two SI schemes, total, difference or ratio of interest</td>
<td>One % per deposit</td>
<td>Write each total %, then one balance</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Same sum, rate or years changed</td>
<td>Change in total %</td>
<td>(new rate x years - old rate x years)% = extra interest</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Becomes n times” at SI</td>
<td>Subtract 1</td>
<td>Time is proportional to (n - 1)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Amounts after two different years at SI</td>
<td>Yearly slice</td>
<td>Gap / years between, then subtract back</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Same sum at SI and at CI</td>
<td>Effective % each side</td>
<td>Difference of the two % = given rupees</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Sums in a ratio in two schemes</td>
<td>Parts x %</td>
<td>Add parts x their %, compare with the total</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Rates differ by year</td>
<td>Add (SI) or a + b + ab/100 (CI)</td>
<td>One total %</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Interest or amount moved to another scheme</td>
<td>Scheme by scheme</td>
<td>Backwards: undo the last scheme first</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>CI minus SI, same sum and rate</td>
<td>Rate% of rate%</td>
<td>2 years: r% of r% of the sum</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Universal fallback
When a question fits no engine, use the **100% line**. Call the unknown sum 100%. Walk through the question one step at a time, writing each interest or amount as a % of that sum, until you reach the rupee figure given. That rupee figure equals some % of the sum, so 1% is known and everything follows. If the unknown is a rate or a time instead, plug in the middle option and compare the result with the given figure.
### Phrase to maths
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Phrase in the question</td>
<td>Meaning</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Amount”</td>
<td>Sum + interest</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Difference between the amount and the interest”</td>
<td>The sum itself</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Becomes 3 times”, “triples”</td>
<td>Interest = 2 sums</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Becomes 1.6 times”</td>
<td>Interest = 60% of the sum</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Interest is 3/5 of the sum”</td>
<td>Interest = 60%; rate x years = 60</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Interest is 300% more than the investment”</td>
<td>Interest = 4 sums (bank usage)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Five times more than”</td>
<td>6 times (bank usage)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Number of years equals the rate” and interest is 16/25 of the sum</td>
<td>Rate squared = 64, so rate = 8</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Had the rate been 5% higher”</td>
<td>5 extra percentage points per year</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Rate is increased by 20%”</td>
<td>Unclear: either 20 points or 20% of the rate; test both against the options</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Interest for the 2nd year alone”</td>
<td>One year’s interest on the grown amount, not 2 years’ interest</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Compounded half-yearly”</td>
<td>Half the rate, twice the periods</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“p.c.p.a.”</td>
<td>Percent per annum</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“For 146 days”</td>
<td>146 / 365 = 2/5 year</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Invested the interest in scheme B” versus “invested the amount”</td>
<td>Only the interest moves, or the sum plus interest moves</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Red flags
- **Amount or interest.** Check which one the last line asks for. Both usually sit among the options.
- **CI is not linear.** You cannot halve or double CI when the rate or the years change. Recompute from the table.
- **CI is not always larger.** When the SI scheme has the higher rate, SI can earn more.
- **Answer is not the sum.** “Find P - 200” or “find 3.12X” appear in real papers; finish the last step.
- **Extra sum.** For “X + 1,400” take the interest on 1,400 out as a plain number before balancing.
- **Decimals are allowed.** Answers such as 1,814.4 or 7.5% are common here. Use divisibility hacks only when every option is whole and none is “None of these”, and always back-check.

## 6. Verification Log
Built 5 Oct 2026. This module is checked, not guaranteed; a new exam variant can still fall outside it.
### What was tested
- **Answers.** All 434 bank questions were rewritten as SI and CI relations and solved by script. 431 match the answer key (420 exactly, 11 after rounding where the option itself is rounded). 2 could not be machine-checked and 1 does not match; all three are listed below.
- **Methods.** Formula shortcuts were run by script on every question that uses them and compared with the solved values: 2-year CI minus SI (24 questions), 3-year CI minus SI (12), SI and CI both given (6), n times at SI (6), amounts at two dates (5). All 53 pass. The 1% value, effective-percent and reinvestment methods are direct arithmetic; their check is the answer check. Every value in the Section 1 tables was recomputed by script.
- **Sister-exam questions.** All 12 were solved by script. Ten match the key printed in the paper, one paper printed no key, and one disagrees (see below).
- **Drills and ladder.** All 11 drill answers were computed by script; the trap values quoted in the warnings were worked by hand. All 36 ladder answers were read from the solver output.
### What failed and was fixed
- A first plan to treat “difference between CI and SI” as a Core archetype was dropped: it has 40 bank questions but appears in none of the 30 papers, so it is labelled Bank only.
- Drill 2 first had two options divisible by 44; one option was changed so the hack leaves a single answer.
- The option reader first mistook “None of these” answers for near-matches on two constructed questions; both are exact “None of these” cases and are counted as matching.
### Stated exceptions
- **G-sp-6 (Guidely, “Simple Interest Based on Principal” Q6).** The computed sum is 8,400. The key says 8,000 and 8,400 is not among that copy’s options. The identical question in the “Months” set is keyed 8,400. Treat the key as wrong.
- **S-MT30-50 (Sreedhar).** The options are images, so the key could not be checked. The computed rate is 5 1/3%.
- **G-cic-10.** Keyed “Cannot be determined”; checked by hand (one fact, two unknowns).
- **G-roi-9.** The key needs “330 is the interest of the second year alone”. Read literally, as second-year minus first-year interest, the answer is 18,480, which is not an option.
- **G-rat-3.** The timeline is unclear; the key fits 6 months for the first part and 2 years for the second.
- **G-sch-10.** Needs equal sums in both schemes, which this copy does not state (the repeat in the Miscellaneous set does).
- **S-MT61-62.** “Rate increased by 20%” must be read as 20% of the rate (20% to 24%) to match the key.
- **G-sn-2, G-sn-6, G-sr-4.** “n times more than” and “300% more than” are used to mean (n + 1) times.
- **G-inc-8.** A profit-and-loss question filed under SI and CI; left out of the archetypes.
- **Sister question, IBPS PO Prelims 22 Aug 2026 Shift 1.** As extracted, the sum is 1,400 and the answer should be 836 (an option), but the extracted key says 792. The twin question in Shift 2 matches its key. Not resolved; the PDF text may have been misread.
- **Sister question, SBI Clerk Prelims 20 Sep 2025 Shift 2.** No key in the paper; 3,600 is computed.

# Percentages - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened on the web: 12 sister-exam papers (4 SBI Clerk Prelims, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims, 4 IBPS PO Prelims); 8 of them had a standalone percentage question. PYQs found: 18 IBPS Clerk Prelims questions from 15 paper labels (2016-2025, taken from your bank) plus 10 sister-exam questions. Bank questions used: 330 (30 constructed, 181 Guidely, 119 Sreedhar). Of these, 37 Sreedhar items are chart-based DI questions filed under Percentage and 11 are not percentage questions; both groups are left out of the archetypes. 11 are repeats. The floor of 8 target-exam shifts plus 6-10 sister questions is met. All papers are memory-based reconstructions.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Exact Mechanics & Conversion Grid
**Central law.** A percent is a fraction of some base, so always ask “percent of what?”. A change of p% multiplies the base by (100 + p)/100 or (100 - p)/100. Changes one after another multiply; they do not add. Most questions end by finding what 1% (or one fraction part) of the base is worth.
### Base operations
1. **Percent of a number.** Build it from 10% and 1% blocks. 35% of 840: 10% = 84, so 30% = 252 and 5% = 42; total 294.
2. **Whole from a part.** “45% is 378”: 1% = 378 / 45 = 8.4, so the whole is 840.
3. **Change factor.** Up 20% is x 6/5. Down 20% is x 4/5. Up 12.5% is x 9/8. Use the fraction table.
4. **Two changes in a row.** Net % = a + b + (a x b)/100, with minus signs for decreases. Up 10% then down 20%: 10 - 20 - 2 = -12%.
5. **Going backwards.** Divide by the factor. A value of 81,000 after two falls of 10% was 81,000 / 0.81 = 1,00,000.
6. **Changing the base.** If A is 1/n more than B, then B is 1/(n + 1) less than A. 25% more (1/4) turns into 20% less (1/5).
7. **“Of the remaining” chain.** Multiply the fractions that are kept. Spend 25%, then 20% of the rest, then 20% of the rest: kept = 3/4 x 4/5 x 4/5 = 48%.
8. **Equal percents.** If x% of A = y% of B, then A : B = y : x (swap the numbers).
9. **Price times quantity.** With spending fixed, a price rise of 1/n needs a cut of 1/(n + 1) in quantity; a price fall of 1/n buys 1/(n - 1) more.

<td>5/8</td>
<td>62.5%</td>
<td>7/8</td>
<td>87.5%</td>
</tr>
</table>

### Calculation facts to keep ready
- Three rises of 10% multiply to 1.331; two give 1.21. Three falls of 10% give 0.729; two give 0.81.
- Rises of 20%: 1.44 after two, 1.728 after three. A rise of 25% then a fall of 20% cancels exactly.
- Non-linear warning: a rise and a fall of the same p% do not cancel. The result is a fall of (p x p)/100 percent.
## 2. Archetypes
Fourteen archetypes cover the usable bank and the sister-exam questions. The salary-spending chain (“of the remaining”) is the clear leader: 9 of the 27 papers carry it. Elections, pass marks, Venn sets and mensuration percentages have many bank questions but none in the papers; they are Insurance. “Papers” counts paper labels.

Worked examples below are real questions from the papers.
### P3. “Of the remaining” spending chain (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “Spends 25% of his income on food. From the remaining he spends 20% on rent … and saves the rest.” A ratio split of one slice (“in the ratio 3 : 1 : 1”) is common.
**Shortcut engine: multiply what is kept.**
1. For each “of the remaining” step, write the fraction kept (spend 20%, keep 4/5).
2. Multiply the kept fractions: that is the final share of the income.
3. Final share = given rupees gives 1% of income. If a step says “of his income” instead, subtract that % directly, do not multiply.
**Worked example (SBI Clerk Prelims, 22 Feb 2025, Shift 1).** Spends 25% on food, 20% of the remaining on rent, 20% of the remaining on education, and saves 19,200. Kept = 3/4 x 4/5 x 4/5 = 48%. 48% = 19,200, so 1% = 400 and the income is 40,000.
**Difference version (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2022, Adda247 paper).** 60% on accommodation, 15% of the remaining on entertainment, rest saved. Accommodation minus savings = 3,510. Remaining 40%; savings = 85% of 40 = 34%. Gap = 60 - 34 = 26% = 3,510, so 1% = 135 and the salary is 13,500.
### P4. Successive change over years (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “Increased by 10% in the first year and decreased by 20% in the second”, “increases by 10% every year”, “two years ago”.
**Shortcut engine: multiply the factors.**
1. Two steps: net % = a + b + ab/100. Three or more: multiply the fraction factors.
2. Forward: multiply. Backward (“was … years ago”): divide.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 2).** 25,000 rises 10% then falls 20%. Net = 10 - 20 - 2 = -12%. Answer 22,000.
**Backward version (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2023, Guidely paper).** Up 10% every year; 2,66,200 in 2022. Average of 2019 and 2020? 2019 = 2,66,200 / 1.331 = 2,00,000. 2020 = 2,20,000. Average 2,10,000.
### P5. Two quantities linked by percents (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “45% of X is equal to 60% of Y”, “5% of X is 25% more than Y”, “A is 20% more than B”, followed by a sum, difference or average.
**Shortcut engine: turn the sentence into a ratio.**
1. x% of A = y% of B gives A : B = y : x. “A is p% more than B” gives A : B = (100 + p) : 100.
2. Match the given sum, difference or average to its parts and find 1 unit.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2021).** 45% of X = 60% of Y; the average of X and Y is 20 less than X. Find 60% of Y. X : Y = 60 : 45 = 4 : 3. Average = 3.5 units = 4 units - 20, so 1 unit = 40. Y = 120 and 60% of it is 72.
**“More than” version (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2016).** 5% of X is 25% more than Y; X - Y = 96. X/20 = 5Y/4, so X : Y = 25 : 1. Gap 24 units = 96, 1 unit = 4, X = 100.
### P7. Two groups, each with its own percent (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** Boys and girls, males and females, two schools or two teams. Each group has its own pass %, graduate % or growth %, and a combined figure is given or asked.
**Shortcut engine: one line per group.**
1. Write each group as a % or ratio of the total.
2. Apply that group’s own % to it.
3. Add or compare the two lines and match with the given number.
**Worked example (SBI Clerk Prelims, 22 Feb 2025, Shift 1).** 40% of a town are male. 40% of the females are graduates, and 540 females are not. Find the males. Females = 60% of the town; non-graduate females = 60% of 60 = 36% = 540. 1% = 15, so males = 40% = 600.
**Count version (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 4).** 360 passed; 60% of boys and 40% of girls passed; 66 2/3% of the passed boys is 160. Passed boys = 160 x 3/2 = 240 = 60% of boys, so boys = 400. Passed girls = 120 = 40% of girls, so girls = 300. Total 700.
### P2. Shares of one whole (Confirmed)
**Shortcut engine.** All shares are “of the total”, so they add to 100%. The unnamed share = 100% minus the rest; match it to its number.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 1).** 40% science fair, 25% sports, the rest (315) cultural. Rest = 35% = 315, so 1% = 9. Difference between science and sports = 15% = 135.
### P6. Income, expenditure and savings (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** Draw three boxes: income = expenditure + savings. Put 100 on whichever box the percent is “of”, fill the other two, then scale.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 3).** Income is 25% more than expenditure; expenditure is 24,000. Expenditure 100, income 125, savings 25. Savings = 25% of 24,000 = 6,000.
### P8. Price, consumption and expenditure (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** Price x quantity = spending. For “a price cut lets him buy k kg more for Rs S”: the money saved (cut % of S) is exactly what buys the extra k kg at the new price.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 3 Sep 2022, Slot 2).** A 16.66% cut buys 3 kg more for Rs 180. Saved = 1/6 of 180 = 30. So 3 kg cost 30 at the new price: new price 10. Original = 10 x 6/5 = 12.
### P9. Constant part (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** One ingredient does not change (fruit pulp, pure alcohol). Find its weight first; then it is a known % of the new total.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2017, Guidely model paper).** Fresh fruit is 68% water; dry fruit is 20% water. From 100 kg fresh: pulp = 32 kg. In dry fruit pulp is 80%, so dry fruit = 32 / 0.8 = 40 kg.
### P10. Fraction with numerator and denominator changed (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** New fraction = old fraction x (numerator factor / denominator factor). To get the old one, multiply the new one by the upside-down of that.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2016).** Numerator down 25% (x 3/4), denominator up 250% (x 7/2), result 6/5. Combined factor = 3/4 / 7/2 = 3/14. Original = 6/5 x 14/3 = 28/5.
### P1. One-step percent of a number (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** Write every piece as a % of the same number and combine.
**Worked example (IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims, 13 Dec 2025, Shift 1).** A number raised 40% and the same number cut 80% differ by 360. 140% - 20% = 120% = 360, so the number is 300.
### P12. Pass marks (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Two students around the pass mark: maximum marks = (marks short + marks extra) / (difference of their %). Scores 30% and fails by 30; scores 45% and passes by 15: 45 marks = 15%, so the maximum is 300.
### P11. Elections (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Write the chain: voters on the list, votes polled, valid votes, winner and loser. Each % applies to the level just above it. Winning margin = (winner % - loser %) of valid votes. Example: 20% of polled votes invalid, winner gets 58% of valid and wins by 1,920. Margin = 16% of 80% = 12.8% of polled = 1,920, so 15,000 were polled.
### P14. Percent change in area or volume (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Area uses two changes: a + b + ab/100. Each side up 20% gives 44%. Volume needs three factors: each edge up 30% gives 1.3 x 1.3 x 1.3 = 2.197, a rise of 119.7%. Non-linear warning: the area change is never just double the side change.
### P13. Two overlapping sets (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** At least one = A + B - both. Neither = 100% - at least one. Example: 35% fail English, 25% fail Maths, 10% fail both. Failed at least one = 50%, so passed both = 50%.
## 3. Drill Set
Ten original questions, one per Core archetype. Every answer and every trap value was computed by script. Try each before reading the method.
### Drill 1. Easy cutoff-buster (P2)
In a housing society, 45% of the residents own a car, 30% own only a bike and the rest own neither. If 175 residents own neither, find the difference between the number of car owners and bike-only owners.
1. 95 (B) 105 (C) 115 (D) 125 (E) 100
- **Conventional method.** 0.25 T = 175 gives T = 700. Then 0.45 T - 0.30 T = 105.
- **30-second exam method.** The rest = 100 - 45 - 30 = 25% = 175, so 1% = 7. The gap asked is 45 - 30 = 15% = 105. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The answer is 15 x (1%) and 1% = 7, so it must divide by 7 and by 15. Only 105 does. Back-check: 700 residents, 315 and 210. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Do not find the total first unless asked. Go straight from 25% to 15%.
### Drill 2. Two-step moderate (P3)
Meera spends 40% of her monthly salary on rent and 25% of the remaining on food, and saves the rest. If her savings are Rs 9,000 more than the amount spent on food, find her monthly salary (in Rs).
1. 24,000 (B) 25,000 (C) 30,000 (D) 36,000 (E) 90,000
- **Conventional method.** Food = 0.25 x 0.6 S = 0.15 S. Savings = 0.45 S. 0.30 S = 9,000.
- **30-second exam method.** Remaining after rent = 60%. Food = a quarter of 60 = 15%. Savings = 45%. Gap = 30% = 9,000, so 1% = 300 and the salary is 30,000. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** “25% of the remaining” is 15% of salary, not 25%. Taking 25% of salary gives a 10% gap and option (E) 90,000.
### Drill 3. Non-linear trap (P4)
The price of an article was increased by 25%, then decreased by 20% and then increased by 10%. If the final price is Rs 1,650, find the original price (in Rs).
1. 1,400 (B) 1,435 (C) 1,500 (D) 1,550 (E) 1,600
- **Conventional method.** P x 1.25 x 0.8 x 1.1 = 1,650.
- **30-second exam method.** Up 1/4 then down 1/5 cancel exactly (5/4 x 4/5 = 1). Only the 10% rise is left: 110% = 1,650, so the original is 1,500. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Once the first two changes cancel, 110% of the right option must equal 1,650. Only 1,500 works. Back-check: 1,500, 1,875, 1,500, 1,650. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Adding the changes (25 - 20 + 10 = 15%) gives about 1,435, option (B). Changes multiply; they never add.
### Drill 4. Phrasing trap (P5)
A’s salary is 25% more than B’s salary, and B’s salary is 40% less than C’s salary. By what percent is A’s salary less than C’s salary?
1. 15% (B) 20% (C) 25% (D) 30% (E) 35%
- **Conventional method.** A = 1.25 B and B = 0.6 C, so A = 0.75 C.
- **30-second exam method.** Put C = 100. B = 60. A = 60 + a quarter of 60 = 75. A is 25 below C’s 100: 25% less. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed; put 100 on the last person named as the base.
- **IBPS trap warning.** 40 - 25 = 15 is option (A). Each percent has a different base, so they cannot be subtracted.
### Drill 5. Two groups (P7)
In a company, 60% of the employees are male. 30% of the males and 45% of the females are graduates. If the number of non-graduate employees is 1,280, find the total number of employees.
1. 1,800 (B) 2,000 (C) 2,400 (D) 2,500 (E) 1,600
- **Conventional method.** Graduates = 0.6 x 0.3 T + 0.4 x 0.45 T = 0.36 T. Then 0.64 T = 1,280.
- **30-second exam method.** Take 100 employees: 60 males, 40 females. Graduates = 18 + 18 = 36. Non-graduates = 64% = 1,280, so 1% = 20 and the total is 2,000. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Do not average 30% and 45%. Each applies to a group of a different size.
### Drill 6. Income and savings (P6)
Ravi’s monthly income is 20% more than Sunil’s. Ravi saves 25% of his income and Sunil saves 30% of his income. If Ravi’s monthly expenditure is Rs 4,000 more than Sunil’s, find Sunil’s monthly income (in Rs).
1. 16,000 (B) 18,000 (C) 20,000 (D) 24,000 (E) 25,000
- **Conventional method.** 0.75 x 1.2 S - 0.7 S = 4,000.
- **30-second exam method.** Sunil’s income 100: he spends 70. Ravi’s income 120: he spends three-quarters = 90. Gap 20 units = 4,000, so 1 unit = 200 and Sunil earns 20,000. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (D) 24,000 is Ravi’s income. The question asks Sunil’s.
### Drill 7. Price cut (P8)
A reduction of 20% in the price of sugar enables a buyer to get 4 kg more for Rs 640. Find the original price of sugar per kg (in Rs).
1. 32 (B) 36 (C) 40 (D) 45 (E) 50
- **Conventional method.** 640 / (0.8 p) - 640 / p = 4.
- **30-second exam method.** Money saved = 20% of 640 = 128. That 128 buys the extra 4 kg at the new price: new price = 32. Original = 32 x 5/4 = 40. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The new price is 4/5 of the answer and 640 must split evenly at both prices. 640 / 40 = 16 kg and 640 / 32 = 20 kg: 4 kg more. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 32 is the reduced price. The question asks the original price.
### Drill 8. Constant part (P9)
Fresh dates contain 72% water and dry dates contain 16% water. How many kg of dry dates can be obtained from 150 kg of fresh dates?
1. 42 (B) 45 (C) 48 (D) 50 (E) 54
- **Conventional method.** Pulp = 0.28 x 150 = 42. Dry weight x 0.84 = 42.
- **30-second exam method.** Pulp never changes: 28% of 150 = 42 kg. In dry dates pulp is 84%, so dry dates = 42 / 0.84 = 50 kg. **Answer (D).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 42 is only the pulp. Dry dates still hold 16% water, so the answer is larger.
### Drill 9. Fraction (P10)
If the numerator of a fraction is increased by 40% and the denominator is decreased by 30%, the resultant fraction is 8/5. Find the original fraction.
1. 3/5 (B) 4/5 (C) 16/5 (D) 5/8 (E) 2/3
- **Conventional method.** 1.4 n / 0.7 d = 8/5, so n/d = 8/5 x 0.7/1.4.
- **30-second exam method.** Numerator x 7/5, denominator x 7/10: the fraction is multiplied by 7/5 / 7/10 = 2. The original is half of 8/5 = 4/5. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (C) 16/5 comes from multiplying by 2 again. Going back to the original means dividing.
### Drill 10. One-step percent (P1)
A number is increased by 30%, and the same number is decreased by 45%. If the difference between the two results is 450, find the number.
1. 500 (B) 540 (C) 600 (D) 750 (E) 3,000
- **Conventional method.** 1.3 x - 0.55 x = 450.
- **30-second exam method.** The two results are 130% and 55% of the number. Gap = 75% = 450, so the number is 600. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (E) 3,000 comes from 45 - 30 = 15%. One change goes up and the other down, so the gap is 30 + 45 = 75%.
## 4. Cheat Sheet & 30-Second Recall Matrix
### Which engine to use
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>What the question gives</td>
<td>Engine</td>
<td>Key step</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Several shares “of the total”, one left over</td>
<td>Shares add to 100%</td>
<td>Leftover % = its number; find 1%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“x% of the remaining” steps</td>
<td>Multiply what is kept</td>
<td>Product of kept fractions = final share</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Rise and fall over years</td>
<td>Multiply factors</td>
<td>a + b + ab/100; divide to go back</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“x% of A = y% of B”, “p% more than”</td>
<td>Convert to a ratio</td>
<td>A : B = y : x; then units</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two groups with different %</td>
<td>One line per group</td>
<td>Group share x its own %</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Income, expenditure, savings</td>
<td>Three boxes</td>
<td>Put 100 on the base, fill, scale</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Price change with fixed spending</td>
<td>Price x quantity</td>
<td>Saved money buys the extra quantity</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Fresh and dry, dilution</td>
<td>Constant part</td>
<td>Find the unchanged ingredient first</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Numerator and denominator changed</td>
<td>Combined factor</td>
<td>Divide by (numerator factor / denominator factor)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two students around the pass mark</td>
<td>Gap method</td>
<td>(short + extra) / % difference</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Election</td>
<td>Level chain</td>
<td>List, polled, valid, winner and loser</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Universal fallback
When a question fits no engine, use the **100 method**. Put 100 on the quantity every other percent ultimately hangs from (total salary, total votes, the third person). Rewrite every other quantity as a plain number out of that 100, one sentence at a time. The rupee or head-count figure given will equal some number of those units, which fixes 1 unit. If two different bases appear and cannot be linked, plug in the middle option instead and test it.
### Phrase to maths
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Phrase in the question</td>
<td>Meaning</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“25% of the remaining”</td>
<td>A quarter of what is left, not of the total</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“25% of his income” in the middle of a chain</td>
<td>A quarter of the total; subtract it directly</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A is 25% more than B”</td>
<td>A : B = 5 : 4 (base is B)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A is 25% less than B”</td>
<td>A : B = 3 : 4 (base is B)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“B is what percent less than A”</td>
<td>Base switches to A</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“200% more than”</td>
<td>3 times</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Increased by 250%”</td>
<td>Multiplied by 3.5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“x% of A is equal to y% of B”</td>
<td>A : B = y : x</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Increased by 2 percentage points”</td>
<td>7% becomes 9%, not 7.14%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“16.66%”, “33.33%”, “12.5%”, “37.5%”, “62.5%”</td>
<td>1/6, 1/3, 1/8, 3/8, 5/8</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Fails by 30 marks”</td>
<td>Score = pass mark - 30</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“20% of the votes polled were invalid”</td>
<td>Valid = 80% of polled, not of the list</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Wins by a majority of 1,728”</td>
<td>Winner - loser = 1,728</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Population two years ago”</td>
<td>Divide by the two factors</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Red flags
- **Wrong base.** Most wrong options come from applying a percent to the total when it belongs to “the remaining”, or the other way round.
- **Adding successive changes.** Up 25% then down 20% is no change, not +5%.
- **Averaging two group percents.** Weight each by the size of its group.
- **Direction words.** “More” and “less” versions of the same number often both appear as options.
- **Stopping early.** Finding the total when the question asks a difference, or one person’s income when it asks the other’s.
- **Decimals and fractions are allowed.** Answers such as 28/5, 37.5% and 12.5 appear. Use divisibility hacks only when every option is whole and none is “None of these”, and always back-check.
## 5. Practice Ladder
Thirty-six bank questions in three rungs. IDs: PYQ-PCT and CON-PCT are the `source.id` values in `clerk/questions.json`; S-MTm-q is question q of Sreedhar model test m; G-code-n is question n of a Guidely set, with the set codes listed in the Pattern Index.
**Rung 1. Untimed until all correct.**
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>ID</td>
<td>Engine</td>
<td>Answer</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>CON-PCT-05</td>

## 6. Verification Log
Built 5 Oct 2026. This module is checked, not guaranteed; a new exam variant can still fall outside it.
### What was tested
- **Answers.** All 348 bank questions (18 PYQs plus 330 practice) were read and 304 were solved by script; all 304 match the answer key (297 exactly, 7 after rounding where the option itself is rounded). No wrong key was found in this topic. The other 44 could not be machine-checked and are listed below.
- **Direction words.** The script compares numbers only. For the nine questions whose options differ only by “increase” or “decrease” (“more” or “less”), the direction was checked by hand; all agree with the key.
- **Methods.** Two formula shortcuts were run by script against the solved values: the pass-mark gap method (3 questions) and the consumption-cut rule (3 questions). All 6 pass. The other engines (kept-fraction chain, factor multiplication, ratio conversion, group lines) are direct arithmetic; their check is the answer check.
- **Sister-exam questions.** All 10 were solved by script and match the keys printed in the papers.
- **Drills and ladder.** All 10 drill answers and the quoted trap values were computed by script. All 36 ladder answers were read from the solver output.
### What failed and was fixed
- Drill 7 was first written with the reduced price as the answer; the script showed the original price is 40, so the options and key were rewritten.
- Drill 3’s elimination hack first relied on an unjustified divisibility claim; it was replaced by a direct test of the options.
- The option reader was extended to read fractions (28/5), mixed numbers (16 2/3, 27 3/11) and Indian comma grouping (2,16,832) after first misreading them.
### Stated exceptions
- **37 chart-based questions (Sreedhar model tests 49, 51, 53, 60, 61, 73, 75).** These are data-interpretation sets filed under Percentage. They need their chart images and were not solved; they belong with the DI topic.
- **11 non-percentage questions** (ticket counts, price proportion, simple averages and similar) are filed here; 10 were solved and match their keys, but they are not counted in any archetype.
- **3 questions with image options (S-MT75-31, S-MT51-56, S-MT17-34).** Keys could not be read. Computed answers: 16 2/3%, 150% and 78 4/7%.
- **4 “Cannot be determined” items (G-elec-2, G-inex-2, G-rat-10, S-MT9-54)** were checked by hand and agree with the key.
- **G-cons-5.** The key needs the consumption cut read as (x - 5)%, although the text says kg.
- **G-elec-7.** The key (4,000) needs 12.5% of A’s votes moving to B. Read literally, the answer is about 8,571. Sreedhar S-MT56-49 is a clean version of the same idea.
- **G-marks-5.** The key needs “10% failed” read as 10% of those who attended.
- **Sister paper, SBI Clerk Prelims 20 Sep 2025 Shift 2.** A follow-up question to the salary question (difference between mutual fund and rent) was left out: the computed value is 4,300 but the extracted key says 4,200.
### What could not be opened or checked
- None of the 15 IBPS Clerk source PDFs was re-opened for this topic. The 18 PYQs are used as transcribed in your bank.
- Four sister papers had no standalone percentage question: SBI Clerk 21 Sep 2025 Shift 1, IBPS RRB Clerk 6 Dec 2025 Shift 2, and both IBPS PO 22 Aug 2026 shifts. Percentages there appear only inside DI sets and simplification.
- No Mains papers were found or used. This module reflects Prelims only.
- Web PDFs were read through a text-extraction tool, so quoted wording may differ slightly from the PDF.
- Paper counts use paper labels; undated labels may be the same paper as a dated one.
- The method books and channels in your Priority 3 list were not read or watched.
### Source links (pages opened)
The same 12 sister-exam PDFs as in the Simple & Compound Interest module were opened again for this topic; their links are listed in that module’s Verification Log. The 15 IBPS Clerk paper links are stored with each question in `clerk/questions.json` (`source.url`).
