# Partnership - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened on the web: 12 sister-exam papers (4 SBI Clerk Prelims, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims, 4 IBPS PO Prelims); every one had a partnership question. PYQs found: 16 IBPS Clerk Prelims questions from 16 paper labels (2018-2025, taken from your bank) plus 12 usable sister-exam questions from 11 papers. Bank questions used: 278 (30 constructed, 211 Guidely, 37 Sreedhar; 18 are repeats and 2 are not partnership questions). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts is met; the sister count (12) is just above the 6-10 target. All papers are memory-based reconstructions.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Exact Mechanics & Conversion Grid
**Central law.** A partner’s claim on the profit is his money multiplied by the time it stayed in the business. Call this his **weight** (capital x months). Profits are shared in the ratio of the weights. So there are three linked ratios: capital, time and profit, with profit = capital x time.
### Base operations
1. **Weight.** Weight = capital x months. Cancel common factors first: 24,000 for 12 months and 30,000 for 4 months is 24 x 12 : 30 x 4 = 288 : 120 = 12 : 5.
2. **Share from total.** One part = total profit / sum of weights. Each share = his parts x one part.
3. **Total from one share.** One part = his share / his parts. Total = one part x sum of parts.
4. **Months for a late joiner or early leaver.** Joined after 4 months in a 12-month year: 8 months. Left after 4 months: 4 months. Left 4 months before the end: 8 months.
5. **Capital changed midway.** Split that partner’s time into blocks and add: 9,000 for 8 months then 12,000 for 4 months = 72 + 48 = 120 (in thousands).
6. **The triangle.** Profit ratio = capital ratio x time ratio. So time ratio = profit ratio / capital ratio, and capital ratio = profit ratio / time ratio, taken term by term.
7. **Joining three capitals.** A : B = 3 : 5 and B : C = 15 : 11. Make B the same: 9 : 15 : 11.
8. **Fractions as a ratio.** 1/2 : 1/3 : 1/4 becomes 6 : 4 : 3 after multiplying by 12.
### Conversion grid
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Situation in a 12-month year</td>
<td>Months to use</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Starts the business</td>
<td>12</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Joins after 3 months</td>
<td>9</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Joins after 4 months</td>
<td>8</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Joins after 6 months</td>
<td>6</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Leaves after 9 months</td>
<td>9</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Leaves 5 months before the end</td>
<td>7</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Doubles his capital after 4 months</td>
<td>4 months at 1 part + 8 months at 2 parts</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Withdraws half after 8 months (2-year business)</td>
<td>8 months at full + 16 months at half</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Calculation facts to keep ready
- Always cancel zeros and common factors before multiplying. Weights are only ever needed as a ratio.
- Non-linear warning: profit depends on the product of capital and time. Sharing in the capital ratio alone is wrong whenever the times differ, and going backwards means dividing the ratios, not multiplying them.
## 2. Archetypes
Seven archetypes cover the bank and the sister-exam questions. The reverse question (profit known, find a capital, a time or a ratio) dominates: 13 of the 28 papers carry it. Charity, working-partner salary and similar deductions have 21 bank questions but appear in no paper. “Papers” counts paper labels.

</tr>
<tr>
<td>T3</td>

Worked examples below are real questions from the papers.
### T3. Profit known, find capital, time or a ratio (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “The profit shares are in the ratio 2 : 1. Find X.” “B’s share is Rs 21,000 out of a total profit of Rs 49,000.” “Find the ratio of their investment periods.” “After X months B left.”
**Shortcut engine: the triangle, term by term.**
1. Turn the profit data into a ratio. If one share and the total are given, subtract to get the other share first.
2. Write the known side (capitals or months) under it.
3. Divide profit by the known side, term by term, to get the missing side as a ratio. Then match with any real number given.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2023, Adda247 paper).** A starts with 8,000; after six months B joins with X. B gets 21,000 of 49,000. Profits A : B = 28 : 21 = 4 : 3. Months 12 and 6. Capital ratio = 4/12 : 3/6 = 1/3 : 1/2 = 2 : 3. A’s 2 parts = 8,000, so X = 12,000.
**Ratio version (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 12 Dec 2021, Set 2).** Investments 3 : 2 : 1, profits 5 : 4 : 2. Times = 5/3 : 4/2 : 2/1 = 5 : 6 : 6.
**Months version (SBI Clerk Prelims, 21 Sep 2025, Shift 1).** Ravi 9,000 and Sohan 6,000; Sohan leaves after Y months; Sohan gets 1,500 of 4,500. Profits 3,000 : 1,500 = 2 : 1. Ravi’s weight = 9 x 12 = 108, so Sohan’s is 54 = 6 x Y. Y = 9.
**“X and X + something” version (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 12 Dec 2021, Set 1).** x for 24 months, x + 4,000 for 16 months; B gets 1,440 of 2,400. Profits 960 : 1,440 = 2 : 3. Capital ratio = 2/24 : 3/16 = 4 : 9. The 5-part gap is 4,000, so 1 part = 800 and x = 3,200.
### T4. Capital changed midway (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “After 3 months A withdrew Rs 600”, “doubles his investment”, “increased his investment by 50%”, “withdraws half”.
**Shortcut engine: time blocks.**
1. For each partner list his blocks: capital x months for every stretch between changes.
2. Add the blocks to get one weight per partner. Then share as usual.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 3 Sep 2022, Slot 2).** A starts with 1,800. After three months B joins with 1,200 and A withdraws 600. Total profit 500. A = 18 x 3 + 12 x 9 = 162. B = 12 x 9 = 108. Ratio 3 : 2. A gets 300.
**Step-down version (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 4).** Rohit invests twice Shikhar’s sum, halves it after 8 months and halves it again after 8 more; 2 years in all. Rohit = 2 x 8 + 1 x 8 + 0.5 x 8 = 28. Shikhar = 1 x 24 = 24. Ratio 7 : 6.
### T2. Different months, fixed capitals (Confirmed)
**Wording clues.** “After 4 months B left”, “C joined after 6 months”, “invested for 8 months and 10 months”. All capitals and times are given; a share, the total or a ratio is asked.
**Shortcut engine.** Weight = capital x months for each partner, simplify, then one part = known rupees / matching parts.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 4 Oct 2025, Shift 2).** A 24,000 and B 30,000; B leaves after 4 months; profit 3,400. Weights 24 x 12 : 30 x 4 = 12 : 5. One part = 3,400 / 17 = 200. B gets 1,000.
**Difference version (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2024, Guidely paper).** Frenz invests 1.2p for 8 months, Dolly p for 12 months; Frenz gets 600 less. Weights 9.6 : 12 = 4 : 5. One part = 600. Total = 9 parts = 5,400.
### T1. Same time for all partners (Confirmed)
**Shortcut engine.** Time cancels, so profit ratio = capital ratio.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims, 5 Oct 2025, Shift 1).** A 4,000, B 6,000, C X, one year. B gets 2,400 of 7,200, which is one-third. So B’s capital is one-third of the total: total 18,000 and X = 8,000.
### T5. Capital changed midway and an unknown to find (Seen once)
**Shortcut engine.** Build each weight with time blocks, keeping the unknown as it is. Set weight ratio = profit ratio and cross-multiply once. No pure ratio shortcut exists here because the unknown sits inside a sum of blocks, so one line with X is used.
**Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2022, Adda247 paper).** P invests X and Q invests X + 100. After three months P withdraws 40%. P gets 210 of 530. P’s weight = 3X + 9 x 0.6X = 8.4X. Q’s weight = 12(X + 100). Profits 210 : 320 = 21 : 32. One line: 32 x 8.4X = 21 x 12(X + 100), so 268.8X = 252X + 25,200 and X = 1,500.
### T6. Something taken out before sharing (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** Take the deductions off the top as a percent of the profit. Share only what is left in the weight ratio. Add back any salary to the working partner. Example: capitals 3 : 2, working partner A gets 10% of 15,000 = 1,500; the other 13,500 is split 3 : 2, so A gets 8,100 more: 9,600 in all.
### T7. Profit as a percent of investment, shared rent (Bank only)
**Shortcut engine.** If profit is p% of the total investment and times are equal, each partner’s profit is p% of his own investment. For a shared pasture, weight = animals x months and the rent is split like a profit.
## 3. Drill Set
Seven original questions: one per Core archetype, the phrasing and product traps, and one Insurance type. Every answer was computed by script. Try each before reading the method.
### Drill 1. Easy cutoff-buster (T1)
A, B and C started a business by investing Rs 4,500, Rs 6,000 and Rs 7,500 respectively for one year. If the total profit at the end of the year is Rs 9,600, find the difference between the profit shares of C and A (in Rs).
1. 1,200 (B) 1,600 (C) 2,000 (D) 2,400 (E) 800
- **Conventional method.** Shares = 9,600 x 4,500 / 18,000 and 9,600 x 7,500 / 18,000; subtract.
- **30-second exam method.** Capitals 45 : 60 : 75 = 3 : 4 : 5, twelve parts. One part = 9,600 / 12 = 800. C minus A = 2 parts = 1,600. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The answer is 2 parts and one part is 9,600 / 12, so it must be 9,600 / 6. Only 1,600 fits.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Do not compute both shares. Go straight to the difference in parts. Option (E) 800 is one part.
### Drill 2. Two-step moderate (T2)
A started a business with Rs 12,000. After 4 months B joined with Rs 15,000, and after 2 more months C joined with Rs 18,000. If the total profit at the end of the year is Rs 18,600, find C’s share of the profit (in Rs).
1. 4,800 (B) 5,400 (C) 6,000 (D) 7,440 (E) 6,600
- **Conventional method.** Weights 12,000 x 12, 15,000 x 8 and 18,000 x 6; C’s share = 18,600 x 108,000 / 372,000.
- **30-second exam method.** Months are 12, 8 and 6. Weights 12 x 12 : 15 x 8 : 18 x 6 = 144 : 120 : 108 = 12 : 10 : 9, total 31. One part = 18,600 / 31 = 600. C = 9 parts = 5,400. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** C has 9 parts, so the answer must divide by 9. Only 5,400 does. Back-check: 7,200 + 6,000 + 5,400 = 18,600. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** “After 2 more months” means 6 months from the start, so C stays 6 months. Sharing by capital alone (4 : 5 : 6) gives option (D) 7,440.
### Drill 3. Product trap (T3)
The investments of A and B in a business are in the ratio 5 : 6 and their profits are in the ratio 3 : 4. If B invested for 10 months, for how many months did A invest?
1. 7.5 (B) 8 (C) 9 (D) 12 (E) 15
- **Conventional method.** (5 x a) / (6 x 10) = 3/4, so a = 9.
- **30-second exam method.** Time = profit / capital, term by term: 3/5 : 4/6 = 18 : 20 = 9 : 10. B’s 10 parts are 10 months, so A invested 9 months. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Test 9: A’s weight 5 x 9 = 45, B’s 6 x 10 = 60, and 45 : 60 = 3 : 4. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Profit is a product (capital x time). To get time you must divide the ratios. Multiplying them (3 x 5 : 4 x 6) is the usual mistake.
### Drill 4. Phrasing trap (T2)
P and Q started a business with Rs 16,000 and Rs 20,000 respectively. Q left the business 4 months before the end of the year. If the total profit for the year is Rs 17,600, find Q’s share (in Rs).
1. 6,400 (B) 8,000 (C) 8,800 (D) 9,600 (E) 7,200
- **Conventional method.** Q’s share = 17,600 x (20,000 x 8) / (16,000 x 12 + 20,000 x 8).
- **30-second exam method.** Q stayed 12 - 4 = 8 months. Weights 16 x 12 : 20 x 8 = 192 : 160 = 6 : 5. One part = 17,600 / 11 = 1,600. Q = 5 parts = 8,000. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Q has 5 parts of 11, so the answer is 17,600 x 5 / 11. Only 8,000 fits.
- **IBPS trap warning.** “Left 4 months before the end” is 8 months of investment. “Left after 4 months” would be 4 months. Using 4 here gives a share that is not a whole number, which is a signal to re-read.
### Drill 5. Capital changed midway (T4)
A and B started a business with Rs 9,000 and Rs 12,000 respectively. After 8 months A invested Rs 3,000 more and B withdrew Rs 3,000. If the total profit at the end of the year is Rs 25,200, find A’s share (in Rs).
1. 10,800 (B) 12,000 (C) 12,600 (D) 13,200 (E) 11,400
- **Conventional method.** A = 9,000 x 8 + 12,000 x 4; B = 12,000 x 8 + 9,000 x 4; share in that ratio.
- **30-second exam method.** In thousands: A = 9 x 8 + 12 x 4 = 120. B = 12 x 8 + 9 x 4 = 132. Ratio 10 : 11. One part = 25,200 / 21 = 1,200. A = 12,000. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** A has 10 parts of 21, so the answer is 25,200 x 10 / 21. Back-check: 12,000 + 13,200 = 25,200. Correct.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (D) 13,200 is B’s share. Option (C) 12,600 is an equal split.
### Drill 6. Changed capital with an unknown (T5)
A started a business with Rs X and B with Rs (X + 2,000). After 6 months A doubled his investment. If A’s share is Rs 5,400 out of a total profit of Rs 9,600 at the end of the year, find X.
1. 8,000 (B) 10,000 (C) 12,000 (D) 14,000 (E) 16,000
- **Conventional method.** (6X + 12X) / (12X + 24,000) = 5,400 / 4,200. Solve.
- **30-second exam method.** A’s weight = 6X + 6 x 2X = 18X. B’s weight = 12(X + 2,000). Profits 5,400 : 4,200 = 9 : 7. One line: 7 x 18X = 9 x 12(X + 2,000), so 126X = 108X + 2,16,000 and X = 12,000. **Answer (C).** One line with X is used because the unknown sits inside a sum of blocks.
- **Option elimination hack.** Plug in the middle option: X = 12,000 gives weights 216 and 168 (thousands), which is 9 : 7. Correct at the first try.
- **IBPS trap warning.** B’s share is not given; subtract first (9,600 - 5,400 = 4,200). Using 5,400 : 9,600 as the ratio is wrong.
### Drill 7. Deductions first (T6, Insurance)
A and B invested in a business in the ratio 5 : 3. 10% of the total profit goes to charity. A, being the working partner, gets 20% of the remaining profit, and the rest is shared in the ratio of their investments. If A receives Rs 6,300 in all, find the total profit (in Rs).
1. 8,400 (B) 9,000 (C) 10,000 (D) 10,500 (E) 12,000
- **Conventional method.** 0.2 x 0.9P + (5/8) x 0.8 x 0.9P = 6,300.
- **30-second exam method.** Take profit 100. Charity 10, leaving 90. Salary = 20% of 90 = 18, leaving 72. A’s 5/8 of 72 = 45. A’s total = 63. So 63% = 6,300 and the profit is 10,000. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed.
- **IBPS trap warning.** The salary is 20% of the remaining 90, not of the full 100. Taking it from 100 gives a total that is not a round option.
## 4. Cheat Sheet & 30-Second Recall Matrix
### Which engine to use
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>What the question gives</td>
<td>Engine</td>
<td>Key step</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>All capitals, same time</td>
<td>Capital ratio</td>
<td>Profit ratio = capital ratio</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Capitals and months all known</td>
<td>Weights</td>
<td>Capital x months, simplify, one part</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Profit ratio or shares known, one side missing</td>
<td>Triangle</td>
<td>Missing ratio = profit / known ratio, term by term</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Someone adds, withdraws or doubles</td>
<td>Time blocks</td>
<td>Add capital x months for each stretch</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Changed capital and an unknown X</td>
<td>Blocks + one line</td>
<td>Weight ratio = profit ratio, cross-multiply</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Charity, salary, equal split</td>
<td>Off the top</td>
<td>Deduct as % of profit, share the rest</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Universal fallback
When a question fits no engine, build the **weight table**. One row per partner; one column per stretch of time between changes. Fill each cell with capital x months (keep any unknown as it is), add across each row, and write the row totals as a ratio. That ratio equals the profit ratio, whatever the story. Compare it with the profit data given and solve the one balance that results.
### Phrase to maths
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Phrase in the question</td>
<td>Meaning</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“After 4 months B joined”</td>
<td>B’s months = total months - 4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“After 2 more months C joined”</td>
<td>Count from the start: add the earlier gap</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“B left after 4 months”</td>
<td>B’s months = 4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Left 4 months before the end”</td>
<td>Months = total - 4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Doubled his investment after 4 months”</td>
<td>4 months at 1 part, the rest at 2 parts</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Withdrew 40% of his investment”</td>
<td>Remaining capital = 60%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“B’s share is Rs 21,000 out of Rs 49,000”</td>
<td>Ratio A : B = 28 : 21 (subtract first)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Profit of A is 1/6 less than B’s”</td>
<td>A : B = 5 : 6</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A’s profit is 20% more than B’s”</td>
<td>A : B = 6 : 5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Invested in the ratio 1/2 : 1/3 : 1/4”</td>
<td>6 : 4 : 3</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A : B = 3 : 5 and B : C = 15 : 11”</td>
<td>9 : 15 : 11</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Sleeping partner”, “working partner gets 10%”</td>
<td>Take the salary out first</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Red flags
- **Capital-only split.** If any partner joined late or left early, the capital ratio is not the profit ratio.
- **Counting months.** “After 4 months” is 8 months of investment in a year; “for 4 months” is 4.
- **Business longer than a year.** Check for “at the end of 2 years” or “20 months” before using 12.
- **Share against total.** A share “out of” a total is not the ratio of the two partners. Subtract first.
- **Order of a ratio.** “Ratio of Kavin to Sandy” after naming Sandy first reverses the order.
- **Whose share.** The other partner’s share is always among the options.

## 6. Verification Log
Built 5 Oct 2026. This module is checked, not guaranteed; a new exam variant can still fall outside it.
### What was tested
- **Answers.** All 294 bank questions (16 PYQs plus 278 practice) were rewritten as capital x time weights and solved by script. 289 match the answer key (283 exactly, 6 after rounding where the option itself is rounded). 4 were checked by hand and 1 does not match; both groups are listed below.
- **Methods.** Every engine in this topic is the weight rule (capital x months) used forwards or backwards, so no separate formula test exists; the method check is the answer check on all 289 questions.
- **Sister-exam questions.** 12 were solved by script and all match the keys printed in the papers. A 13th was unusable (see below).
- **Drills and ladder.** All 7 drill answers were computed by script. All 36 ladder answers were read from the solver output.
### What failed and was fixed
- My first relation for PYQ-PTN-08 compared P’s share with the total instead of with Q’s share and gave no solution; it was corrected and now returns 1,500, matching the key.
- The option reader was extended to read number words (“Three”) after failing on one Sreedhar question.
- Three drills first had profits that did not divide evenly; the totals were changed so every share is a whole number.
### Stated exceptions
- **S-MT73-59 (Sreedhar).** The question asks Y’s investment period as a percent of X’s, which is about 70%. The key (143%) is the reverse, X’s as a percent of Y’s. Treat the key as answering the reversed question.
- **G-comp-1.** Keyed “None of these”; the data cannot fix an answer, checked by hand.
- **G-irt-6 and G-simp-8.** Keyed “Cannot be determined”; checked by hand and agree.
- **G-misc-10.** A fill-in-the-blanks item; checked by hand, only pair III fits, as keyed.
- **G-char-2.** The key needs charity (15%) and tax (10%) both taken from the whole profit.
- **G-comp-6.** The key needs the added amounts to be in the same units as the starting ratio.
- **G-irt-2 and G-ps-7** are not partnership questions (simple interest and plain shares); they match their keys but are left out of the archetypes.
- **Sister paper, IBPS PO Prelims 24 Aug 2025 Shift 1.** Its partnership question lost a number in extraction (“profit obtained by Neeraj is less than …”) and was not used.
### What could not be opened or checked
- None of the 16 IBPS Clerk source PDFs was re-opened for this topic. The 16 PYQs are used as transcribed in your bank.
- No Mains papers were found or used. This module reflects Prelims only.
- Web PDFs were read through a text-extraction tool, so quoted wording may differ slightly from the PDF.
- Paper counts use paper labels; undated labels may be the same paper as a dated one.
- The method books and channels in your Priority 3 list were not read or watched.

# Time & Work - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Pipes & Cisterns is folded into this module because it is the same maths. Papers opened: 21 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers/shifts (2016 to Oct 2025, from your bank) giving 25 PYQs (16 work + 9 pipes), plus 12 sister papers opened on the web (4 SBI Clerk, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk, 4 IBPS PO Prelims) of which 10 carried a question, giving 12 sister questions. Bank questions used: 495 (25 PYQ + 470 practice). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts and 6 to 10 sister questions is met. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, not official papers.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Mechanics and Conversion Grid
**The one idea: turn the job into chocolates.** Never work with fractions like 1/12 + 1/18. Make the total work a number of units (the LCM of the given days), then everybody eats a whole number of units per day.
**The LCM-unit algorithm (use it on every question):**
1. Total work = LCM of all the “days” (or hours) given.
2. Each person’s daily units = Total ÷ their days. A group’s daily units = Total ÷ the group’s days.
3. Add units for people working together. Subtract for an outlet pipe or a leak.
4. Time = Work left ÷ units per day of whoever is working.
Example: A 10 days, B 15 days. Total = 30 units. A = 3 a day, B = 2 a day. Together 5 a day, so 6 days.
**Mechanics you must know cold:**
- **Efficiency and time are upside-down partners.** Efficiency 3 : 2 means time 2 : 3. Flip the ratio, never the percentage.
- **Men-days.** Men × Days (× Hours) = Work. Same work on both sides, so 12 men × 15 days = 20 men × 9 days.
- **“6 men or 9 women”** means 6 men = 9 women, so 2 men = 3 women. Convert everybody into one type, then use men-days.
- **Pipes.** Filling pipe = plus units. Emptying pipe or leak = minus units.
- **Part of the work.** “60% of the work” means 60% of the total units, so 60% of the full time for that person.
- **Wages** are shared in the ratio of work done (units per day × days worked), not in the ratio of days.
**Conversion grid: “more efficient” to “less time”**
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Efficiency statement</td>
<td>Efficiency ratio (fast : slow)</td>
<td>Time ratio (fast : slow)</td>
<td>Fast one saves</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>20% more efficient</td>
<td>6 : 5</td>
<td>5 : 6</td>
<td>1/6 of the time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>25% more efficient</td>
<td>5 : 4</td>
<td>4 : 5</td>
<td>1/5 of the time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>33⅓% more efficient</td>
<td>4 : 3</td>
<td>3 : 4</td>
<td>1/4 of the time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>50% more efficient</td>
<td>3 : 2</td>
<td>2 : 3</td>
<td>1/3 of the time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>100% more (twice as good)</td>
<td>2 : 1</td>
<td>1 : 2</td>
<td>1/2 of the time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2.5 times as good</td>
<td>5 : 2</td>
<td>2 : 5</td>
<td>3/5 of the time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>37.5% less efficient</td>
<td>5 : 8 (slow : fast)</td>
<td>8 : 5</td>
<td>n/a</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>40% less efficient</td>
<td>3 : 5 (slow : fast)</td>
<td>5 : 3</td>
<td>n/a</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Non-linear warning.** “25% more efficient” is not “25% less time”; it is 20% less time. And days never add: 10 days and 15 days together is not 25 days and not 12.5 days. Only units per day add.
**Universal fallback (works on every question in this topic):** LCM units, then write one line: *units done by each phase add up to the total*. If you are stuck, do that and nothing else.

### Core archetypes (Confirmed and Seen once)
**W1. Combine known rates. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* every person’s or pipe’s own time is given; asks the time together. *Shortcut engine:* LCM units, add fillers, subtract emptiers, divide. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* A fills in 10 h, B in 15 h, C empties in 20 h. Tank = 60 units. A +6, B +4, C −3. Net 7 an hour. Time = 60/7 hours.
**W2. Group minus sub-group. Confirmed (the single most repeated Clerk pattern here).** *Pattern clues:* “A, B and C together… B and C together… find A”, or “pipe takes 12 h, with a leak 18 h”. *Shortcut engine:* LCM units for the two group times. Big group’s units minus small group’s units = the missing one’s units. A leak is just the missing “minus” pipe. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* All three 12 days, B and C 20 days. Work = 60. All = 5 a day, B+C = 3 a day, so A = 2 a day. A alone = 30 days. *Leak example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2023):* Pipe 12 h, with leak 18 h. Tank = 36. Pipe +3, pipe with leak +2, so leak = −1. Leak empties in 36 hours.
**W3. Efficiency as ratio, percent or “times”. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “50% more efficient”, “1.5 times as efficient”, “time ratio 4 : 6 : 3”. *Shortcut engine:* write the efficiency ratio as whole numbers (use the grid). Those numbers are the units per day. Total work = (sum of units of whoever worked) × days. Then divide by whoever is asked. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Dec 2020):* Times are 4 : 6 : 3, so units per day are 3 : 2 : 4 (divide 12 by each). Together 9 a day for 8 days = 72 units. A alone = 72 ÷ 3 = 24 days. Three-fourths of the work = 18 days.
**W4. Phased work. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “worked for 5 days then left”, “B joins after 8 days”, “pipe A is closed after 2 hours”. *Shortcut engine:* LCM units. Units done in phase 1 = rate × days. Units left ÷ new rate = phase 2 time. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2024):* A 15 days, B 18 days. Work = 90. A = 6, B = 5. A works 5 days = 30 units. Left 60. B needs 60 ÷ 5 = 12 days.
**W5. Men-days, chain rule, men and women. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* numbers of men, women, days, hours; “some more join”; “6 men or 9 women”. *Shortcut engine:* Work = Men × Days. Find the work, subtract what is done, divide the rest. For men and women, convert all to one type first. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2018):* 20 women, 12 days = 240 woman-days. First 3 days use 60. Left 180 to be done in 6 days needs 30 women. So 10 joined.
**W6. Days-difference puzzle. Seen once.** *Pattern clues:* nobody’s time is given outright; “B takes 6 days more than twice the time taken together”. *Shortcut engine:* this is the one place where the honest route is a quadratic, which is too slow. So **test the options**: together time of two people = product ÷ sum of their days. Start with the middle option. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2018):* A takes 20 days. B = 6 more than twice the together time. Try B = 30: together = 20 × 30 ÷ 50 = 12. Twice 12 plus 6 = 30. Fits. Answer 30 days.
**W7. Alternate days. Seen once (IBPS PO Prelims 22 Aug 2026 Shift 2).** *Pattern clues:* “work on alternate days starting with A”. *Shortcut engine:* units in one 2-day cycle = A + B. Take as many full cycles as fit, then finish the leftover day by day in the starting order. *Worked example (that PO paper):* A and B together 15 days, A alone 40 days. Work = 120. Pair = 8 a day, A = 3, so B = 5. One 2-day cycle = 8 units. 15 cycles = 120. Time = 30 days.
**W9. Leaves some days before completion. Seen once (IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims 6 Dec 2025 Shift 2).** *Pattern clues:* “B left 6 days before the work was finished”. *Shortcut engine:* work backwards. The last stretch is done by the one who stayed: take those units off first. The rest was done by both together. Add the two times. *Worked example (that RRB paper):* A and B together 15 days, B alone 36 days, B leaves 6 days before the end. Work = 180. Pair = 12, B = 5, so A = 7. Last 6 days A alone = 42. Rest 138 ÷ 12 = 11.5 days. Total 17.5 days.
### Insurance archetypes (Bank only)
**W8. Wages. Bank only.** *Shortcut engine:* share money in the ratio of units done. If all work the same days, that is just the efficiency ratio. *Worked example (bank CON-TNW-08):* A 10 days, B 15 days, paid ₹3,000. Units per day 3 : 2. A gets 3/5 of 3,000 = ₹1,800.
**W10. Tank capacity in litres. Bank only.** *Shortcut engine:* the litres given match a fraction of the tank. Find that fraction, then scale up. *Worked example (bank G-cap-2):* 3/5 full, add 28 litres, becomes 5/6 full. 5/6 − 3/5 = 7/30 of the tank = 28 litres. So 1/30 = 4 litres and the tank = 120 litres.
Note: one Clerk PYQ (2016, pipes 16 h and 12 h, tank 240 litres) is a W1 question with a litres step at the end, so the litres idea has appeared, but only as a finishing step.
## 3. Drill Set
All eight questions are original, written for this module, and each answer was checked by script. One drill per Core archetype.
### Drill 1 (Easy, W1)
Pipes A and B fill a tank in 12 hours and 18 hours. Pipe C empties the full tank in 36 hours. All three are opened together. In how many hours is the tank full?
1. 8 (B) 9 (C) 10 (D) 12 (E) 6
**Conventional Method:** 1/12 + 1/18 − 1/36 = 3/36 + 2/36 − 1/36 = 4/36 = 1/9. Time = 9 hours.
**30-Second Exam Method:** Tank = 36 units. A +3, B +2, C −1. Net 4 an hour. 36 ÷ 4 = 9. **Answer (B).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** all three rates are whole units, so the net rate is whole and the answer must divide 36. That removes 8 and 10. Six hours would need 6 units an hour, more than both fillers give (5), so 6 goes. Twelve hours is A alone, but B adds more than C removes, so it must be faster than 12. Only 9 is left. Back-check: 9 × 4 = 36.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** adding the emptying pipe instead of subtracting gives 6, which is option (E).
### Drill 2 (Phrasing trap, W2)
A, B and C together finish a work in 8 days. B and C together finish it in 12 days. In how many days can A alone finish 75% of the work?
1. 18 (B) 24 (C) 16 (D) 20 (E) 12
**Conventional Method:** A’s one-day work = 1/8 − 1/12 = 1/24. A alone takes 24 days; 75% of the work takes 18 days.
**30-Second Exam Method:** Work = 24 units. All three 3 a day, B and C 2 a day, so A does 1 a day. 75% of 24 = 18 units, so 18 days. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** no safe one here; the direct route is already two steps. Back-check: 18 days × 1 unit = 18 = three-quarters of 24.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** 24 is sitting in the options for people who stop at “A alone”. The Clerk papers of 24 Aug 2024 and 2025 both asked for 60% of the work, not the whole work. Read the last line.
### Drill 3 (Product / non-linear trap, W3)
A is 25% more efficient than B. A takes 6 days less than B to finish a work alone. In how many days can B alone finish it?
1. 30 (B) 24 (C) 32 (D) 36 (E) 28
**Conventional Method:** let B’s days be b; A’s days are b ÷ 1.25 = 0.8b; b − 0.8b = 6, so b = 30.
**30-Second Exam Method:** efficiency A : B = 5 : 4, so time A : B = 4 : 5. The gap of 1 part = 6 days. B = 5 parts = 30 days. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** B’s time is 5 parts, so it must be a multiple of 5. Only 30 qualifies. Back-check: A = 24, and 30 − 24 = 6.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** “25% more efficient” does not mean “25% less time”. Taking 6 days as 25% of B’s time gives 24, which is option (B) (and is actually A’s time).
### Drill 4 (Two-step, W4)
A can finish a work in 20 days and B in 30 days. They work together for 6 days, then A leaves. In how many more days does B finish the remaining work?
1. 15 (B) 12 (C) 10 (D) 18 (E) 21
**Conventional Method:** work done in 6 days = 6 × (1/20 + 1/30) = 6 × 1/12 = 1/2. B does the other half in 30 × 1/2 = 15 days.
**30-Second Exam Method:** Work = 60 units. A 3, B 2, together 5. Six days = 30 units. Left 30 ÷ 2 = 15 days. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** B alone does 2 units a day and 30 units are left, so no trick is needed. Back-check: 6 × 5 + 15 × 2 = 60.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** “how many more days” is 15; “in how many days in total” would be 21. Both are in the options. The Clerk paper of 4 Oct 2025 Shift 4 asked for the total, the Shift 3 pipes question asked for the additional time.
### Drill 5 (Phrasing trap, W5)
6 men or 9 women can finish a work in 12 days. In how many days can 4 men and 12 women finish it?
1. 6 (B) 8 (C) 9 (D) 4 (E) 7
**Conventional Method:** 6 men = 9 women, so 1 man = 1.5 women. 4 men + 12 women = 6 + 12 = 18 women. 9 women take 12 days, so 18 women take 6 days.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 2 men = 3 women, so 4 men = 6 women. Team = 18 women, which is double 9 women. Double the people, half the days: 6. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** the work is 9 × 12 = 108 woman-days, and the team is a whole number of women, so the answer must divide 108. That removes 8 and 7. Back-check: 18 × 6 = 108.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** “6 men **or** 9 women” is an equation between men and women. Reading it as “6 men **and** 9 women” makes the question unsolvable or wrong.
### Drill 6 (W6)
A and B together finish a work in 6 days. Working alone, A takes 5 days less than B. In how many days can B alone finish the work?
1. 15 (B) 10 (C) 12 (D) 18 (E) 20
**Conventional Method:** 1/(b − 5) + 1/b = 1/6 leads to a quadratic, b² − 17b + 30 = 0, so b = 15. Too slow for the exam.
**30-Second Exam Method:** test options with product ÷ sum. B = 15 means A = 10. 15 × 10 ÷ 25 = 6. Fits. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** the slower worker always takes more than twice the together time, so B is more than 12. That removes 10 and 12 before you test anything. Back-check: 10 and 15 give 150 ÷ 25 = 6.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** 10 is A’s time and it is in the options. Check whose time is asked.
### Drill 7 (W7)
A can finish a work in 12 days and B in 18 days. They work on alternate days, starting with A. In how many days is the work finished?
1. 14⅓ (B) 14½ (C) 14.4 (D) 15 (E) 14
**Conventional Method:** two days’ work = 1/12 + 1/18 = 5/36. After 14 days, 35/36 is done. The 15th day is A’s: 1/36 left ÷ 1/12 = 1/3 day. Total 14⅓ days.
**30-Second Exam Method:** Work = 36. A 3, B 2, cycle of 2 days = 5 units. Seven cycles (14 days) = 35. One unit left, A’s turn, A does 3 a day, so one-third of a day. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** options are not whole numbers, so do not try one. Do it directly.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** doubling the together time (7.2 × 2 = 14.4) is option (C) and it is wrong, because the last bit is done at A’s speed, not at the average speed. Also check whose turn the leftover falls on.
### Drill 8 (W9)
A can finish a work in 20 days and B in 30 days. They start together, but A leaves 5 days before the work is finished. In how many days in total is the work finished?
1. 15 (B) 10 (C) 12 (D) 17 (E) 14
**Conventional Method:** let the total be T days. B works T days, A works T − 5: T/30 + (T − 5)/20 = 1, so T = 15.
**30-Second Exam Method:** Work = 60. A 3, B 2. Last 5 days B alone = 10 units. The other 50 units at 5 a day = 10 days. Total 10 + 5 = 15. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** together all the way would take 12 days; A leaving early can only make it longer. That removes 10 and 12. Back-check: 10 × 5 + 5 × 2 = 60.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** “leaves 5 days before completion” is not “leaves after 5 days”. And 10 (the together part only) is in the options for those who forget to add the last 5 days.
## 4. Cheat Sheet and 30-Second Recall Matrix
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>If you see…</td>
<td>Archetype</td>
<td>Do this</td>
<td>Watch for</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Everyone’s own time given, “together?”</td>
<td>W1</td>
<td>LCM units, add fillers, subtract emptiers, divide</td>
<td>Adding the outlet</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“A+B+C in…, B+C in…, find A” or a leak</td>
<td>W2</td>
<td>Big group units minus small group units</td>
<td>“60% of the work” in the last line</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“% more efficient”, “times as good”, time ratio</td>
<td>W3</td>
<td>Efficiency ratio = units per day; flip for time</td>
<td>% more efficient is not % less time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“left after”, “joined after”, “closed after”</td>
<td>W4</td>
<td>Phase 1 units, then left ÷ new rate</td>
<td>Remaining time or total time?</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Men, women, days, hours</td>
<td>W5</td>
<td>Men × Days = Work; convert to one type</td>
<td>“or” means equals</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“takes d days more than…” with no times given</td>
<td>W6</td>
<td>Test options with product ÷ sum</td>
<td>Whose time is asked</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“alternate days starting with A”</td>
<td>W7</td>
<td>Units per 2-day cycle, full cycles, then leftover in order</td>
<td>Do not double the together time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“left d days before completion”</td>
<td>W9</td>
<td>Take off the lonely last stretch first, then joint rate</td>
<td>Add the last stretch back</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Rupees to be shared</td>
<td>W8</td>
<td>Ratio of units done</td>
<td>Not the ratio of days</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Litres and fractions of a tank</td>
<td>W10</td>
<td>Fraction gap = litres given, scale up</td>
<td>Capacity or water still needed?</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Five facts to carry in:**
1. Together time of two = product ÷ sum of their days.
2. Efficiency ratio flipped = time ratio.
3. The slower one alone always takes more than twice the together time; the faster one always takes less than twice.
4. Double the workers, half the days.
5. If stuck: LCM units and add up the phases to the total.

- 457 were machine-checked and all match the keyed answer. Three of those match only after rounding (PYQ-TNW-01, G-cap-1, G-wpct-3).

- 28 Sreedhar questions have their options as images, so their keys could not be checked. They were still classified.

**Wording problems found in the bank (answers still reachable)**
- G-leak-6 and G-npipe-10 say “hours” where the numbers only work as minutes.
- G-npipe-3: working gives 90 hours; the keyed option reads “90 minutes”.
- G-pvar-1 depends on how the timing sentence is read.
- The Guidely “before completion” set has stems with a stray prefix; the questions themselves are fine.
**Repeats:** 33 bank questions are exact repeats of another bank question (26 found automatically, 7 by hand). They are counted in the totals above.
**Exceptions to the methods:** W6 is the only archetype where the no-algebra route is option testing rather than a direct computation. If a W6 question came with “None of these” as the true answer, option testing would take longer.
**Not verified:** how often each archetype appears beyond this sample; the three sister questions with no visible key; the 28 image-option questions. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, so exact numbers in the real paper may have differed.
**Corrections:** none so far.

# Speed, Time & Distance - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Trains is folded into this module because it is the same maths with one extra idea (the train’s own length). Boat & Stream is not included; it is its own topic. Papers opened: 17 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers/shifts (2017 to Oct 2025, from your bank) giving 20 PYQs (7 speed-time-distance + 13 trains), plus 12 sister papers opened on the web (4 SBI Clerk, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk, 4 IBPS PO Prelims), all 12 of which carried a question, giving 17 sister questions. Bank questions used: 394 (20 PYQ + 374 practice). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts and 6 to 10 sister questions is met. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, not official papers.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Mechanics and Conversion Grid
**The one idea: Distance = Speed × Time, and one of the three is always fixed.** Find which one is fixed and the other two become a simple ratio.
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>What stays the same</td>
<td>What happens</td>
<td>Example</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Distance</td>
<td>Speed and time are upside-down partners</td>
<td>Speeds 3 : 4 means times 4 : 3</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Time</td>
<td>Distance follows speed directly</td>
<td>Speeds 3 : 4 means distances 3 : 4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Speed</td>
<td>Distance follows time directly</td>
<td>Twice the time, twice the distance</td>
</tr>
</table>
**The ratio-parts algorithm (use it on most questions):**
1. Write the speed ratio in small whole numbers.
2. Same distance? Flip it to get the time ratio.
3. The question gives a gap (“30 minutes more”, “15 minutes late and early”). That gap = the difference in parts.
4. Find one part, then the part asked.
**Mechanics you must know cold:**
- **Units.** km/h to m/s: multiply by 5/18. m/s to km/h: multiply by 18/5. Minutes to hours: divide by 60.
- **Two bodies.** Towards each other: add the speeds. Same direction: subtract the speeds. Time = gap ÷ that combined speed.
- **Train passing something.** Distance = train’s own length + length of the thing passed. A pole or a man has zero length. A platform, bridge or other train has length.
- **Train passing a moving man or another train.** Use add or subtract speeds as above, with the distance from the line before.
- **Average speed** = total distance ÷ total time. Never the plain average of the speeds, unless the times are equal.
- **Head start.** First move the early starter alone, then treat what is left as a fresh two-body question.
**Conversion grid: km/h to m/s (worth memorising)**
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>km/h</td>
<td>18</td>
<td>36</td>
<td>45</td>
<td>54</td>
<td>63</td>
<td>72</td>
<td>81</td>
<td>90</td>
<td>108</td>
<td>126</td>
<td>144</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>m/s</td>
<td>5</td>
<td>10</td>
<td>12.5</td>
<td>15</td>
<td>17.5</td>
<td>20</td>
<td>22.5</td>
<td>25</td>
<td>30</td>
<td>35</td>
<td>40</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Speed change to time change (same distance)**
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Speed becomes</td>
<td>Time becomes</td>
<td>Time change</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>5/4 (25% more)</td>
<td>4/5</td>
<td>1/5 less</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>4/3 (33⅓% more)</td>
<td>3/4</td>
<td>1/4 less</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3/2 (50% more)</td>
<td>2/3</td>
<td>1/3 less</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2 times</td>
<td>1/2</td>
<td>1/2 less</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>4/5 (20% less)</td>
<td>5/4</td>
<td>1/4 more</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3/4 (25% less)</td>
<td>4/3</td>
<td>1/3 more</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Non-linear warning.** Going at 40 and returning at 60 does not average 50; it averages 48. And 25% more speed does not save 25% of the time; it saves 20%.
**Universal fallback (works on every question in this topic):** convert everything to the same units, then write Distance = Speed × Time for each leg or each body and line them up. If stuck, do that and nothing else.

Seven bank questions fit no archetype (races, circular track, right-angle directions, a sum of hourly distances) and none of those ideas appeared in any paper opened. They are left out.
What stands out: 13 of the 20 Clerk PYQs and 9 of the 17 sister questions are train questions. Trains is where this topic’s marks are.
### Core archetypes (Confirmed and Seen once)
**T7. One train passes a pole, platform or bridge. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “crosses a pole in…, crosses a platform in…”. *Shortcut engine:* pole time covers the train’s length. Platform time covers train + platform. So the **extra seconds cover exactly the platform**. Speed = platform length ÷ extra seconds. Train length = speed × pole time. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 2):* bridge 240 m in 28 s, pole in 16 s. Extra 12 s covers 240 m, so speed 20 m/s = 72 km/h. Length = 20 × 16 = 320 m.
**T8. Two trains cross each other. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* two trains, “opposite direction” or “same direction” or “overtakes”. *Shortcut engine:* distance = sum of both lengths. Speed = sum of speeds (opposite) or difference (same direction). Work in m/s. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Dec 2020):* lengths add to 600 m, crossed in 20 s in opposite directions, so combined speed 30 m/s = 108 km/h. A does 36, so B does 72 km/h.
**T6. Ratio of speeds, times or distances. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “speeds in the ratio 3 : 4”, “A takes 30 minutes more than B”. *Shortcut engine:* the ratio-parts algorithm from section 1. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2017 model paper):* speeds A : B = 3 : 4, same distance, so times 4 : 3. One part = 30 minutes. A = 4 parts = 2 hours.
**T2. Average speed and journeys in parts. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “goes at… returns at…”, “first part by bus, rest by car”. *Shortcut engine:* for a go-and-return, pick the distance as the LCM of the two speeds, find each time, then total distance ÷ total time. For parts, find each leg’s time and add. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* 60 km/h out, 90 km/h back. Take 180 km each way: 3 h + 2 h = 5 h for 360 km. Average 72 km/h.
**T5. Two bodies meeting or chasing. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* two towns, two start times, “when do they meet”, “how far from A”. *Shortcut engine:* if one starts early, move it alone first. Then time to meet = remaining gap ÷ sum of speeds (or ÷ difference if chasing). *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2017 model paper):* 330 km apart. First train leaves at 7 a.m. at 60 km/h; second leaves the other end at 8 a.m. at 75 km/h. By 8 a.m. the gap is 270. Closing speed 135, so 2 hours. They meet at 10 a.m.
**T1. Plain distance = speed × time. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* one body, or two bodies with a simple link between their speeds; often a unit change. *Shortcut engine:* find the one speed you can, apply the link, multiply. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 3 Sep 2022 Slot 2):* car does D km in 50 minutes at 36 km/h, so D = 30 km. Bike must do 60 km in 40% less time, which is 30 minutes. Speed = 120 km/h.
**T4. Speed change gives time change. Seen once.** *Pattern clues:* “late by… early by…”, “usual time”, “by what percent should he increase his speed”. *Shortcut engine:* same distance, so time ratio is the speed ratio flipped. The minutes gained or lost = the difference in parts. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2021):* he wants to cut the time by three-fifths, so the new time is 2/5 of the old. Speed must become 5/2 of the old, which is 150% more. The 20 km and the 5 km/h are not needed.
**T9. Train passes a moving man. Seen once.** *Pattern clues:* “a man walking in the same/opposite direction”. *Shortcut engine:* distance = train length only. Speed = add (opposite) or subtract (same direction). *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* 300 m train crosses a 190 m platform in 14 s, so speed = 490 ÷ 14 = 35 m/s. Raised 20% = 42 m/s. Man at 10.8 km/h = 3 m/s, opposite direction, so 45 m/s. Time = 300 ÷ 45 = 6.67 s.
### Insurance archetype (Bank only)
**T3. Stoppages. Bank only.** *Shortcut engine:* minutes stopped per hour = (speed lost ÷ speed without stops) × 60. *Worked example (bank CON-TSD-11):* 60 km/h without stops, 48 km/h with stops. Lost 12 out of 60, so the bus stops 12 minutes in every hour.
## 3. Drill Set
All eight questions are original, written for this module, and each answer was checked by script. One drill per Core archetype.
### Drill 1 (Easy, T7)
A train crosses a pole in 12 seconds and a 180 m long platform in 21 seconds. What is the length of the train (in metres)?
1. 200 (B) 240 (C) 260 (D) 180 (E) 300
**Conventional Method:** let the length be L and speed S. L = 12S and L + 180 = 21S. Subtract: 180 = 9S, S = 20 m/s, L = 240 m.
**30-Second Exam Method:** extra 9 seconds cover the 180 m platform, so speed = 20 m/s. Length = 20 × 12 = 240. **Answer (B).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** none that finishes it; the direct route is two divisions. Back-check: 240 + 180 = 420 m in 21 s is 20 m/s, same as 240 m in 12 s.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** dividing 180 by 21 instead of by the extra 9 seconds. The platform time includes the train’s own length.
### Drill 2 (Product / non-linear trap, T2)
A man rides to his office at 40 km/h and returns by the same road at 60 km/h. What is his average speed for the whole trip (in km/h)?
1. 50 (B) 45 (C) 48 (D) 52 (E) 46
**Conventional Method:** average speed = 2 × 40 × 60 ÷ (40 + 60) = 4,800 ÷ 100 = 48.
**30-Second Exam Method:** take the road as 120 km. Going takes 3 h, returning 2 h. 240 km in 5 h = 48. **Answer (C).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** he spends more time at the slower speed, so the average sits below the middle value 50. That removes 50 and 52. Back-check: 120 ÷ 40 + 120 ÷ 60 = 5 hours, and 240 ÷ 5 = 48.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** 50, the plain average of 40 and 60, is option (A). The same question came in IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 (60 and 90) and SBI Clerk Prelims 20 Sep 2025 (20 and 30).
### Drill 3 (Two-step, T4)
Riding his scooter at 30 km/h, Ravi reaches office 10 minutes late. At 40 km/h he reaches 5 minutes early. How far is the office (in km)?
1. 20 (B) 25 (C) 40 (D) 30 (E) 35
**Conventional Method:** let the distance be d. d/30 − d/40 = 15/60, so d/120 = 1/4 and d = 30.
**30-Second Exam Method:** speeds 3 : 4, so times 4 : 3. The gap of 1 part = 10 + 5 = 15 minutes. Time at 30 km/h = 4 parts = 60 minutes. Distance = 30 km. **Answer (D).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** test an option: the two travel times must differ by exactly 15 minutes. 20 km gives 40 and 30 minutes (gap 10), out. 30 km gives 60 and 45 minutes (gap 15), fits. Back-check done in the same step.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** late and early are on opposite sides of the right time, so the gap is 10 + 5, not 10 − 5.
### Drill 4 (Phrasing trap, T6)
The speeds of A and B are in the ratio 5 : 6. For the same journey A takes 20 minutes more than B. How many minutes does A take?
1. 100 (B) 90 (C) 110 (D) 140 (E) 120
**Conventional Method:** let B’s time be t minutes; A’s is t + 20. Same distance: 5(t + 20) = 6t, so t = 100 and A takes 120.
**30-Second Exam Method:** speeds 5 : 6, so times 6 : 5. One part = 20 minutes. A = 6 parts = 120. **Answer (E).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** A’s time is 6 parts, so it must be a multiple of 6. That leaves 90 and 120. With 90 one part would be 15, not 20. Back-check: 120 and 100 are 6 : 5 and differ by 20.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** 100 is B’s time and it is option (A). Also, forgetting to flip the ratio gives A the smaller time, which contradicts “A takes more”.
### Drill 5 (T5)
Towns X and Y are 350 km apart. A car leaves X for Y at 8:00 a.m. at 50 km/h. Another car leaves Y for X at 9:00 a.m. at 70 km/h. At what time do they meet?
1. 10:55 a.m. (B) 11:30 a.m. (C) 11:00 a.m. (D) 12:00 noon (E) 11:15 a.m.
**Conventional Method:** let them meet t hours after 9:00. 50(t + 1) + 70t = 350, so 120t = 300 and t = 2.5.
**30-Second Exam Method:** by 9:00 the first car has done 50 km, so the gap is 300 km. Closing speed 50 + 70 = 120. 300 ÷ 120 = 2.5 hours after 9:00 = 11:30. **Answer (B).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** options are clock times, so do not try one. Back-check: first car 3.5 h × 50 = 175, second 2.5 h × 70 = 175, total 350.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** ignoring the head start (350 ÷ 120 hours after 8:00) gives 10:55, which is option (A).
### Drill 6 (T8)
Two trains, 220 m and 180 m long, run in the same direction on parallel tracks at 54 km/h and 36 km/h. How many seconds does the faster train take to pass the slower one completely?
1. 80 (B) 16 (C) 40 (D) 44 (E) 72
**Conventional Method:** relative speed = 54 − 36 = 18 km/h = 18 × 5/18 = 5 m/s. Distance = 220 + 180 = 400 m. Time = 400 ÷ 5 = 80 s.
**30-Second Exam Method:** same direction, so subtract: 15 − 10 = 5 m/s. Lengths add: 400 m. 400 ÷ 5 = 80. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** overtaking is slow. Even at the faster train’s full 15 m/s, 400 m takes about 27 s, so anything at or below that (16) is impossible. Back-check: 80 × 5 = 400.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** adding the speeds (the opposite-direction rule) gives 16, option (B). Using only one length gives 44 or 36.
### Drill 7 (T9)
A 240 m long train runs at 63 km/h. How many seconds does it take to pass a man walking at 9 km/h in the same direction?
1. 12 (B) 15 (C) 16 (D) 18 (E) 20
**Conventional Method:** relative speed = 63 − 9 = 54 km/h = 15 m/s. Time = 240 ÷ 15 = 16 s.
**30-Second Exam Method:** subtract speeds: 54 km/h is 15 m/s from the grid. 240 ÷ 15 = 16. **Answer (C).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** none needed. Back-check: 16 × 15 = 240.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** if the man walked the opposite way the answer would be 240 ÷ 20 = 12, which is option (A). The Clerk paper of 5 Oct 2025 used the opposite direction. Read the direction word.
### Drill 8 (T1)
A covers 96 km in 3 hours. A’s speed is 20% less than B’s speed. How many km does B cover in 5 hours?
1. 192 (B) 160 (C) 180 (D) 200 (E) 210
**Conventional Method:** A’s speed = 32 km/h. 32 = 0.8 × B’s speed, so B = 40 km/h. In 5 hours B covers 200 km.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 20% less means A : B = 4 : 5. A is 32, so one part is 8 and B is 40. 40 × 5 = 200. **Answer (D).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** B is faster than A, and A would do 160 km in 5 hours, so the answer is above 160. That removes 160. Back-check: 40 less 20% is 32.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** “A is 20% less than B” is not “B is 20% more than A”. Adding 20% to 32 gives 38.4 and then 192, which is option (A).

<tr>

</tr>
<tr>
<td>With and without stoppages</td>
<td>T3</td>
<td>Speed lost ÷ full speed × 60 minutes</td>
<td>Dividing by the slower speed</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Five facts to carry in:**
1. km/h × 5/18 = m/s. 72 km/h = 20 m/s; 54 = 15; 90 = 25; 108 = 30.
2. Same distance: speed ratio flipped = time ratio.
3. Towards each other add speeds; same direction subtract.
4. Crossing distance = own length + length of the thing crossed (zero for a pole or a man).
5. Average speed for equal distances is always below the plain average.

**Wording problems found in the bank (answers still reachable)**
- Two Sreedhar questions (S-MT24-32, S-MT2-47) do not state the direction of the trains; only the opposite-direction reading matches the key.
- Several Guidely train questions say “A crosses B” when B is actually the faster train; the relative speed still gives the keyed answer.
- Four Guidely “earlier/later” questions have a missing or garbled figure and were solved on the only reading that makes sense.
- Many Guidely questions carry an unrelated passage above the question; ignore it.
- Clock-time options (for example “11:00” against “12:00”) cannot be told apart by the script, so those 14 or so answers were confirmed by reading the working.
**Repeats:** 17 bank questions are exact repeats of another bank question. They are counted in the totals above.
**Exceptions to the methods:** none found. Every archetype’s method reached the key on every question that has a sound key.
**How the check was run:** the 394 questions were split into five batches and each batch was encoded and checked by a separate helper run, then all were re-run together by me in one pass. I did not re-read every encoding line by line.

# Profit & Loss - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened: 18 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers/shifts (2016 to Oct 2025, from your bank) giving 19 PYQs, plus 12 sister papers opened on the web (4 SBI Clerk, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk, 4 IBPS PO Prelims), all 12 of which carried a question, giving 13 sister questions. Bank questions used: 293 (19 PYQ + 274 practice). Partnership questions and chart-based questions were kept out. The floor of 8 target-exam shifts and 6 to 10 sister questions is met. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, not official papers.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Mechanics and Conversion Grid
**The one idea: put the cost price at 100 and walk along the chain.**
Cost Price (CP) → mark up → Marked Price (MP) → discount → Selling Price (SP).
- Profit and loss percent are always measured on **CP**.
- Discount percent is always measured on **MP**.
- Markup percent is measured on **CP**.
**The CP = 100 algorithm (use it on almost every question):**
1. Let CP = 100 units (not rupees).
2. Apply the markup to get MP in units. Apply the discount to MP to get SP in units.
3. Whatever rupee figure the question gives (a profit, a discount, a difference) matches some number of units.
4. Find 1 unit in rupees, then the thing asked.
Example: marked 25% above cost, 20% discount, discount is Rs 50. CP 100, MP 125, SP 100. Discount = 25 units = Rs 50, so 1 unit = Rs 2 and CP = Rs 200.
**Mechanics you must know cold:**
- **Multiplying factors.** 20% profit means × 1.2. 25% discount means × 0.75. Chain them: SP = CP × markup factor × discount factor.
- **Successive discounts multiply, they do not add.** 20% then 15% = 0.8 × 0.85 = 0.68, a 32% discount, not 35%.
- **Two scenarios, one cost.** “Sold at 25% loss; for Rs 378 more, 20% profit”: the rupee gap equals the percent gap of CP.
- **Equal cost prices, two articles.** Take each CP as 100; add the two results; compare with 200.
- **Equal selling prices, one at x% profit and one at x% loss:** always an overall loss of x²/100 percent.
- **Dishonest dealer selling “at cost”:** gain % = weight cheated ÷ weight actually given × 100.
**Conversion grid: percent to fraction and factor**
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Percent</td>
<td>Fraction</td>
<td>Profit factor</td>
<td>Loss / discount factor</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>10%</td>
<td>1/10</td>
<td>11/10</td>
<td>9/10</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>11 1/9%</td>
<td>1/9</td>
<td>10/9</td>
<td>8/9</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>12.5%</td>
<td>1/8</td>
<td>9/8</td>
<td>7/8</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>16 2/3%</td>
<td>1/6</td>
<td>7/6</td>
<td>5/6</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>20%</td>
<td>1/5</td>
<td>6/5</td>
<td>4/5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>25%</td>
<td>1/4</td>
<td>5/4</td>
<td>3/4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>33 1/3%</td>
<td>1/3</td>
<td>4/3</td>
<td>2/3</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>40%</td>
<td>2/5</td>
<td>7/5</td>
<td>3/5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>50%</td>
<td>1/2</td>
<td>3/2</td>
<td>1/2</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Non-linear warning.** Mark up 40% and discount 25% is not a 15% profit; it is 1.4 × 0.75 = 1.05, a 5% profit. Percentages on different bases never add or subtract.
**Universal fallback (works on every question in this topic):** CP = 100 units, walk the chain, match the rupee figure to units. If stuck, do that and nothing else.

Eleven bank questions are not really profit and loss (plain percentage, linear equations, one partnership question, one caselet that cannot be solved from its text) and are left out.
What stands out: the marked-price chain (P2) is 6 of the 19 Clerk PYQs and 10 of the 13 sister questions. With P3 and P10, which are the same chain in other clothes, it is 12 of 19. No paper opened asked a plain “CP and SP, find profit %” question on its own; that skill only appears as a step inside the others.
### Core archetypes (Confirmed and Seen once)
**P2. Marked price, discount and profit together. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “marked x% above cost”, “allows a discount of”, a rupee figure for profit or discount. *Shortcut engine:* CP = 100 units, walk the chain, match rupees to units. If MP is given in rupees, walk it directly. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 3):* MP Rs 180, discount 25%, loss 10%. SP = three-quarters of 180 = 135. That is 90% of CP, so CP = 135 ÷ 0.9 = Rs 150.
**P5. Two articles, overall profit or loss. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “two articles”, “same cost price” or “same selling price”, “overall loss of Rs”. *Shortcut engine:* equal CPs: take 100 each, add the two results, the net units = the rupee figure. Equal SPs: find each CP by dividing SP by its factor. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 24 Aug 2024 Shift 1):* equal CPs, A at +20%, B at −30%, overall loss Rs 50. On 100 each: +20 − 30 = −10 units = Rs 50, so 1 unit = Rs 5 and each CP = Rs 500. B at 40% profit = Rs 700.
**P3. Successive discounts. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “two successive discounts”, “first discount is 10%, find the second”. *Shortcut engine:* multiply the factors; or peel one discount off and see what factor is left. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 2):* MP Rs 1,050, bought for Rs 756, first discount 10%. After the first: 945. 756 ÷ 945 = 0.8, so the second discount is 20%.
**P10. Ratio of MP, SP, CP. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “ratio of marked price to cost price is 5 : 3”. *Shortcut engine:* use the ratio numbers as the units directly; bring in the third price with its factor. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 12 Dec 2021 Set 2):* MP : CP = 5 : 3, profit 20%. SP = 3 × 1.2 = 3.6. Discount = 5 − 3.6 = 1.4 on 5, which is 28%.
**P8. One article, two selling scenarios. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “had he sold it for Rs … more, he would have gained”. *Shortcut engine:* percent gap between the two scenarios = the rupee gap. Loss to profit: add the two percents. Profit to bigger profit: subtract. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 12 Dec 2021 Set 1):* 25% loss; Rs 378 more gives 20% profit. Gap = 25 + 20 = 45% of CP = 378. CP = Rs 840.
**P7. Dishonest dealer. Seen once.** *Pattern clues:* “professes to sell at cost price but uses a weight of”. *Shortcut engine:* gain % = grams cheated ÷ grams actually given × 100. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2016):* gives 800 g for 1 kg. Cheats 200 on 800 given: 25%.
### Insurance archetypes (Bank only)
**P1. Plain CP, SP, profit %.** Profit % = profit ÷ CP × 100. SP = CP × factor. CP = SP ÷ factor.
**P4. CP of x articles = SP of y articles.** Profit % = (x − y) ÷ y × 100; negative means loss. *Example (bank S-MT70-51):* CP of 16 = SP of 12. Gain = 4 on 12 = 33 1/3%.
**P6. Chain sale.** Multiply every factor along the chain. *Example:* A sells to B at 20% profit, B to C at 25% profit, C pays Rs 600. A’s cost = 600 ÷ (1.2 × 1.25) = Rs 400.
**P9. Mix then sell.** Find the cost of the whole mixture and the money received for the whole mixture, then treat as P1. *Example:* 2 kg at Rs 30 and 3 kg at Rs 40 cost Rs 180 for 5 kg; sold at Rs 45 a kg gives Rs 225; profit 45 on 180 = 25%.
## 3. Drill Set
All seven questions are original, written for this module, and each answer was recomputed. Four cover the required types; the rest give every Core archetype a drill.
### Drill 1 (Easy, P2)
A shopkeeper marks an article 40% above its cost price and allows a 25% discount. What is his profit percent?
1. 15% (B) 10% (C) 5% (D) 8% (E) 12%
**Conventional Method:** let CP = 100. MP = 140. SP = 140 − 25% of 140 = 105. Profit = 5%.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 1.4 × 0.75 = 1.05. Profit 5%. **Answer (C).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** the discount is taken on a bigger number than the markup, so the profit must be below 40 − 25 = 15. That removes 15. Back-check: three-quarters of 140 is 105.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** 40 − 25 = 15 is option (A). RRB Clerk Prelims 13 Dec 2025 Shift 1 asked this exact form with 40% and 20%.
### Drill 2 (Two-step, P2)
An article is marked 60% above its cost price and sold at a 25% discount. If the profit is Rs 90, find the marked price (in Rs).
1. 450 (B) 540 (C) 600 (D) 720 (E) 640
**Conventional Method:** let CP = c. MP = 1.6c, SP = 1.2c, profit = 0.2c = 90, so c = 450 and MP = 720.
**30-Second Exam Method:** CP 100, MP 160, SP 120. Profit 20 units = Rs 90, so 1 unit = Rs 4.5. MP = 160 units = 720. **Answer (D).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** MP is 160 units and CP is 100 units, so MP must be 8/5 of a whole rupee cost and larger than both CP and SP. 450 is the CP and 540 is the SP of the same article; the largest of that family, 720, is the MP. Back-check: 720 less 25% = 540; 540 − 450 = 90.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** the CP (450) and SP (540) are both in the options. Read which price is asked.
### Drill 3 (Product / non-linear trap, P3)
A jacket marked at Rs 2,500 is sold after two successive discounts of 20% and 15%. What is its selling price (in Rs)?
1. 1,625 (B) 1,700 (C) 1,750 (D) 1,650 (E) 1,725
**Conventional Method:** after 20%: 2,000. After 15% of 2,000 (300): 1,700.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 0.8 × 0.85 = 0.68. 68% of 2,500 = 1,700. **Answer (B).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** the second discount is taken on a smaller price, so the total discount is less than 35% and SP is more than 1,625. That removes 1,625. Back-check: 2,500 − 500 − 300 = 1,700.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** adding the discounts (35%) gives 1,625, option (A).
### Drill 4 (Phrasing trap, P5)
A trader sells two watches at Rs 1,200 each. On one he gains 20% and on the other he loses 20%. What is his overall result?
1. No profit, no loss (B) Loss of Rs 96 (C) Profit of Rs 100 (D) Loss of Rs 50 (E) Loss of Rs 100
**Conventional Method:** CP of first = 1,200 ÷ 1.2 = 1,000. CP of second = 1,200 ÷ 0.8 = 1,500. Total CP 2,500, total SP 2,400. Loss Rs 100.
**30-Second Exam Method:** same SP, same percent up and down: always a loss of 20 × 20 ÷ 100 = 4% of total CP. Total SP 2,400 is 96% of CP, so CP = 2,500 and loss = 100. **Answer (E).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** equal selling prices with equal gain and loss percent can never break even or give a profit, so (A) and (C) go at once. Back-check: 1,000 + 1,500 = 2,500.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** “same selling price” and “same cost price” behave differently. With the same cost price, +20% and −20% do cancel. Also, 4% of the SP total (Rs 96) is option (B); the 4% is on CP.
### Drill 5 (P8)
An article is sold at a loss of 12%. Had it been sold for Rs 360 more, there would have been a profit of 8%. Find the cost price (in Rs).
1. 1,800 (B) 1,500 (C) 2,000 (D) 1,440 (E) 2,400
**Conventional Method:** let CP = c. 1.08c − 0.88c = 360, so 0.2c = 360 and c = 1,800.
**30-Second Exam Method:** from 12% below cost to 8% above cost is a 20% swing = Rs 360. CP = 360 × 5 = 1,800. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** 20% of the answer must be 360; test: 20% of 1,800 = 360. Back-check: 88% of 1,800 = 1,584; add 360 = 1,944 = 108% of 1,800.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** loss and profit sit on opposite sides of cost, so the percents add (12 + 8), not subtract. SBI Clerk Prelims 21 Sep 2025 used profit to bigger profit (20% to 30%), where they subtract.
### Drill 6 (P10)
The ratio of the marked price to the cost price of an article is 8 : 5. If a discount of 25% is allowed, what is the profit percent?
1. 35% (B) 25% (C) 15% (D) 20% (E) 30%
**Conventional Method:** let MP = 8k and CP = 5k. SP = 75% of 8k = 6k. Profit = k on 5k = 20%.
**30-Second Exam Method:** MP 8, CP 5. Three-quarters of 8 = 6. Profit 1 on 5 = 20%. **Answer (D).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** markup is 60% (3 on 5); profit must be less than 60 − 25 = 35. That removes 35. Back-check: 6 ÷ 5 = 1.2.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** 60 − 25 = 35 is option (A). And profit is 1 on 5 (CP), not 1 on 6 or 1 on 8.
### Drill 7 (P7)
A dishonest shopkeeper claims to sell rice at cost price but uses a 900 g weight in place of 1 kg. What is his gain percent?
1. 10% (B) 9 1/11% (C) 11 1/9% (D) 12 1/2% (E) 11%
**Conventional Method:** he charges for 1,000 g and gives 900 g. Gain = 100 g on a cost of 900 g = 100/900 × 100 = 11 1/9%.
**30-Second Exam Method:** cheated 100, given 900. 100 ÷ 900 = 1/9 = 11 1/9%. **Answer (C).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** options are not whole numbers, so do not try one. Back-check: 1/9 is in the grid as 11 1/9%.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** dividing by 1,000 (what he pretends to give) gives 10%, option (A). Divide by what he actually gives.

</tr>

<td>A sells to B, B to C</td>
<td>P6</td>
<td>Multiply all factors</td>
<td>Adding the percents</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Mix two lots, sell</td>
<td>P9</td>
<td>Total cost, total receipts, then plain profit %</td>
<td>Averaging prices without weights</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Five facts to carry in:**
1. Profit and loss on CP; discount on MP.
2. SP = CP × markup factor × discount factor.
3. Successive changes multiply.
4. Same SP with +x% and −x% is always a loss of x²/100 percent.
5. Markup m% then discount d%: profit is always less than m − d.

## 6. Verification Log
**Built:** 6 Oct 2026.
**What was checked by script**
- 293 bank questions were each given an archetype and solved by script from the question wording, then compared with the keyed answer.
- 280 were machine-checked and match the key. Nineteen of those match only after rounding, because the options are rounded.
- 7 were checked by hand and agree: five whose options are words (for example “no profit, no loss”) and two keyed “cannot be determined”.
- 4 Sreedhar questions have image options, so their keys could not be checked.
- 1 caselet question (Guidely caselet no. 2) cannot be solved from the text given.
- 1 does not match: Guidely “mixture” question 5. Adding 200 ml water to every 1,000 ml milk and selling at cost gives 20%; the key says 25%. Treat that key as wrong.
- All 19 IBPS Clerk PYQs match their keys.
- All 7 drill answers were recomputed.

**Wording problems found in the bank (answers still reachable)**
- Several Guidely caselet questions are printed without their passage; they were solved from the passage attached to a neighbouring question.
- About ten Guidely and Sreedhar questions have a missing or garbled figure and were solved on the only reading that reaches an option; each is noted in the working files.
- Recurring percents such as 16.67% and 11.11% were treated as exact fractions (1/6, 1/9).
**Repeats:** 9 bank questions are exact repeats of another bank question. They are counted in the totals above.
**Exceptions to the methods:** none found. The CP = 100 fallback reached the key on every question with a sound key.
**How the check was run:** the 293 questions were split into four batches, each encoded and checked by a separate helper run, then all were re-run together by me in one pass. I did not re-read every encoding line by line.
**Not verified:** how often each archetype appears beyond this sample; the two sister questions flagged above; the four image-option questions. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, so exact numbers in the real paper may have differed.
**Corrections:** 6 Oct 2026: the SBI Clerk Pre 20 Sep 2025 S2 paper prints no answer key at all (noted while building Mixtures). The “key 2,400” recorded above for that paper was not a printed key. My working of 1,800 is one of the printed options and stands as unconfirmed-by-key, not as a disagreement.

# Boat & Stream - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened: 21 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers/shifts (2016 to Oct 2025, from your bank) giving 22 PYQs, plus 12 sister papers opened on the web (4 SBI Clerk, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk, 4 IBPS PO Prelims), 11 of which carried a question, giving 11 sister questions. Bank questions used: 247 (22 PYQ + 225 practice). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts and 6 to 10 sister questions is met. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, not official papers.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Mechanics and Conversion Grid
**The one idea: there are only four speeds, and they sit on a number line with equal gaps.**
Upstream (U) … Boat in still water (B) … Downstream (D), and the gap on each side is the Stream (S).
- D = B + S
- U = B − S
- B is the middle of D and U: B = (D + U) ÷ 2
- S is half the gap: S = (D − U) ÷ 2
Give me any two of the four and the other two follow. Every question in this topic is: find two of the four, get the rest, then use Distance = Speed × Time.
**The four-box algorithm (use it on every question):**
1. Draw four boxes: U, B, D, S.
2. Fill what the question gives. A trip (“60 km downstream in 2.5 hours”) fills D or U directly. A ratio fills boxes in parts.
3. Complete the boxes with the number-line rules.
4. Answer with distance ÷ the right box.
**Mechanics you must know cold:**
- **Ratio B : S = a : b** means D = a + b parts and U = a − b parts.
- **Ratio D : U = p : q** means B = (p + q)/2 parts and S = (p − q)/2 parts.
- **Same distance, times in ratio** means speeds in the flipped ratio (times D : U = 3 : 5 means speeds D : U = 5 : 3).
- **“Stream is 20% of boat”** is the ratio B : S = 5 : 1.
- **Still-water trip:** use B, not D or U.
- **Round trip time** = distance ÷ D + distance ÷ U. Never total distance ÷ B.
- **Two boats moving towards each other on the same river:** the stream cancels; closing speed = sum of their still-water speeds.
**Conversion grid: ratio to the four boxes (in parts)**

<tr>
<td>Given</td>
<td>B</td>
<td>S</td>
<td>D</td>
<td>U</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>B : S = 3 : 1</td>
<td>3</td>
<td>1</td>
<td>4</td>
<td>2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>B : S = 4 : 1</td>
<td>4</td>
<td>1</td>
<td>5</td>
<td>3</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>B : S = 5 : 1</td>
<td>5</td>
<td>1</td>
<td>6</td>
<td>4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>B : S = 7 : 2</td>
<td>7</td>
<td>2</td>
<td>9</td>
<td>5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>D : U = 2 : 1</td>
<td>3</td>
<td>1</td>
<td>4</td>
<td>2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>D : U = 5 : 3</td>
<td>4</td>
<td>1</td>
<td>5</td>
<td>3</td>
</tr>
<tr>

<td>5</td>

<td>8</td>

</tr>

<td>5</td>

</tr>
</table>
(For D : U rows the parts are doubled where needed so that B and S come out whole.)
**Non-linear warning.** A boat at 10 km/h in a 2 km/h stream does not average 10 km/h over a round trip. It goes 12 one way and 8 the other, and the slow leg takes longer, so the round trip is slower than in still water.
**Universal fallback (works on every question in this topic):** four boxes, fill, complete, divide. If stuck, do that and nothing else.
