Sorting rule used: if a ratio or percent links the speeds, the question is B4 even when it also has a round trip, because the ratio is what unlocks it.
What stands out: half of the Clerk PYQs (11 of 22) hand you a ratio or a percent between the speeds. Learn the grid in section 1 and half this topic is done.
### Core archetypes (all Confirmed)
**B4. A ratio or percent links the speeds. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “ratio of boat to stream is 7 : 2”, “stream is 20% of boat”, “ratio of downstream to upstream speed”. *Shortcut engine:* write all four boxes in parts. One real number in the question (a trip or a speed) gives the size of a part. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 12 Dec 2021 Set 1):* B : S = 7 : 2, so D = 9 parts and U = 5 parts. 432 km downstream in 6 h means D = 72, so 1 part = 8 and U = 40. Time for 280 km upstream = 7 hours.
**B2. Two trips give D and U. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “covers x km downstream in t₁ and y km upstream in t₂”; or “sum of upstream and downstream speeds”. *Shortcut engine:* divide to get D and U. B = middle, S = half the gap. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2016):* 32 km downstream in 240 minutes: D = 8. 48 km upstream in 1,440 minutes: U = 2. Stream = (8 − 2) ÷ 2 = 3 km/h.
**B3. Round trip or time difference. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “goes and comes back in 18 hours”, “takes 4 hours more upstream than downstream”. *Shortcut engine:* find D and U. Take a trial distance equal to the LCM of D and U, find its time (total or difference), then scale to the time given. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2017 model paper):* B = 6, S = 4, so D = 10 and U = 2. Trial distance 10 km each way: 1 h + 5 h = 6 h. Given 18 h is three times that, so 30 km each way. Total distance 60 km.
**B6. A speed is changed. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “if the speed of the boat is decreased by 20%”, “stream speed increased by 80%”. *Shortcut engine:* fill the four boxes first. Change only the box named. Rebuild D or U. Then do the new trip. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* 192 km downstream in 4 h: D = 48. 210 km upstream in 5 h: U = 42. B = 45. Cut 20%: 36. In still water for 3 h: 108 km.
**B1. Two speeds given outright. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “upstream speed is 6 km/h and stream is 2 km/h”. *Shortcut engine:* complete the boxes, divide. *Worked example (IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims 6 Dec 2025 Shift 2):* U = 6, S = 2, so B = 8 and D = 10. Time for 80 km downstream = 8 hours.
### Insurance archetypes (Bank only)
**B7. Two boats, meeting.** Towards each other on the same river: time = gap ÷ sum of still-water speeds; the stream cancels. Same direction: gap ÷ difference of still-water speeds. *Example (bank CON-BOT-15):* 108 km apart, still-water speeds 12 and 15, stream 3. They meet in 108 ÷ 27 = 4 hours.
**B5. Two mixed trips.** This is the one place the honest route is two equations, which is too slow. Test the options: pick the option’s D and U and see if both trips give the stated times. Only 2 of 247 bank questions are of this kind and no paper opened had one.
## 3. Drill Set
All five questions are original, written for this module, and each answer was recomputed. One drill per Core archetype; between them they cover the easy, two-step, non-linear and phrasing types.
### Drill 1 (Easy, B1)
The upstream speed of a boat is 9 km/h and the speed of the stream is 3 km/h. How many hours does the boat take to cover 60 km downstream?
1. 5 (B) 4 (C) 6 (D) 3 (E) 10
**Conventional Method:** B = U + S = 12. D = B + S = 15. Time = 60 ÷ 15 = 4.
**30-Second Exam Method:** downstream is two stream-steps above upstream: 9 + 3 + 3 = 15. 60 ÷ 15 = 4. **Answer (B).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** downstream speed must be above 12 (the still-water speed), so the time must be under 5. That removes 5, 6 and 10. Back-check: 4 × 15 = 60.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** adding the stream only once gives 12, which is the still-water speed, and 60 ÷ 12 = 5 is option (A). IBPS RRB Clerk Prelims 6 Dec 2025 Shift 2 asked this exact form.
### Drill 2 (B2)
A boat covers 84 km downstream in 3 hours and 60 km upstream in 3 hours. What is the speed of the stream (in km/h)?
1. 8 (B) 24 (C) 4 (D) 6 (E) 2
**Conventional Method:** D = 28, U = 20. S = (28 − 20) ÷ 2 = 4.
**30-Second Exam Method:** D 28, U 20, gap 8, half the gap = 4. **Answer (C).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** none needed. Back-check: B = 24; 24 + 4 = 28 and 24 − 4 = 20.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** forgetting to halve the gap gives 8, option (A). The still-water speed, 24, is option (B).
### Drill 3 (Product / non-linear trap, B3)
A boat’s speed in still water is 10 km/h and the stream flows at 2 km/h. The boat goes 48 km downstream and returns to the start. What is the total time taken (in hours)?
1. 9.6 (B) 9 (C) 12 (D) 10 (E) 8
**Conventional Method:** D = 12, U = 8. Time = 48/12 + 48/8 = 4 + 6 = 10.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 48 ÷ 12 = 4, 48 ÷ 8 = 6, total 10. **Answer (D).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** in still water the 96 km would take 9.6 h, and a stream always makes a round trip slower, so the answer is above 9.6. That removes 9.6, 9 and 8. Back-check: 4 × 12 = 48 and 6 × 8 = 48.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** 96 ÷ 10 = 9.6 is option (A). The stream does not cancel over a round trip.
### Drill 4 (Phrasing trap, B4)
The speed of the stream is 25% of the speed of a boat in still water. The boat covers 90 km upstream in 6 hours. How many hours will it take to cover 100 km downstream?
1. 5 (B) 6 (C) 3 (D) 8 (E) 4
**Conventional Method:** let B = b, S = b/4. U = 3b/4 = 15, so b = 20 and S = 5. D = 25. Time = 100 ÷ 25 = 4.
**30-Second Exam Method:** B : S = 4 : 1, so U = 3 parts and D = 5 parts. U = 90 ÷ 6 = 15, so 1 part = 5 and D = 25. 100 ÷ 25 = 4. **Answer (E).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** downstream is faster than upstream (15), so 100 km takes less than 6.67 h; and faster than still water (20), so less than 5 h. That leaves 3 and 4. Back-check: 25 − 5 − 5 = 15.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** “25% of the boat’s speed in still water”, not 25% of the upstream speed. And using the still-water speed 20 for the downstream trip gives 5, option (A).
### Drill 5 (Two-step, B6)
A boat covers 96 km downstream in 4 hours. The ratio of the boat’s speed in still water to the stream’s speed is 5 : 1. If the stream’s speed increases by 50%, how many hours will the boat take to cover 70 km upstream?
1. 5 (B) 4 (C) 7 (D) 6 (E) 10
**Conventional Method:** D = 24 = 6 parts, so B = 20 and S = 4. New S = 6. New U = 14. Time = 70 ÷ 14 = 5.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 24 ÷ 6 parts = 4 a part. B 20, S 4. Stream becomes 6, upstream becomes 14. 70 ÷ 14 = 5. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** new upstream speed is below the old one (16), so the time is above 70 ÷ 16, about 4.4. That removes 4. Back-check: 5 × 14 = 70.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** only the stream changes; the boat’s own speed stays 20. Raising the boat by 50% instead, or using the old upstream speed, gives a wrong time. IBPS PO Prelims 23 Aug 2025 Shift 2 used the same build with an 80% rise.
## 4. Cheat Sheet and 30-Second Recall Matrix
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>If you see…</td>
<td>Archetype</td>
<td>Do this</td>
<td>Watch for</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>A ratio or percent between speeds</td>
<td>B4</td>
<td>All four boxes in parts; one real number sizes the part</td>
<td>Which two speeds the ratio is about</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two trips with distance and time</td>
<td>B2</td>
<td>D and U by division; B = middle, S = half gap</td>
<td>Forgetting to halve</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Round trip, or “t hours more upstream”</td>
<td>B3</td>
<td>Trial distance = LCM of D and U; scale to the given time</td>
<td>Using B for a round trip</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“Speed increased / decreased by”</td>
<td>B6</td>
<td>Fill boxes, change one box, rebuild</td>
<td>Changing the wrong box</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two speeds stated</td>
<td>B1</td>
<td>Complete boxes, divide</td>
<td>U to D is two stream-steps</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two boats</td>
<td>B7</td>
<td>Still-water speeds add (towards) or subtract (same way)</td>
<td>Adding the stream twice</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two mixed trips</td>
<td>B5</td>
<td>Test options</td>
<td>Trying to solve two equations</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Five facts to carry in:**
1. D = B + S, U = B − S.
2. B = (D + U) ÷ 2, S = (D − U) ÷ 2.
3. B : S = a : b gives D : U = (a + b) : (a − b).
4. Same distance: time ratio is the speed ratio flipped.
5. A round trip in a stream is always slower than the same trip in still water.

- Percents such as 14.28% and 16.67% were treated as exact fractions (1/7, 1/6).

The re-sort used a word search on the question text, so a handful of B3 and B4 labels could be argued the other way. It does not affect any answer check.
**Exceptions to the methods:** B5 (two mixed trips) is the only type where the no-algebra route is option testing.
**How the check was run:** the 247 questions were split into three batches, each encoded and checked by a separate helper run, then all were re-run together by me in one pass. I did not re-read every encoding line by line.

# Mixtures & Alligations - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened: 18 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers/shifts (2017 to Oct 2025, from your bank) giving 18 PYQs, plus 12 sister papers opened on the web (4 SBI Clerk, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk, 4 IBPS PO Prelims), all 12 of which carried a question, giving 16 sister questions. Bank questions used: 245 (18 PYQ + 227 practice). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts and 6 to 10 sister questions is met. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, not official papers.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Mechanics and Conversion Grid
**The one idea: follow the ingredient that does not change.**
In almost every exam question, something is added or removed, and one ingredient is left untouched. Fix your eyes on that one.
- **Add water:** milk does not change.
- **Add milk:** water does not change.
- **Take out some mixture:** both go down, but the ratio does not change.
**The litres-table algorithm (use it on every question):**
1. Turn the starting ratio or percent into litres of each ingredient.
2. If mixture is removed, shrink both by the same fraction (the ratio stays).
3. Add what is added, to the right ingredient only.
4. Read off the answer, or match the final ratio to find the unknown.
Example: 56 litres, milk : water = 4 : 3, so 32 and 24. Water is added until the ratio is 8 : 7. Milk is still 32 = 8 parts, so a part is 4 and water is 28. Added: 4 litres.
**When litres are not given, use the unchanged ingredient to line up the two ratios.** Before 5 : 4, after adding 2 litres of water 10 : 9. Milk is unchanged, so write the first ratio as 10 : 8. Water went from 8 parts to 9 parts: 1 part = 2 litres. New water = 9 parts = 18 litres.
**Mechanics you must know cold:**
- **“Water is 25% of milk”** means milk : water = 4 : 1. It does not mean water is 25% of the mixture.
- **Removing mixture never changes the ratio.** Only the amounts shrink.
- **Remove then replace with water:** milk left = milk before × (fraction of mixture kept).
- **Mixing two vessels:** add the milk litres, add the total litres. Never average the two percents unless the volumes are equal.
- **Repeated replacement (same amount each time):** milk left = milk at start × (fraction kept) × (fraction kept) …
- **Alligation cross (prices or percents):** cheaper : dearer = (dearer − mean) : (mean − cheaper).
**Conversion grid: ratio to share of the mixture**
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Milk : Water</td>
<td>Milk share</td>
<td>Water share</td>
<td>Water as % of milk</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>1 : 1</td>
<td>1/2</td>
<td>1/2</td>
<td>100%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2 : 1</td>
<td>2/3</td>
<td>1/3</td>
<td>50%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3 : 1</td>
<td>3/4</td>
<td>1/4</td>
<td>33 1/3%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>3 : 2</td>
<td>3/5</td>
<td>2/5</td>
<td>66 2/3%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>4 : 1</td>
<td>4/5</td>
<td>1/5</td>
<td>25%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>4 : 3</td>
<td>4/7</td>
<td>3/7</td>
<td>75%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>5 : 3</td>
<td>5/8</td>
<td>3/8</td>
<td>60%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>7 : 3</td>

<td>about 43%</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Non-linear warning.** 20 litres at 70% milk mixed with 30 litres at 60% milk is not 65% milk. It is (14 + 18) ÷ 50 = 64%. The bigger vessel pulls the answer towards itself.
**Universal fallback (works on every question in this topic):** litres table with two columns (milk, water) and one row per step. If stuck, do that and nothing else.

What stands out: 16 of the 18 Clerk PYQs and 11 of the 16 sister questions are M2, M3 or M8, which are all the same litres table with one, two or three rows. Classic textbook items (repeated replacement, the alligation cross on prices) take up 72 bank questions but gave only one question in the 30 papers opened.
### Core archetypes (Confirmed and Seen once)
**M2. Add an ingredient. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “x litres of water is added”, “the ratio becomes”. *Shortcut engine:* the other ingredient is unchanged. Keep its litres (or its parts) fixed and read the new amount of the one that changed. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* 56 litres, 4 : 3, so milk 32 and water 24. New ratio 8 : 7 with milk still 32: water = 28. Y = 4. *When both are added (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* 208 litres, 3 : 5, so 78 and 130. Water + 70 = 200 = 10 parts, so a part is 20 and milk must be 140. X = 140 − 78 = 62.
**M3. Take out some mixture, then add one ingredient. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “litres of the mixture is taken out and replaced with”, “removed and x litres of water added”. *Shortcut engine:* shrink both by the removal (ratio unchanged), then add. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 3):* 40 litres, 3 : 2. Take out 10: 30 litres left = 18 and 12. Add 10 milk: 28 and 12. Ratio 7 : 3.
**M8. Several steps, remove then add both, or remove only. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* three actions in a row; or “replaced with 4 litres of milk and 7 litres of water”. *Shortcut engine:* one row of the litres table per action. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 2):* 150 milk + 50 water. Remove 20%: 120 and 40. Water must become 50%, so water must equal milk, 120. y = 80. *Remove only (IBPS Clerk Prelims 12 Dec 2021 Set 2):* 60 litres at 3 : 2, 20 litres taken out, ratio of what is left? Still 3 : 2. No working needed.
**M5. Two vessels. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “jar P … jar Q … mixed together”, “10 litres transferred from A to B”. *Shortcut engine:* litres of milk from each, add; total litres, add; divide. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 4):* 20 litres at 70% milk = 14. 30 litres at 3 : 2 = 18. Milk 32 in 50 litres = 64%.
**M6. Alligation on prices. Seen once.** *Pattern clues:* “two varieties costing Rs … and Rs … mixed in the ratio”. *Shortcut engine:* if the ratio is given, total cost ÷ total quantity = mean price. If the mean is given and the ratio is asked, use the cross: cheaper : dearer = (dearer − mean) : (mean − cheaper). *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2023):* Rs 30 and Rs 54 a litre in 7 : 5. Cost = 210 + 270 = 480 for 12 litres = Rs 40 a litre. Sold at 44: profit 4 on 40 = 10%.
### Insurance archetypes (Bank only)
**M4. Repeated replacement.** Milk left = starting milk × (fraction kept) once for each round. *Example:* 80 litres of milk; 16 litres drawn and replaced with water, twice. Fraction kept = 64/80 = 4/5. Milk left = 80 × 4/5 × 4/5 = 51.2 litres.
**M7. Water added and sold for profit.** Selling the mixture at the cost price of milk: profit % = water ÷ milk × 100. *Example:* 10 litres of water in 40 litres of milk gives 25%.
**M1. Just read a ratio.** Total ÷ sum of parts = one part.
## 3. Drill Set
All six questions are original, written for this module, and each answer was recomputed. Four cover the required types; the rest give every Core archetype a drill.
### Drill 1 (Easy, M2)
A 60-litre mixture has milk and water in the ratio 3 : 1. How many litres of water must be added so that the ratio becomes 3 : 2?
1. 10 (B) 30 (C) 20 (D) 15 (E) 12
**Conventional Method:** milk 45, water 15. Let w be added: 45 : (15 + w) = 3 : 2, so 15 + w = 30 and w = 15.
**30-Second Exam Method:** milk is unchanged and is 3 parts both times, so a part stays 15 litres. Water goes from 1 part to 2 parts: add 15. **Answer (D).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** one part is 15 litres and water rises by a whole number of parts, so the answer is a multiple of 15. That leaves 15 and 30. Back-check: 45 : 30 = 3 : 2.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** 30 is the final water, not the water added. It is option (B).
### Drill 2 (Two-step, M3)
An 80-litre mixture has milk and water in the ratio 5 : 3. 16 litres of the mixture is taken out and replaced with 16 litres of water. How many litres of water are in the final mixture?
1. 46 (B) 30 (C) 40 (D) 24 (E) 36
**Conventional Method:** milk 50, water 30. 16 litres out is one-fifth: milk 40, water 24. Add 16 water: 40.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 64 litres left at 5 : 3 is 40 and 24. Water 24 + 16 = 40. **Answer (C).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** the final total is 80 again and milk can only have gone down from 50, so water is above 30. And water is at most 30 + 16 = 46 only if no water left with the removed mixture, which it did, so below 46. That leaves 40 and 36. Back-check: milk 40 + water 40 = 80.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** adding 16 to the original 30 gives 46, option (A). The 16 litres taken out carried 6 litres of water with it.
### Drill 3 (Product / non-linear trap, M5)
Jar A has 30 litres of a mixture that is 40% milk. Jar B has 20 litres of a mixture that is 60% milk. Both are poured into one jar. What percent of the new mixture is milk?
1. 50% (B) 48% (C) 52% (D) 45% (E) 46%
**Conventional Method:** milk = 40% of 30 + 60% of 20 = 12 + 12 = 24. Total 50 litres. 24/50 = 48%.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 12 + 12 = 24 in 50, double it: 48%. **Answer (B).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** the bigger jar is the weaker one, so the answer sits below the midpoint 50 and above 40. That removes 50 and 52. Back-check: water 18 + 8 = 26, and 24 + 26 = 50.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** averaging 40 and 60 gives 50, option (A). That is right only when the two jars are the same size.
### Drill 4 (Phrasing trap, M2)
In a 100-litre mixture of milk and water, the water is 25% of the milk. If 20 litres of water is added, what is the ratio of water to milk in the new mixture?
1. 3 : 5 (B) 2 : 3 (C) 1 : 3 (D) 9 : 20 (E) 1 : 2
**Conventional Method:** milk : water = 4 : 1, so milk 80 and water 20. New water 40. Water : milk = 40 : 80 = 1 : 2.
**30-Second Exam Method:** “25% of the milk” is 4 : 1. 100 litres gives 80 and 20. Water becomes 40. 40 : 80 = 1 : 2. **Answer (E).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** options are ratios, so do not try one. Back-check: 20 is 25% of 80.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** reading it as “water is 25% of the mixture” gives 75 and 25, then 45 : 75 = 3 : 5, option (A). Also the question asks water to milk, not milk to water. IBPS Clerk Prelims 24 Aug 2024 Shift 1 used exactly this phrasing.
### Drill 5 (M8)
A 90-litre mixture has milk and water in the ratio 2 : 1. 30 litres of the mixture is taken out, and then 5 litres of milk and 15 litres of water are added. What is the new ratio of milk to water?
1. 9 : 7 (B) 7 : 9 (C) 13 : 9 (D) 5 : 4 (E) 4 : 3
**Conventional Method:** milk 60, water 30. Remove one-third: 40 and 20. Add: 45 and 35. Ratio 9 : 7.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 60 litres left at 2 : 1 = 40 and 20. Plus 5 and 15 = 45 and 35 = 9 : 7. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** options are ratios, so do not try one. Back-check: 45 + 35 = 80 = 90 − 30 + 20.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** skipping the removal gives 65 : 45 = 13 : 9, option (C). Writing water first gives 7 : 9, option (B).
### Drill 6 (M6)
Rice costing Rs 40 a kg is mixed with rice costing Rs 55 a kg so that the mixture costs Rs 46 a kg. In what ratio (cheaper : dearer) are they mixed?
1. 2 : 3 (B) 3 : 5 (C) 5 : 3 (D) 3 : 2 (E) 4 : 3
**Conventional Method:** let c kg of cheaper and d kg of dearer: 40c + 55d = 46(c + d), so 9d = 6c and c : d = 3 : 2.
**30-Second Exam Method:** cross: (55 − 46) : (46 − 40) = 9 : 6 = 3 : 2. **Answer (D).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** the mean, 46, is nearer to 40 than to 55, so there is more of the cheaper rice. That removes 2 : 3 and 3 : 5. Back-check: 3 × 40 + 2 × 55 = 230 for 5 kg = 46.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** the cross is upside-down: the cheaper one gets the gap from the dearer side. Writing it straight gives 2 : 3, option (A).
## 4. Cheat Sheet and 30-Second Recall Matrix
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>If you see…</td>
<td>Archetype</td>
<td>Do this</td>
<td>Watch for</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“x litres of water (or milk) added”</td>
<td>M2</td>
<td>Unchanged ingredient keeps its litres or parts</td>
<td>Added amount vs final amount</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“taken out and replaced with”</td>
<td>M3</td>
<td>Shrink both, then add</td>
<td>Forgetting what left with the removed mixture</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Three actions, or both added after removal</td>
<td>M8</td>
<td>One table row per action</td>
<td>Skipping a step</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>“taken out” and nothing else</td>
<td>M8</td>
<td>Ratio is unchanged</td>
<td>Doing any working at all</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two jars or vessels</td>
<td>M5</td>
<td>Add milk litres, add total litres</td>
<td>Averaging percents</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Two prices and a mean price</td>
<td>M6</td>
<td>Cross: (dearer − mean) : (mean − cheaper)</td>
<td>Cross is upside-down</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Drawn and replaced two or more times</td>
<td>M4</td>
<td>Starting milk × fraction kept, once per round</td>
<td>Subtracting the same litres each time</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Water added, sold at cost</td>
<td>M7</td>
<td>Water ÷ milk × 100</td>
<td>Dividing by the mixture</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Five facts to carry in:**
1. Adding one ingredient leaves the other unchanged.
2. Removing mixture leaves the ratio unchanged.
3. “A is 25% of B” is a ratio between A and B, not a share of the whole.
4. Mixing two vessels: litres add, percents do not.
5. The mean always lies nearer the ingredient there is more of.

Note on the SBI Clerk Pre 22 Feb 2025 question: the printed solution splits 70 litres at 3 : 7 as 30 and 40, which is wrong (it is 21 and 49), but the printed answer 42 is right with the correct split.
**Wording problems found in the bank (answers still reachable)**
- Guidely “more than one vessel” 9 gives no quantities; equal quantities were assumed.
- Guidely “simple mixture” 4: the key of 20% only works as a percent of the starting mixture.
- Guidely “n times” 2 and 5 and “price” 2 have a loose phrase each and were solved on the reading that reaches an option.
- Percents such as 28.57% were treated as exact fractions (2/7).
**Repeats:** 4 bank questions are exact repeats of another bank question. They are counted in the totals above.
**Sorting note:** questions that add both milk and water with nothing removed were filed under M2 by two helper runs and under M8 by one (five Guidely questions). It does not affect any answer check, and the method is the same litres table.
**Exceptions to the methods:** none found. The litres table reached the key on every question with a checkable key.
**How the check was run:** the 245 questions were split into three batches, each encoded and checked by a separate helper run, then all were re-run together by me in one pass. I did not re-read every encoding line by line.
**Not verified:** how often each archetype appears beyond this sample; the three sister questions from the paper with no key; the six image-option questions. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, so exact numbers in the real paper may have differed.
**Corrections:** none so far.

# Simplification - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Added on request; it was not in the original topic list. Approximation is folded in as one archetype because it is the same skill with rounding first. Papers opened: 22 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers/shifts (2016 to Oct 2025, from your bank) giving 227 PYQs (223 simplification + 4 approximation), plus 12 sister papers opened on the web (4 SBI Clerk, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk, 4 IBPS PO Prelims), all 12 of which carried questions, giving 88 sister questions with a readable expression. Bank questions used: 2,227 (227 PYQ + 2,000 practice), of which 436 are printed as images and could not be read. The floor of 8 target-exam shifts and 6 to 10 sister questions is met many times over. Memory-based papers are reconstructions, not official papers.
This is the largest block in the paper: the four 4 Oct 2025 Clerk shifts in your bank carry 10 to 12 of these each. It deserves the most practice time of any topic in this file.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Mechanics and Conversion Grid
**The one idea: never calculate what you can look up, cancel, or split.** Every question here is plain arithmetic. Speed comes from three habits: know the tables below by heart, cancel before you multiply, and split awkward numbers into easy pieces.
**The order rule (BODMAS, with “of” promoted):**
1. Brackets, innermost first.
2. Roots and powers.
3. **“of”** (it binds tighter than ÷ and ×). “1/2 of 90” is one lump, 45.
4. ÷ and ×, strictly left to right.
5. + and −, left to right.
**When the question mark is buried** (it is, in most Clerk questions): finish the side that has no question mark, then undo the other side one step at a time, outermost step first. Example: 35% of 480 + 15% of ? = 204. Known part 168. So 15% of ? = 36. So ? = 36 ÷ 15 × 100 = 240.
**Mechanics you must know cold:**
- **Percent as a fraction.** 25% of 480 is 480 ÷ 4. Use the grid.
- **Percent by splitting.** 35% = 25% + 10%. 15% = 10% + half of it. 45% = 50% − 5%. 65% = 50% + 10% + 5%.
- **Swap rule.** a% of b = b% of a. So 48% of 250 = 250% of 48 = 120.
- **Dividing by decimals.** ÷ 12.5 is × 8 ÷ 100. ÷ 0.25 is × 4. ÷ 2.5 is × 4 ÷ 10. ÷ 1.44 is × 100 ÷ 144.
- **Multiplying by 25, 125, 11.** × 25 is × 100 ÷ 4. × 125 is × 1000 ÷ 8. × 11: write the number, add neighbours (47 × 11 = 4 \| 4+7 \| 7 = 517).
- **Fractions chained with “of”.** Cancel across all of them before multiplying.
- **Mixed numbers.** Add the whole parts and the fraction parts separately.
- **Same base powers.** Multiply: add the powers. Divide: subtract. Power of a power: multiply. First rewrite everything in one base (4 = 2², 8 = 2³, 16 = 2⁴, 32 = 2⁵, 64 = 2⁶; 9 = 3², 27 = 3³, 81 = 3⁴; 25 = 5², 125 = 5³; 49 = 7², 343 = 7³).
- **Difference of squares.** a² − b² = (a + b)(a − b). 140² − 60² = 200 × 80 = 16,000.

**Reading a big root without the table.** Square root of 4489: it ends in 9, so the root ends in 3 or 7; 60² = 3600 and 70² = 4900, so it is sixty-something; 65² = 4225 is below 4489, so it is 67. Cube root of 6859: ends in 9, so the root ends in 9; 6 is between 1³ and 2³, so 19.
**Non-linear warning.** The root of a sum is not the sum of the roots: √(225 + 400) = √625 = 25, not 15 + 20. And ÷ then × goes left to right: 144 ÷ 12 × 3 is 36, not 4.
**Universal fallback (works on every question in this topic):** BODMAS in the order above, one line per step, known side first. If stuck, do that and nothing else.

What stands out:
- Percent questions lead: 76 of the 227 Clerk PYQs, and in every one of the 22 papers.
- In 155 of the 227 Clerk PYQs the question mark is buried inside the expression, not sitting alone after the equals sign. Practise undoing, not just computing.
- Approximation is close to absent in Clerk Prelims: 4 questions, all from one 2017 model paper. In the sister papers it appeared only in IBPS PO Prelims, never in SBI Clerk or RRB Clerk. Your bank has 453 approximation questions; do not let that share set your practice time for Prelims.
### Core archetypes
**S3. Percent of a number. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* one or more “a% of b” terms. *Shortcut engine:* grid fraction if there is one; otherwise split the percent, or use 1% and 10% of the number. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 3):* 23% of 600 + 33% of 800 = ? + 53% of 400. 1% of each is 6, 8 and 4, so 138 + 264 = ? + 212. 402 − 212 = 190.
**S4. Squares, cubes and roots. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* √, ∛, ² or ³. *Shortcut engine:* replace each from the grids first, then it is a plain question. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 3):* √784 ÷ ∛343 × √625 − ∛1728 = 28 ÷ 7 × 25 − 12 = 100 − 12 = 88.
**S1. Plain whole-number BODMAS. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* only whole numbers and + − × ÷. *Shortcut engine:* ÷ and × left to right, cancelling as you go; then + and −. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* ? = 225 ÷ 45 ÷ 5 × 75. 225 ÷ 45 = 5; 5 ÷ 5 = 1; 1 × 75 = 75. *One identity that has come (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2016):* (854³ − 276³) ÷ (854² + 854 × 276 + 276²) is the pattern (a³ − b³) ÷ (a² + ab + b²) = a − b = 578.
**S5. Fractions and mixed numbers. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* “a/b of”, mixed numbers such as 7 1/2. *Shortcut engine:* chain of “of”: cancel across, then multiply once. Mixed numbers: wholes and fractions separately. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 4):* 3/4 of 8/9 of 27/32 of 4608. Cancel: 3 × 8 × 27 over 4 × 9 × 32 comes down to 9/16. 4608 ÷ 16 = 288; × 9 = 2592. *Mixed numbers (same paper):* 7 1/2 + 6 2/3 − 5 1/6 + 3 1/3. Wholes: 7 + 6 − 5 + 3 = 11. Fractions in sixths: 3 + 4 − 1 + 2 = 8 sixths = 1 1/3. Answer 12 1/3.
**S7. Decimals. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* decimal points, exact answer wanted. *Shortcut engine:* turn the decimal into a fraction you know (12.5 = 100/8, 0.75 = 3/4, 2.5 = 10/4), or shift the point on both numbers. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 2):* 3750 ÷ 12.5 + 180. 3750 × 8 ÷ 100 = 300. Answer 480.
**S6. Powers. Confirmed.** *Pattern clues:* 2\^? or 7\^?, or powers above the cube. *Shortcut engine:* rewrite everything in one base, then only add and subtract the powers. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* 2⁴ × 2\^? = 32 × 64 = 2⁵ × 2⁶ = 2¹¹. So ? = 11 − 4 = 7.
**A0. Approximation. Seen once (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2017 model paper).** *Pattern clues:* “approximate value”, numbers like 31.98 or 499.99. *Shortcut engine:* round every number to the nearest friendly whole number first (and percents to grid fractions), then solve as an exact question. Pick the nearest option. *Worked example (that paper):* (31.98)² − (35.02)² + (19.03)² = ?² + √255. Round: 32² − 35² + 19² = 1024 − 1225 + 361 = 160. √255 is about 16. So ?² = 144 and ? = ±12.
There is no Insurance group in this topic: every archetype has appeared in a paper.
## 3. Drill Set
All seven questions are original, written for this module, and each answer was checked by script. One drill per archetype; between them they cover the easy, two-step, non-linear and phrasing types.
### Drill 1 (Easy, S1)
144 ÷ 12 × 3 + 18 × 5 − 26 = ?
1. 68 (B) 104 (C) 100 (D) 96 (E) 110
**Conventional Method:** 144 ÷ 12 = 12; 12 × 3 = 36; 18 × 5 = 90; 36 + 90 − 26 = 100.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 36 + 90 = 126; minus 26 = 100. **Answer (C).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** unit digits only: 6 + 0 − 6 = 0, so the answer ends in 0. That leaves 100 and 110. Back-check: 126 − 26 = 100.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** doing 12 × 3 first gives 144 ÷ 36 = 4, then 68, option (A). ÷ and × go left to right.
### Drill 2 (Two-step, S3)
35% of 480 + 15% of ? = 12² + 60
1. 360 (B) 240 (C) 200 (D) 280 (E) 320
**Conventional Method:** 35% of 480 = 168. Right side = 144 + 60 = 204. So 15% of ? = 36 and ? = 36 × 100 ÷ 15 = 240.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 35% of 480 = 120 + 48 = 168. 204 − 168 = 36. 15% is 36, so 5% is 12 and 100% is 240. **Answer (B).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** test the middle option: 15% of 240 = 24 + 12 = 36. It fits, so stop. Back-check: 168 + 36 = 204.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** stopping at 36 (the value of the percent term) and hunting for it in the options. The question mark is the number the percent is taken of.
### Drill 3 (Product / non-linear trap, S4)
√(225 + 400) + 6³ − 141 = ?
1. 110 (B) 90 (C) 105 (D) 100 (E) 95
**Conventional Method:** 225 + 400 = 625; √625 = 25. 6³ = 216. 25 + 216 − 141 = 100.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 25 + 216 = 241; minus 141 = 100. **Answer (D).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** unit digits: 5 + 6 − 1 = 0, so it ends in 0. That removes 105 and 95. Back-check: 241 − 141 = 100.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** taking √225 + √400 = 15 + 20 = 35 gives 110, option (A). Add inside the root first.
### Drill 4 (Phrasing trap, S5)
3/4 of 480 ÷ 1/2 of 90 + 2 1/2 = ?
1. 8 (B) 12 1/2 (C) 10 1/2 (D) 6 1/2 (E) 18
**Conventional Method:** “of” first: 3/4 of 480 = 360 and 1/2 of 90 = 45. 360 ÷ 45 = 8. 8 + 2 1/2 = 10 1/2.
**30-Second Exam Method:** 360 ÷ 45 = 8; add 2 1/2. **Answer (C).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** the first part is a whole number (8) and 2 1/2 is added, so the answer ends in a half. That removes 8 and 18. Back-check: 45 × 8 = 360.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** “of” is done before ÷. Going left to right (360 ÷ 1/2 = 720, then × 90) gives a huge number. IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 1 used this exact shape with roots.
### Drill 5 (S6)
8³ × 4⁴ ÷ 16² = 2\^?
1. 7 (B) 9 (C) 11 (D) 8 (E) 13
**Conventional Method:** 512 × 256 ÷ 256 = 512 = 2⁹.
**30-Second Exam Method:** in base 2: 8³ = 2⁹, 4⁴ = 2⁸, 16² = 2⁸. 9 + 8 − 8 = 9. **Answer (B).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** none needed once the bases match. Back-check: 2⁹ = 512.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** adding the written powers (3 + 4 − 2 = 5) without first changing 8, 4 and 16 into powers of 2.
### Drill 6 (S7)
3750 ÷ 12.5 + 0.75 × 64 − 148 = ?
1. 196 (B) 204 (C) 248 (D) 180 (E) 200
**Conventional Method:** 3750 ÷ 12.5 = 300. 0.75 × 64 = 48. 300 + 48 − 148 = 200.
**30-Second Exam Method:** ÷ 12.5 is × 8 ÷ 100: 300. Three-quarters of 64: 48. 348 − 148 = 200. **Answer (E).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** unit digits: 0 + 8 − 8 = 0, so it ends in 0. That leaves 180 and 200. Back-check: 12.5 × 300 = 3750.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** misplacing the decimal: 3750 ÷ 12.5 is 300, not 30 or 3000. Quick sense check: 12.5 × 100 is 1250, so the answer is a few hundred.
### Drill 7 (A0)
What approximate value comes in place of the question mark? 24.98% of 639.97 + (17.02)² − √323.9 = ?
1. 431 (B) 411 (C) 451 (D) 471 (E) 391
**Conventional Method:** exact working gives about 159.86 + 289.68 − 18.00 = 431.5.
**30-Second Exam Method:** round: 25% of 640 = 160; 17² = 289; √324 = 18. 160 + 289 − 18 = 431. **Answer (A).**
**Option Elimination Hack:** not needed; the options are 20 apart and the rounding moves the answer by less than 1. Back-check: 449 − 18 = 431.
**IBPS Trap Warning:** rounding 24.98% to 25% is safe; rounding 17.02 to 20 is not. Round to the nearest whole number, or to the nearest value in your tables, never further.

</tr>

<td>A0</td>
<td>Round first, then solve exactly</td>
<td>Rounding too far</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>? in the middle</td>
<td>any</td>
<td>Finish the known side, undo the other side step by step</td>
<td>Stopping one step early</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Three checks that kill options (use only when options are whole numbers):**
1. **Unit digit.** Work only the last digits through + − ×. Does not work through ÷ or roots.
2. **Size.** A rough round-number version tells you the hundred the answer lives in.
3. **Multiple.** If the answer is something × 9, its digits add to a multiple of 9; if something × 5, it ends in 0 or 5.

## 6. Verification Log
**Built:** 6 Oct 2026.
**How this topic was checked (different from the other modules).** These questions are bare expressions, so I wrote one evaluator that reads each expression, tries every option in place of the question mark, and reports which option makes the two sides equal. No question was encoded by hand and no helper runs were used. For approximation questions the evaluator first rounds every decimal to the nearest whole number and looks for an exact fit; if there is none, it solves exactly and takes the nearest option.
**Results on the 2,227 bank questions**
- 1,753 were machine-checked and agree with the key: 1,435 exact, 172 approximation questions exact after rounding, 145 approximation questions where the key is the nearest option, and 1 exponent question confirmed by hand (S-MT62-58).
- 436 are printed as images (432 Sreedhar, 4 Guidely) and could not be read or sorted.
- 4 keys are wrong: S-MT26-54 (63% of 1244 is 783.72, keyed 787.72), S-MT22-58 (the power is 4, keyed 3), S-MT21-65 (the answer is 4, keyed 2), S-MT10-58 (the power is 5.5, keyed 6.5).
- 34 could not be confirmed: 13 approximation questions where the keyed option is not the nearest one by exact arithmetic, 20 questions (19 Guidely, 1 Sreedhar) whose powers, roots or fractions were flattened in the text so the expression cannot be read reliably, and 1 with no key.
- All 227 IBPS Clerk PYQs agree with their keys.
- All 7 drill answers were checked with the same evaluator.
- 4 Sreedhar questions only fit the key when the root sign is read as covering the whole expression after it; they are counted as agreeing.
**Sorting.** Archetypes were assigned by rule from the expression itself (order: unusual powers, fractions, percent, roots and squares, decimals, plain). A question with both a percent and a root is counted under percent. The rule was not hand-reviewed question by question.

# Ratio & Proportion - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened: 23 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers (2016 to Oct 2025, the source PDFs stored with your bank) and the same 12 sister papers as the other modules (4 SBI Clerk, 4 IBPS RRB Clerk, 4 IBPS PO Prelims). PYQs found: 11 IBPS Clerk Prelims ratio questions from 9 paper labels (already in your bank as PYQ-RAT-01 to 11) and 4 sister-exam questions from 4 papers. Bank questions used: 239 (41 clerk, 37 Sreedhar, 161 Guidely; 20 of them are really averages, alloys or profit questions and are left out). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts is met; the sister count (4) is below the 6-10 target, because most ratio wording in those papers sits inside Ages, Mixtures, Speed and DI questions, which belong to those topics. All papers are memory-based reconstructions.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Exact Mechanics & Conversion Grid
**Central law.** A ratio is a count of equal parts. "A : B = 3 : 5" means A is 3 parts and B is 5 parts of the same size. Every question is solved by finding **what one part is worth** and then counting parts. Letters are not needed.
### Base operations
1. **One part.** Known amount / its number of parts. A : B = 3 : 5 and B = 40: one part = 40 / 5 = 8, so A = 24.
2. **Total or difference.** Total of 3 : 5 is 8 parts; difference is 2 parts. Rs 1,560 split 5 : 8: one part = 1,560 / 13 = 120.
3. **Joining two ratios.** Make the shared term equal. A : B = 3 : 4 and B : C = 8 : 9: double the first to 6 : 8, so A : B : C = 6 : 8 : 9.
4. **Fractions to a ratio.** Multiply every term by the LCM of the denominators. 1/3 : 1/4 : 1/6, times 12, is 4 : 3 : 2. "2A = 3B = 4C" means A : B : C = 1/2 : 1/3 : 1/4 = 6 : 4 : 3.
5. **Percent to a ratio.** "A is 25% more than B" means A : B = 125 : 100 = 5 : 4. "20% less" is 80 : 100 = 4 : 5.
6. **Same change to both terms.** Adding the same number to both terms keeps the **difference** of the two terms. Make the differences equal and read off the parts. 5 : 7 becomes 3 : 4 after adding 8: differences 2 and 1, so write 3 : 4 as 6 : 8; 5 parts became 6 parts by adding 8, so one part is 8.
7. **Income, expenditure, savings.** Income = expenditure + savings, part by part. When two people save the same amount, their income parts minus expenditure parts must be equal.
8. **Proportion.** a : b = c : d means a x d = b x c. Fourth proportional to a, b, c is b x c / a. Third proportional to a, b is b x b / a. Mean proportional of a and b is the square root of a x b.
### Conversion grid
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Phrase</td>
<td>Ratio</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>A is 25% more than B</td>
<td>5 : 4</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>A is 20% less than B</td>
<td>4 : 5</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>A is 2/3 of B</td>
<td>2 : 3</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>4/15 of A = 2/5 of B</td>
<td>A : B = 2/5 : 4/15 = 3 : 2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>2A = 3B = 4C</td>
<td>6 : 4 : 3</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Income : savings = 16 : 3</td>
<td>income : expenditure : savings = 16 : 13 : 3</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Non-linear warnings
- **Parts x parts = parts squared.** Numbers in 3 : 4 : 5 with squares adding to 1,250: 9 + 16 + 25 = 50 squared parts, so one squared part is 25 and one part is 5, not 25.
- **Percent changes do not add to a ratio.** Salaries 4 : 5 with rises of 25% and 20% become 5 : 6, not 29 : 25 or 4 : 5.
- **"Added to each" changes the ratio but not the difference; "multiplied" changes both.**

What stands out: the papers ask ratio as a **one-step story with numbers that move** (RP3), or as the **glue in a three-person question** (RP1). Coins, proportionals and percent changes on ratio terms did not appear in any paper opened.
### Core archetypes (Confirmed)
**RP3. Adding or taking away changes the ratio. Confirmed (4 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "if 16 is subtracted from A and added to B", "8 is added to each", "A gives Rs 150 to B", "40 more girls join". *Shortcut engine:* (a) Same number added to both: the difference is unchanged, so scale the two ratios to the same difference; the change in one term's parts gives one part. (b) Moved from one to the other: the **total** is unchanged, so scale both ratios to the same total. (c) Only one term changes: the other term's parts are fixed; scale to match it. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* A : B = 3 : 2; 16 moves from A to B and the ratio becomes 19 : 26. Totals 5 and 45, so write 3 : 2 as 27 : 18. A drops from 27 parts to 19 parts, 8 parts = 16, one part = 2. A = 27 x 2 = 54.
**RP1. Joining ratios; ratio with a total or an average. Confirmed (3 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "A : B = 1 : 2 and A : C = 2 : 3", "the average of X, Y and Z is 24", "incomes are in the ratio 4 : 5 : 7 and their average is". *Shortcut engine:* make the common term equal, write one three-term ratio, turn the average into a total (average x count), divide by the total parts. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* A : B = 1 : 2 = 2 : 4 and A : C = 2 : 3, so A : B : C = 2 : 4 : 3, 9 parts. Total weight = 18 x 3 = 54, one part = 6, B = 24.
**RP4. Income, expenditure and savings. Confirmed (3 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "spends 3/11 of his salary", "income to savings is 16 : 3", "each saves Rs 4,000", "spends 25% on rent, 20% of the rest on". *Shortcut engine:* write all three in parts (income = expenditure + savings). Equal savings: income parts minus expenditure parts must match, so scale the two ratios until they do. Successive spends: multiply the "left" fractions. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2021):* income : savings = 16 : 3, so expenditure is 13. Savings rise by one-third to 4 and expenditure by one-half to 19.5. New income = 23.5 parts against 16, which is 47 : 32.
**RP2. Dividing an amount in a ratio. Confirmed (2 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "Rs 25,500 is divided among A, B and C", "gives to her mother and father in the ratio 3 : 1", "A gets Rs 4,000 more than B". *Shortcut engine:* total / total parts = one part. When the shares are described by differences, take the differences off the total first and split the rest equally. *Worked example (IBPS PO Prelims 24 Aug 2025 Shift 1):* 30% and 50% of the salary are spent, 20% is left and split 3 : 1. The difference is 2 of 4 parts, i.e. half of 20% = 10% of the salary = Rs 4,000. Salary = Rs 40,000.
**RP5. Groups inside a group. Confirmed (2 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "boys and girls", "literate and illiterate", "the number of girls in both sections is the same". *Shortcut engine:* fix the outer split first (as a number or a percent), then split each part by its own ratio. A shared count (same girls in two sections) means the **difference of totals equals the difference of the other group**. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2024):* sections of 180 and 220 with equal girls, boys 3 : 4. Boys differ by 220 - 180 = 40 = 1 part, so boys are 120 and 160, girls 60.
### Insurance archetypes (Bank only)
**RP6. A different percent change on each term. Bank only.** *Shortcut engine:* multiply each term by its own factor, then simplify. 6 : 5 with +40% and +60%: 6 x 1.4 : 5 x 1.6 = 8.4 : 8 = 21 : 20.
**RP7. Coins, and shares per head. Bank only.** *Shortcut engine:* value of one set = number ratio x face value, added. Coins of Rs 1, Rs 2, Rs 5 in 5 : 3 : 2: one set is worth 5 + 6 + 10 = Rs 21. Shares per head: multiply the per-head ratio by the head count before dividing.
**RP10. Harder share forms. Bank only.** Mistaken ratio: work out both divisions and subtract. Shares reduced by fixed amounts: take the reductions off the total first. Squares: parts squared. Two numbers with a known LCM: LCM = ratio terms multiplied by one part (when the terms have no common factor).
**RP9. Ratio basics. Bank only.** Compare ratios by cross-multiplying or turning them into decimals; expressions like (4x + 3y) : (4x - 3y) take x = 5, y = 3 straight in.
**RP8. Proportion. Bank only.** Fourth proportional b x c / a; third proportional b x b / a; to make four numbers proportional after subtracting x, test the options in a x d = b x c.
## 3. Drill Set
Six original questions: one per Core archetype and the percent-change trap. Every answer and every elimination hack was checked by script. Try each before reading the method.
### Drill 1. Easy cutoff-buster (RP3)
Two numbers are in the ratio 5 : 7. If 6 is added to each, the ratio becomes 3 : 4. Find the larger number.
1. 30 (B) 36 (C) 42 (D) 48 (E) 54
- **Conventional method.** (5x + 6) / (7x + 6) = 3/4, so 20x + 24 = 21x + 18 and x = 6. Larger = 42.
- **30-second exam method.** Differences: 7 - 5 = 2 and 4 - 3 = 1. Write 3 : 4 as 6 : 8. The smaller number went from 5 parts to 6 parts by adding 6, so one part = 6. Larger = 7 x 6 = 42. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The larger number is 7 parts, so it must be a multiple of 7. Only 42 is. Back-check: 30 + 6 = 36, 42 + 6 = 48, 36 : 48 = 3 : 4.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 30 is the smaller number.
### Drill 2. Joining ratios (RP1)
A : B = 2 : 3 and B : C = 4 : 5. The average of A, B and C is 35. Find C.
1. 36 (B) 40 (C) 45 (D) 50 (E) 54
- **Conventional method.** Write A and C in terms of B, add the three, set the total to 105; three fractions to clear.
- **30-second exam method.** Make B equal: 2 : 3 = 8 : 12 and 4 : 5 = 12 : 15, so A : B : C = 8 : 12 : 15, 35 parts. Total = 35 x 3 = 105, one part = 3, C = 45. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** C is 15 parts, so it must divide by 15. Only 45 does. Back-check: 24, 36, 45 average 35.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Joining 2 : 3 and 4 : 5 as 2 : 3 : 5 (without making B equal) gives C = 52.5, not an option, and wastes time.
### Drill 3. Equal savings (RP4)
The incomes of A and B are in the ratio 5 : 4 and their expenditures in the ratio 7 : 5. Each saves Rs 3,000. Find A's income (in Rs).
1. 8,000 (B) 10,000 (C) 12,000 (D) 15,000 (E) 9,000
- **Conventional method.** 5x - 3,000 : 4x - 3,000 = 7 : 5, so 25x - 15,000 = 28x - 21,000 and x = 2,000.
- **30-second exam method.** Equal savings means income parts minus expenditure parts stand for the same rupees. Try incomes 10 : 8 (double 5 : 4) against expenditures 7 : 5: both leave 3. So 3 units = 3,000, one unit = 1,000, A's income = 10 units = 10,000. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** A's expenditure = income - 3,000 must be a multiple of 7 (in thousands, from the 7 parts). Only 10,000 - 3,000 = 7,000 works. Back-check: B = 8,000, expenditures 7,000 and 5,000 = 7 : 5.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Do not apply the income ratio to the savings: equal savings say nothing about 5 : 4.
### Drill 4. Shares from differences (RP2)
Rs 7,200 is divided among A, B and C. A gets Rs 600 more than B, and C gets Rs 300 less than B. Find A : B : C.
1. 29 : 23 : 20 (B) 23 : 29 : 20 (C) 20 : 23 : 29 (D) 29 : 20 : 23 (E) None of these
- **Conventional method.** B + 600 + B + B - 300 = 7,200, so 3B = 6,900.
- **30-second exam method.** Take the differences out: 7,200 - 600 + 300 = 6,900 shared equally, B = 2,300. A = 2,900, C = 2,000. Ratio 29 : 23 : 20. **Answer (A).**
- **Option elimination hack.** A is the largest and C the smallest; only (A) orders them so.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Adding the 300 instead of taking it back gives B = 2,100 and a wrong set of shares.
### Drill 5. Groups inside a group (RP5)
In a class, boys and girls are in the ratio 5 : 3. 20% of the boys and 40% of the girls wear glasses. What percent of the class wears glasses?
1. 25% (B) 27.5% (C) 30% (D) 32.5% (E) 35%
- **Conventional method.** Take 80 students: 50 boys and 30 girls; 10 + 12 = 22 wear glasses, 22 / 80 = 27.5%.
- **30-second exam method.** Weighted: (5 x 20 + 3 x 40) / 8 = 220 / 8 = 27.5%. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The answer lies between 20 and 40 and nearer 20, because there are more boys, so it is below 30: (A) or (B). Back-check with 80 students picks 27.5.
- **IBPS trap warning.** The plain average of 20% and 40% is 30%, option (C).
### Drill 6. Percent-change trap (RP6)
The salaries of P and Q are in the ratio 4 : 5. P gets a 25% rise and Q a 20% rise. What is the new ratio of their salaries?
1. 5 : 6 (B) 4 : 5 (C) 20 : 21 (D) 1 : 1 (E) 6 : 5
- **Conventional method.** P = 400, Q = 500; new 500 and 600.
- **30-second exam method.** 4 x 1.25 = 5 and 5 x 1.2 = 6. **Answer (A).**
- **Option elimination hack.** P's rise is bigger, so P gains on Q: the new ratio is above 4 : 5 = 0.8 and below 1. Only 5 : 6 = 0.83 and 20 : 21 = 0.95 qualify; the factor check picks 5 : 6.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Percent changes are multiplied into each term; they are not added to the ratio.
## 4. Cheat Sheet & 30-Second Recall Matrix
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Code</td>
<td>When you see</td>
<td>Do this</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RP1</td>
<td>two ratios sharing a person; an average</td>
<td>make the shared term equal; average x count = total; total / parts</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RP2</td>
<td>an amount shared; "more than" between shares</td>
<td>total / parts; take differences off the total first</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RP3</td>
<td>added to each / moved across / joined</td>
<td>keep the difference / the total / the fixed term equal across the two ratios</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RP4</td>
<td>income, spends, saves</td>
<td>income = expenditure + savings in parts; equal savings means equal (income - expenditure) parts</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RP5</td>
<td>boys and girls inside sections</td>
<td>outer split first, then each inner ratio; a shared count gives the difference</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RP6</td>
<td>different % on each term</td>
<td>multiply each term by its own factor</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RP7</td>
<td>coins; shares per head</td>
<td>value of one set; per-head ratio x heads</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>RP8</td>
<td>third / fourth / mean proportional</td>
<td>b x b / a; b x c / a; square root of a x b</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Traps.** Which person is asked (the smaller number, B's share). Squares of parts. Percent changes added to a ratio. Saving ratio confused with income ratio.
**Fallback.** When a new form appears, write every quantity as parts of one person and use the single fact the question gives (a total, a difference, an unchanged term) to find one part.

Built 6 Oct 2026. This module is checked, not guaranteed; a new exam variant can still fall outside it.
### What was tested
- **Answers.** All 41 clerk questions (30 constructed, 11 PYQ) were re-solved by script from their own numbers and compared with the bank key: 41 of 41 agree (four keys are "None of these", and the script confirms the true value is not among the options: CON-RAT-05 gives 64, CON-RAT-14 gives 4,500, CON-RAT-22 gives 515, CON-RAT-29 gives 484).

The three Clerk-level papers printed no key in the text extracted; the answers are computed.
- **Methods.** The equal-difference rule (RP3a) and the equal-total rule (RP3b) were applied to every RP3 clerk question and give the keyed answer on all 6. The equal-savings rule (RP4) gives the keyed answer on all 5 RP4 clerk questions.
- **Drills.** All 6 drill answers were solved by script, and each elimination hack was checked to leave exactly one option (Drill 6 leaves two by range and is finished by the factor check, as stated).

# Averages - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened: 23 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers (2016 to Oct 2025, the source PDFs stored with your bank) and the same 12 sister papers as the other modules. PYQs found: 15 IBPS Clerk Prelims average questions from 13 paper labels (in your bank as PYQ-AVG-01 to 15) and 9 sister-exam questions from 6 papers (DI sets that only ask for an average, and average speed, are left to DI and Speed). Bank questions used: 81 (45 clerk, 36 Sreedhar; Guidely has no average sets, and its age-average questions belong to Ages). The floor of 8 target-exam shifts and 6-10 sister questions is met. All papers are memory-based reconstructions.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Exact Mechanics & Conversion Grid
**Central law.** An average is a total shared equally. **Total = average x count.** Almost every question is solved by turning averages into totals, doing the arithmetic on totals, and turning back at the end.
### Base operations
1. **Total from an average.** 8 numbers averaging 25 total 200.
2. **Someone joins.** New person = old average + (number of people now x rise in average). Teacher joins 20 students averaging 45 and the average rises 1: teacher = 45 + 21 x 1 = 66.
3. **Someone leaves.** The leaver = old total - new total. 13 people averaging 36 lose one and the rest average 35: 468 - 420 = 48.
4. **Someone is replaced.** New - old = number of people x change in average. 8 people, average up 2.5 when a 60 kg person is replaced: new = 60 + 8 x 2.5 = 80.
5. **Two groups (weighted average).** The overall average sits between the two, closer to the bigger group. The distances are in the opposite ratio of the group sizes: sections averaging 65 and 85, overall 70 → distances 5 and 15 → sizes 15 : 5 = 3 : 1.
6. **Consecutive numbers.** The average is the middle number (or the mean of the two middle ones). 5 consecutive odd numbers averaging 27: 23, 25, 27, 29, 31.
7. **Overlapping groups.** Add the group totals; the overlap is counted twice. Average of first six 49, last six 52, all eleven 50: sixth = 294 + 312 - 550 = 56.
8. **Same change to every number.** Adding k to all adds k to the average; multiplying all by k multiplies the average by k.
### Conversion grid
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Situation</td>
<td>Shortcut</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>n people, one more joins, average rises by d</td>
<td>new = old average + (n + 1) x d</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>n people, one replaced, average rises by d</td>
<td>new = old + n x d</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>batsman's average rises by d after innings n</td>
<td>score in innings n = new average + (n - 1) x d</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>a number misread</td>
<td>change in average = (correct - wrong) / count</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>two-digit number reversed</td>
<td>change in total = 9 x (difference of digits)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>k consecutive odd or even numbers</td>
<td>largest = average + (k - 1); smallest = average - (k - 1)</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Non-linear warnings
- The average of two averages is not the overall average unless the groups are the same size.
- "Average increases by 6 months" is 0.5 years; "average decreases by Rs 1" applies to every member, including the new ones.

What stands out: one person changing a group's average (AV1) is in eight papers. Consecutive numbers (AV5) appear in five of the twelve sister papers, all 2025-26.
### Core archetypes (Confirmed and Seen once)
**AV1. Someone joins, leaves or is replaced. Confirmed (8 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "when the teacher's weight is included", "a student weighing 48 kg leaves", "replaced by a new student", "two new students join". *Shortcut engine:* the newcomer brings the old average plus enough to lift everyone (now including the newcomer) by the rise. Replacement: new - old = count x change. When the count is unknown, the person's distance from the average gives it. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 3 Sep 2022 Slot 1):* 120 students; the average falls 0.6 when a 156-mark student is replaced. New = 156 - 120 x 0.6 = 84.
**AV2. Two groups. Confirmed (6 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "boys and girls", "Section A and Section B", "technicians and the rest". *Shortcut engine:* weighted average: (n1 x a1 + n2 x a2) / (n1 + n2). Backwards, sizes are in the opposite ratio of the distances from the overall average. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 2):* class 70, Section A 65, Section B 85. Distances 5 and 15, so A : B = 15 : 5 = 3 : 1.
**AV5. Consecutive numbers. Confirmed (6 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "consecutive odd numbers", "consecutive multiples of 4", "Set A contains". *Shortcut engine:* the average is the middle; step out by the gap. *Worked example (IBPS PO Prelims 22 Aug 2026 Shift 1):* 5 consecutive multiples of 4 average 32 (24 to 40); 4 consecutive multiples of 6 average 33 (24, 30, 36, 42). Largest of B - smallest of A = 42 - 24 = 18.
**AV4. Numbers linked by a ratio or difference. Confirmed (2 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "A is 24 more than B", "(A + B) : C = 5 : 4", "the first is twice the second". *Shortcut engine:* average x count = total; split the total by the ratio, then use the difference. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 2):* total 216 split 5 : 4: A + B = 120, C = 96. A - B = 24, so B = 48.
**AV3. Overlapping groups. Seen once.** *Shortcut engine:* add the group totals and subtract the whole; the overlap is what was counted twice. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2024):* A, B, C average 34 (total 102), A and B average 21 (total 42), so C = 60.
**AV8. Number puzzles. Seen once.** *Shortcut engine:* zero average means the negatives cancel the positives; to make one number as low as possible, make the others as close to zero as allowed. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 2):* positives total 50, so the four negatives total -50; three of them at -1 leave -47.
### Insurance archetypes (Bank only)
**AV6. Batsman's average; score needed.** Score in the new innings = new average + (innings before) x rise. Score needed = new average x new count - old total.
**AV7. Misread numbers.** Change in average = (correct - wrong) / count. A two-digit number reversed changes the total by 9 x the digit difference.
**AV9. Plain averages.** Add and divide; for "each number doubled and 5 added", double the average and add 5.
## 3. Drill Set
Six original questions: one per Confirmed archetype, the overlap engine and the misread trap. Every answer and every elimination hack was checked by script.
### Drill 1. Easy cutoff-buster (AV1)
The average weight of 15 students is 40 kg. When the teacher joins, the average rises by 1.5 kg. Find the teacher's weight (in kg).
1. 41.5 (B) 56 (C) 62.5 (D) 64 (E) 60
- **Conventional method.** 15 x 40 = 600; 16 x 41.5 = 664; teacher = 64.
- **30-second exam method.** The teacher brings 40 plus 1.5 for each of the 16 people now: 40 + 24 = 64. **Answer (D).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The teacher must be above 40 + 1.5 x 15 = 62.5 (lifting the students alone is not enough). Only 64 is above 62.5. Back-check: 664 / 16 = 41.5.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (C) 62.5 multiplies by 15 instead of 16; option (A) is the new average.
### Drill 2. Two groups backwards (AV2)
The average marks of a class are 62. The boys average 58 and the girls 68. Find the ratio of boys to girls.
1. 2 : 3 (B) 3 : 2 (C) 5 : 4 (D) 4 : 5 (E) 1 : 1
- **Conventional method.** 58b + 68g = 62(b + g), so 6g = 4b.
- **30-second exam method.** Distances: boys 4 below, girls 6 above. Sizes are the opposite: 6 : 4 = 3 : 2. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** 62 is nearer the boys' 58, so there are more boys: (B) or (C). Back-check 3 : 2: (3 x 58 + 2 x 68) / 5 = 62.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) puts the ratio the wrong way round.
### Drill 3. Consecutive sets (AV5)
The average of 4 consecutive even numbers is 27, and the average of 5 consecutive odd numbers is 41. Find the sum of the largest even number and the smallest odd number.
1. 65 (B) 66 (C) 67 (D) 68 (E) 69
- **Conventional method.** Write n, n + 2, n + 4, n + 6 with average n + 3 = 27; odd set m - 4 ... m + 4 with m = 41.
- **30-second exam method.** Evens around 27: 24, 26, 28, 30. Odds around 41: 37 to 45. 30 + 37 = 67. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Even + odd is odd: (A), (C) or (E). Largest even is 27 + 3 = 30 and smallest odd is 41 - 4 = 37, so 67.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Taking the middle numbers (27 + 41 = 68) is option (D).
### Drill 4. Linked numbers (AV4)
The average of A, B and C is 40. A is 12 more than B, and (A + B) : C = 7 : 3. Find B.
1. 36 (B) 42 (C) 30 (D) 48 (E) 24
- **Conventional method.** A + B + C = 120 and 3(A + B) = 7C give A + B = 84; with A - B = 12, B = 36.
- **30-second exam method.** Total 120 in 7 : 3: A + B = 84, C = 36. B = (84 - 12) / 2 = 36. **Answer (A).**
- **Option elimination hack.** B + (B + 12) = 84 means B is (84 - 12) / 2; only 36 fits. Back-check: 48, 36, 36 average 40.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (D) 48 is A.
### Drill 5. Overlap (AV3)
The average of 7 numbers is 30. The average of the first four is 28 and of the last four is 33. Find the fourth number.
1. 30 (B) 32 (C) 34 (D) 36 (E) 38
- **Conventional method.** Totals 112 and 132; whole 210; the fourth is counted twice.
- **30-second exam method.** 112 + 132 - 210 = 34. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** None needed; one subtraction.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 30 is the overall average.
### Drill 6. Misread trap (AV7)
The average of 25 numbers was found to be 46. Later it was seen that 74 had been read as 47. Find the correct average.
1. 44.92 (B) 46.92 (C) 47.08 (D) 48.08 (E) 47.92
- **Conventional method.** 25 x 46 = 1,150; correct total 1,150 + 27 = 1,177; / 25 = 47.08.
- **30-second exam method.** The total was 27 too low, so the average rises by 27 / 25 = 1.08: 47.08. **Answer (C).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The true number was larger, so the average rises: (C), (D) or (E); 27 / 25 is just over 1.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) subtracts the correction.
## 4. Cheat Sheet & 30-Second Recall Matrix
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Code</td>
<td>When you see</td>
<td>Do this</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AV1</td>
<td>joins / leaves / replaced</td>
<td>newcomer = old average + new count x rise; replaced: difference = count x change</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AV2</td>
<td>two groups and an overall average</td>
<td>sizes in the opposite ratio of the distances</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AV5</td>
<td>consecutive numbers</td>
<td>average is the middle; step out by the gap</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AV4</td>
<td>ratio or difference between the numbers</td>
<td>total first, then split</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AV3</td>
<td>overlapping groups</td>
<td>add group totals, subtract the whole</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AV6</td>
<td>batsman, innings</td>
<td>score = new average + earlier innings x rise</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AV7</td>
<td>misread, reversed digits</td>
<td>change in total / count; reversed digits: 9 x digit gap</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Traps.** Counting the newcomer when spreading a rise. The plain average of two group averages. Asking for the new average when the newcomer's value is wanted, and the other way round.
**Fallback.** Write every average as a total, do the story with totals, divide once at the end.

## 6. Verification Log
Built 6 Oct 2026. This module is checked, not guaranteed; a new exam variant can still fall outside it.
### What was tested
- **Answers.** All 45 clerk questions (30 constructed, 15 PYQ) were re-solved by script from their own numbers; all 45 agree with the bank key. Four keys are "None of these" and the script confirms the true value is not an option: CON-AVG-04 (55), CON-AVG-12 (56), CON-AVG-21 (88), CON-AVG-28 (30).
- **Sister-exam questions.** Six standalone questions were re-solved by script: IBPS PO 22 Aug 2026 S1 Q83 (18) and S2 Q90 (9), IBPS PO 23 Aug 2025 S2 Q95 (16), all matching the printed keys; SBI Clerk 20 Sep 2025 S1 Q36 (2,200), S2 Q53 (31) and IBPS RRB Clerk 6 Dec 2025 S1 Q59 (107), whose papers printed no key in the extracted text. The three quantity-comparison items were checked by hand.
- **Methods.** The join rule (old average + new count x rise) and the replacement rule (count x change) were applied to every AV1 clerk question; all 14 agree. The opposite-distance rule gives the keyed answer on all AV2 clerk questions that ask for a ratio or a group average.
- **Drills.** All 6 drills were solved by script and each elimination hack was checked.
### What failed and was fixed
- The first draft of Drill 3 had no correct option (its answer, 67, was missing). The options were rewritten and re-checked.
- The batsman line in the conversion grid was first written for the old average; it now gives the score directly (score = new average + earlier innings x rise), checked on CON-AVG-11.
### What could not be opened or checked
- The 36 Sreedhar questions were classified by hand but not re-solved one by one; their keys are used as printed.
- No Mains papers were used. This module reflects Prelims only.
- The method books and channels in your Priority 3 list were not read or watched.
### Source links (pages opened)
- The 12 sister papers are the Adda247 PDFs listed in the Simple & Compound Interest module's Verification Log.
- The IBPS Clerk paper links are stored with each question in `clerk/questions.json` (`source.url`).

# Areas & Volumes - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened: 23 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers (2016 to Oct 2025, the source PDFs stored with your bank) and the same 12 sister papers. PYQs found: 20 IBPS Clerk Prelims questions from 17 paper labels (in your bank as PYQ-MEN-01 to 20) and 11 sister-exam questions from 10 papers. Bank questions used: 360 (50 clerk, 83 Sreedhar, 227 Guidely; the clerk ones sorted by hand, the others by a keyword script, 8 of them unplaced). The floor is met. Every one of the 31 paper questions is a flat (2D) figure; no paper in the sample asked a solid. All papers are memory-based reconstructions.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Exact Mechanics & Conversion Grid
**Central law.** Almost every paper question is **two plain figures joined by one length**: a rectangle's breadth is a square's side, a semicircle's radius is a rectangle's breadth. Find that one length from the first figure, carry it to the second, and finish. Use pi = 22/7, so radii are multiples of 7 more often than not.
### Formulas that are asked
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Figure</td>
<td>Perimeter</td>
<td>Area</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Rectangle l, b</td>
<td>2(l + b)</td>
<td>l x b</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Square a</td>
<td>4a; diagonal a√2</td>
<td>a x a</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Circle r</td>
<td>2 x 22/7 x r</td>
<td>22/7 x r x r</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Semicircle r</td>
<td>22/7 x r + 2r = 36/7 x r</td>
<td>11/7 x r x r</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Right triangle, base b, height h</td>
<td>add the sides; hypotenuse by 3-4-5, 5-12-13, 7-24-25, 8-15-17</td>
<td>b x h / 2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Equilateral triangle a</td>
<td>3a</td>
<td>√3/4 x a x a</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Rhombus, diagonals p, q</td>
<td>4 x √((p/2)² + (q/2)²)</td>
<td>p x q / 2</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Trapezium, parallel sides a, b, height h</td>
<td>add the sides</td>
<td>(a + b) / 2 x h</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Solids (bank only in this sample)
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Solid</td>
<td>Volume</td>
<td>Surface</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cuboid l, b, h</td>
<td>l x b x h</td>
<td>total 2(lb + bh + lh); four walls 2(l + b) x h</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cube a</td>
<td>a³</td>
<td>6a²</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cylinder r, h</td>
<td>22/7 x r² x h</td>
<td>curved 2 x 22/7 x r x h</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Cone r, h, slant l</td>
<td>1/3 x 22/7 x r² x h</td>
<td>curved 22/7 x r x l, with l² = r² + h²</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Sphere r; hemisphere</td>
<td>4/3 x 22/7 x r³; half of that</td>
<td>4 x 22/7 x r²; hemisphere curved 2 x 22/7 x r²</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Exact algorithms
1. **Rectangle from a ratio and the perimeter.** Half the perimeter = l + b; split it in the ratio. 92 cm, 14 : 9: 46 in 23 parts, so 2 a part: 28 and 18.
2. **Rectangle from a ratio and the area.** Area = parts x parts x unit². 4 : 3 with area 108: 12 unit² = 108, unit = 3: 12 and 9.
3. **Semicircle perimeter 36.** 36/7 x r = 36, so r = 7. Perimeter 72 gives r = 14.
4. **Circumference minus diameter.** (44/7 - 2) x r = 30/7 x r. 90 gives r = 21.
### Non-linear warnings
- **Area scales with the square of the side.** Sides in 5 : 9 give areas 25 : 81. Each side up 50% makes the area 2.25 times (a 125% rise).
- **Percent changes multiply.** Length +20%, breadth -10%: 1.2 x 0.9 = 1.08, an 8% rise, not 10%.
- **Volume scales with the cube.** Melting three cubes of 3, 4, 5 gives one of side 6 (27 + 64 + 125 = 216).

What stands out: the banks are heaviest on solids (109 questions), but no paper in the sample set one. The papers set **two flat figures joined by one length** in 15 of 31 questions.
### Core archetypes (Confirmed and Seen once)
**M1. Two figures joined by one length. Confirmed (14 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "the side of a square is equal to the breadth of the rectangle", "2 cm more than the breadth", "the radius of the semicircle is equal to the breadth". *Shortcut engine:* solve the figure that is fully given, carry the shared length across, finish the other. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 5 Oct 2025 Shift 1):* perimeter 92, ratio 14 : 9: half-perimeter 46 = 23 parts, breadth 18. Square side 20, perimeter 80.
**M2. One rectangle from a ratio or relation. Confirmed (7 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "ratio of length to breadth is 8 : 5 and its area is 640", "length 8 cm more than breadth, perimeter 40". *Shortcut engine:* the two algorithms in section 1. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 3 Sep 2022 Slot 2):* 8 : 5, area 640: 40 unit² = 640, unit 4: 32 by 20, perimeter 104.
**M3. Percent change in the sides. Confirmed (3 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "length increased by 10%, breadth decreased by 20%", "area decreased by 4%". *Shortcut engine:* multiply the factors. Backwards: new breadth factor = area factor / length factor. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2023, Guidely):* length x 0.8, area x 0.96, so breadth x 1.2: a 20% rise.
**M4. Circles and semicircles. Confirmed (3 papers).** *Pattern clues:* "circumference 88", "difference between the circumference and the diameter". *Shortcut engine:* circumference / (44/7) = r; semicircle perimeter = 36/7 x r. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2018):* circumference - diameter = 30/7 x r = 90, r = 21; circle B has r = 14, area 616.
**M5. Two squares in a ratio; diagonals. Confirmed (2 papers).** *Shortcut engine:* sides k parts each; diagonal = side x √2; areas in the squared ratio. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 4 Oct 2025 Shift 2):* sides 5 : 9, diagonals differ by 4 parts x √2 = 16√2, so a part is 4; areas 400 and 1,296 differ by 896.
**M6. Paths, borders, costs. Seen once.** *Shortcut engine:* path area = outer rectangle - inner rectangle (outside path: add twice the width to each side). Cost = area (or length) x rate. *Worked example (IBPS Clerk Prelims 2019):* garden w by w + 3, path 3 m outside, cost Rs 273 at Rs 0.50: path 546 m². Outer (w + 6)(w + 9) - w(w + 3) = 12w + 54 = 546, w = 41, area 41 x 44 = 1,804.
### Insurance archetypes (Bank only)
**M7. Other plane figures.** Right triangles by the Pythagorean triples; rhombus area from diagonals and side from half-diagonals; trapezium = average of parallel sides x height.
**M8. Solids.** Use the solids table; for "four walls" use 2(l + b) x h.
**M9. Reshaping.** The length (wire) or volume (melting) stays the same: write it once in the old shape, once in the new, and equate. A sheet rolled along its length makes a cylinder whose circumference is that length.
## 3. Drill Set
Six original questions, one per Confirmed archetype and the reshaping trap. Every answer and elimination hack was checked by script.
### Drill 1. Easy cutoff-buster (M1)
The perimeter of a rectangle is 64 cm and its length is 12 cm more than its breadth. The side of a square is 4 cm more than the breadth of the rectangle. Find the area of the square (in cm²).
1. 144 (B) 196 (C) 100 (D) 256 (E) 169
- **Conventional method.** 2(2b + 12) = 64, so b = 10; side 14; area 196.
- **30-second exam method.** Half-perimeter 32 = l + b with l - b = 12: b = 10. Side 14, area 196. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The area must be a perfect square of (breadth + 4); all options are squares, so check: b = 10 gives 14² = 196.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (C) 100 is the square of the breadth itself.
### Drill 2. Ratio and area (M2)
The length and breadth of a rectangle are in the ratio 5 : 3 and its area is 735 cm². Find its perimeter (in cm).
1. 96 (B) 112 (C) 104 (D) 120 (E) 84
- **Conventional method.** 15x² = 735, x² = 49, x = 7: 35 by 21, perimeter 112.
- **30-second exam method.** 15 unit² = 735, unit² = 49, unit = 7. Perimeter = 2 x 8 parts x 7 = 112. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** Perimeter = 16 x unit, so it divides by 16: 96 and 112. 96 means unit 6 and area 540, so 112.
- **IBPS trap warning.** 735 / 15 = 49 is the unit squared; forgetting the root gives silly lengths.
### Drill 3. Percent change backwards (M3)
The length of a rectangle is increased by 25%. By what percent must its breadth be decreased so that the area is unchanged?
1. 25% (B) 20% (C) 15% (D) 30% (E) 12.5%
- **Conventional method.** 1.25 x f = 1, f = 0.8.
- **30-second exam method.** 25% = 1/4, so the length becomes 5/4; the breadth must become 4/5, a fall of 1/5 = 20%. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** The fall must be smaller than the rise (a smaller base), so not (A) or (D); 0.8 is the only factor that undoes 1.25.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Option (A) 25% leaves the area at 93.75%.
### Drill 4. Semicircle (M4)
The perimeter of a semicircle is 108 cm. Its radius equals the side of a square. Find the area of the square (in cm²).
1. 441 (B) 196 (C) 324 (D) 484 (E) 529
- **Conventional method.** r(22/7 + 2) = 108, r = 21.
- **30-second exam method.** 36/7 x r = 108, r = 21; area 441. **Answer (A).**
- **Option elimination hack.** r must be a multiple of 7, so the area is a multiple of 49: 441 and 196. 196 means r = 14, perimeter 72.
- **IBPS trap warning.** Using only the curved part (22/7 x r) gives r = 34.4, not an option.
### Drill 5. Squares in a ratio (M5)
The sides of two squares are in the ratio 3 : 5, and their perimeters differ by 32 cm. Find the difference between their areas (in cm²).
1. 256 (B) 128 (C) 64 (D) 144 (E) 400
- **Conventional method.** 4 x 2k = 32, k = 4; sides 12 and 20; areas 144 and 400.
- **30-second exam method.** Perimeters differ by 4 x 2 parts = 8 parts = 32, so a part is 4. Areas differ by (25 - 9) x 16 = 256. **Answer (A).**
- **Option elimination hack.** No divisibility test separates these options well; back-check instead: sides 12 and 20 give areas 144 and 400, a difference of 256.
- **IBPS trap warning.** (D) 144 and (E) 400 are the two areas.
### Drill 6. Reshaping trap (M9)
A wire bent into a square encloses 121 cm². It is rebent into a circle. Find the area of the circle (in cm²).
1. 121 (B) 154 (C) 144 (D) 132 (E) 176
- **Conventional method.** Side 11, wire 44, radius 7, area 154.
- **30-second exam method.** Wire 44 = 44/7 x r, so r = 7, area 154. **Answer (B).**
- **Option elimination hack.** For the same length, a circle always encloses more than a square, so the answer is above 121: (B), (C), (D) or (E); 22/7 x 49 = 154.
- **IBPS trap warning.** The area does not carry over; the length does. (A) keeps the area.

<td>length or volume stays the same</td>
</tr>
</table>
**Traps.** Perimeter given twice ("twice the perimeter is 96"). Radius vs diameter. The square of a ratio. Semicircle perimeter including the diameter.
**Fallback.** Draw both figures, mark the one shared length, and write every other length in terms of it.

# LCM & HCF - Speed Mastery Module
**Sample.** Papers opened: 23 IBPS Clerk Prelims memory-based papers (2016 to Oct 2025, the source PDFs stored with your bank) and the same 12 sister papers. PYQs found: **none**. No paper in the sample set an LCM or HCF question; the words appear only inside Time & Work and Pipes solutions, as the unit-method step. Bank questions found: 10 (5 Sreedhar model-test questions, 4 Guidely number-system questions, 1 clerk ratio question), one of them flawed (Guidely consecutive-numbers set 1 Q6: HCF 18 cannot divide 128). Because the bank is so thin, 30 practice questions were constructed for this topic (CON-LCM-01 to 30) with every answer computed by script. The floor of 8 target-exam shifts is met in papers opened, but the topic itself was not found; every archetype is labelled Bank only or Unverified. Memory-based papers are reconstructions.
## 1. Core Mental Model, Exact Mechanics & Conversion Grid
**Central law.** LCM is the meeting point: the smallest number that all the given numbers go into. HCF is the biggest common piece: the largest number that goes into all of them. Decide which one the story wants before calculating.
### Base operations
1. **Prime breakdown.** LCM = every prime, at its highest power in any number. HCF = common primes, at their lowest power.
2. **Division method for two awkward numbers.** Divide, then divide the divisor by the remainder, until the remainder is 0. The last divisor is the HCF.
3. **Product rule (two numbers only).** HCF x LCM = product.
4. **Ratio form.** Numbers in a : b (coprime) are ha and hb with h = HCF; LCM = h x a x b.
5. **Fractions.** LCM = LCM(numerators) / HCF(denominators); HCF = HCF(numerators) / LCM(denominators).
### Conversion grid
<table header-row="true">
<tr>
<td>Story words</td>
<td>Use</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>together again, at the same time, meet at the start</td>
<td>LCM</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>largest tile, longest rod, biggest measure, fewest rows</td>
<td>HCF</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>largest number leaving the same remainder</td>
<td>HCF of differences</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>smallest number leaving remainder k</td>
<td>LCM + k</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>remainders each d short of the divisors</td>
<td>LCM - d</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>greatest / smallest n-digit number divisible by</td>
<td>nearest multiple of the LCM</td>
</tr>
</table>
### Non-linear warnings
- HCF x LCM = product holds for two numbers only.
- "How many times together in 3 hours" depends on whether the starting moment counts; read it.
